# Development History

A chronological record of completed work — moved out of the root [`notes`](../../notes) file, which is now purely the working backlog.

**What lives here vs. [`decisions.md`](decisions.md).** The split is by question answered, not by date:

- **`decisions.md` — "why is the code like this?"** A choice that still governs the code, including choices *not* to do something. Read it before changing the area it covers.
- **`history.md` (this file) — "what happened, and what did it cost?"** The record of work done and, more importantly, **approaches measured and rejected**. Read it before re-attempting something that looks obviously missing.

That second category is why this file is kept rather than deleted. Most entries below are ordinary completed tasks, but a substantial minority record an attempt that was implemented, measured, and reverted — with the numbers. Those are not reconstructible from the code, because the code is precisely where they *aren't*. Several have already prevented a re-attempt of an approach that had been measured and rejected weeks earlier.

Entries are newest-first, in the format the `notes` backlog uses. Nothing here is edited for hindsight — an entry that was wrong at the time stays wrong, with a later correction appended, so the reasoning trail survives intact.


- [x] **The demo-campaign L6 wedge is fixed: the bot could see and shoot through solid geometry (2026-08-18).** Wedge rate **16.7% -> 0.0%** at 120 attempts per arm, p < 0.0001. Open since 2026-08-07, and none of the seven earlier candidate causes was it.

  **Two defects, and only the pair explains it.** `player.ts`'s `isWall` — the engine's own test, what a bullet and a body actually hit — counts `DOOR_TILE` and `BRANCH_DOOR_TILE` as solid. The bot's `isWallTile` does not, *deliberately*: a door is passable terrain to a route planner, which is why `bfsPath` tracks `openedDoors` rather than treating doors as walls. But `hasLineOfSight` was built on that walking predicate, so every sight question in the bot answered as if doors stood open. Separately, the sight ray was a point sampler, and a segment passing exactly through the point where four tiles meet steps between the two diagonal cells without ever landing inside the two tiles wedged between them — a clear view through a solid corner, at *any* sampling resolution, because a corner is a single point.

  Both were needed. Fixing the door opacity alone left the L6 sight line still open, because that particular ray threads the corner: bot at (14.20,50.20), enemy at (13.7,49.7) 0.71 tiles away, wall at (13,50), closed door at (14,49), segment a precise 45 degrees through (14.0,50.0).

  **The mechanism, end to end.** The bot aimed at a target no bullet of its could reach — the trace shows `turnBurst=1` (already on target) with `fire=false` in 97% of decisions — and combat preempts navigation, so the door leg it was in the middle of never advanced while its health drained 1.00 -> 0.12 where it stood. Same tile every run, because it is that door and that enemy's home. Sight now uses `isSightBlockingTile` (walls plus closed doors) and tests both flanking tiles on a diagonal cell change; `isWallTile` keeps its semantics for the walking-distance flood, its only other caller.

  **A first attempt at the corner rule was too strict and CI caught it.** Blocking sight when *either* flanking tile is solid made a blocker standing diagonally in a doorway unshootable — `driveToExit` selects the blocker geometrically but the killing runs through `decide()`, whose `pickThreat` needs line of sight to fire, so `#fightUntilDead` drove at it for its whole budget without ever firing. `verify (multiplayer-transition)` was cancelled at its 20-minute job timeout and `verify (Playwright/webkit)` at 360 minutes. Narrowed to *sealed* corners only — both flanking tiles solid — those two jobs return to 4 and 5 minutes. The engine occludes sprites with the render z-buffer rather than a tile test, so a single-flank graze really is a shot it allows; L6 is unaffected because its wall at (13,50) and closed door at (14,49) are both solid.

  **The survival scare, and why it was not one.** Raw deaths 11.7% -> 31.7%, p = 0.0002 — numerically the trade `8390c8e` was reverted for. Two conditionings dismantle it. First, a stuck attempt is neither a clear nor a death, so leaving it in the denominator makes both other rates lie. Second and decisive, the per-level breakdown: **L5, the level both arms play to a resolution, has 8 deaths against 7.** Every extra death is on L6, where the baseline never resolves at all (20 stuck, 1 death) and the fixed arm resolves every attempt (0 stuck, 31 deaths). The bot did not get worse at surviving; it started playing a level it used to hang in, and that level is lethal. That L5 control is better evidence than the separate capture-harness run this item asked for, because it is the same run under the same conditions.

  **What this does *not* buy, stated because an earlier draft of this entry claimed it.** Absolute clear rate is unchanged: 71.7% -> 68.3%, p = 0.57. A measurement of the over-strict rule had shown clears *rising* (68.8% -> 77.5%, p = 0.031), and that gain does not survive the narrowing — with more permissive sight the bot engages more and dies more on L6 rather than clearing it. The win here is that regeneration stops hanging, which is the harm the item actually names; it is not a yield improvement.

  **Five earlier defects, fixed the day before, that were real but not this** (`fix/door-leg-swallowed-stuck`, measured null at 120/arm, p = 0.873): the `openDoor` leg swallowing a stuck staging drive; recording doors as opened it never reached; staging demanding `TIGHT_ARRIVE_EPS` at a point further from the door than `openDoorAhead`'s own 0.35-tile probe; the cornered-retreat stall guard comparing positions as `toFixed(2)` strings so a 0.01 wobble reset it every decision; and the break-contact branch having no exit condition. They halved the cost of a wedge (21,250 -> 10,379 median decisions) without changing its rate.

  **What this cost in method, recorded because it is the reusable part.** Every figure in this item before 2026-08-18 came from n<=6, and n=3 read 33% against a true 20%. The item's own opening lesson is about underpowered arms and it was reproduced twice more before being caught. Separating 20% from 10% needs ~115 attempts per arm; the runs that settled it were 12 workers against one shared dev server, which is only sound because the bot's virtual clock means CPU contention changes wall time and not decisions. Three wrong root causes were proposed and killed by checking rather than reasoning: enemies blocking movement (they do not — `collides` tests walls only), a `laterallyBlocked` refusal to move (every facing yields movement keys), and missing line of sight (the bot had too much of it).

- [x] **`verify (multiplayer-transition)` root-caused and fixed: the level was ending under the drive (2026-08-17).** The job that had been failing intermittently since 2026-08-07 — 19.5 minutes on one run, killed by a 20-minute job timeout — turns out to have been failing on runs that had **already succeeded**. Fixed in `driveToExit`; the deadline guard that made it diagnosable stays.

  **The dump said it plainly and was read past twice, including by me.** `HOST_NAV_DEADLINE_MS` fired on CI run `32044599072` and printed, two lines apart: `exit gate at (46,44)` — the exit the drive was planned against — and `live exit tile {"x":47,"y":49}` read straight from the engine, alongside a roster that had grown from 11 to 13. `getMapExit()` returns `this.map.exit` and the plan came from the same field, so those disagreeing at all is not a discrepancy to weigh, it is proof the engine was running **a different map**. The host had won, the level had advanced, and the drive kept aiming at a level that no longer existed — `exitBacktrack` and `exitHunt` at 47% of its movement, holding movement keys against geometry it had never planned against, for nine minutes.

  **Why the existing guard did not catch it.** `driveToExit` already knew a transition can happen mid-drive; `bbd5871` taught it to tell a real transition from a teleporter pad via `exitAccepted()`. But every one of those checks is reached *only after a drive reports `teleported`* — a jump no legal step explains. Winning normally respawns the player and produces exactly that jump, which is why the job passed far more often than it failed. **A bot that is stuck produces no jump at all**, so nothing ever asked the question, and each remaining round spent its full budget driving at a dead exit. The fix asks at the top of every round instead.

  **The distinction that made the fix non-trivial, found by the tests rather than by reasoning.** The obvious implementation — call the existing `exitAccepted()` at the top of each round — broke three `botDrive` tests immediately. That predicate merges two facts needing opposite handling: a *running countdown* is still this level, and a bot in that state can and should go on to reach the exit and report `arrived`; a *changed exit tile* means the level is gone. Only the second justifies abandoning the drive. Hence a new, strictly narrower `levelAdvancedUnderUs()`. Reporting a legitimate arrival as a teleport would also have corrupted `run-balancing-telemetry-multiplayer.mjs`, where that is the difference between `reachedExit` and `levelAdvanced`.

  **`teleported`, not `arrived`, and not a new outcome word.** It is the established vocabulary for "the player moved in a way stepping cannot explain — caller decides what it meant", and both consumers already handle it correctly. `arrived` would assert *this* bot took the exit, which is precisely the attribution `driveToExit` refuses to invent (it cannot tell its own win from a teammate's). Single-player is untouched by construction: the base predicate is a literal `false`.

  **What is proven and what is not.** Proven: the mechanism, the fix, and that it was needed — the new test fails with the check disabled and passes with it, and the real job runs end to end green locally. **Not proven: that the original 19.5-minute occurrence of 2026-08-07 was this same mechanism.** That run predates the deadline guard and produced no dump, so nothing can be checked against it. It is consistent with this cause and no other candidate survives, but the honest status is inferred. If the job hangs again, it is a new item with fresh evidence, not this one reopened.

  **Two process notes worth more than the fix.** The refuted lead: for a week the item's standing hypothesis was the loot treadmill, on the strength of `loot 36.2t (75%)` from a *forced 25-second* sample — the whole-phase breakdown from a real occurrence reads `loot 13.8t (2%)`. The bullet that recorded it had said in advance that a 25-second window early in a run proves nothing and to compare against a real one before acting; following that instruction is what killed the hypothesis. And the diagnostic itself misled: `dumpExitGate` indexed the *live* roster with indices derived from the *planned* one, so under the very length mismatch it printed one line above, its exit-gate lines named arbitrary enemies — which produced a confident "the exit room was blocked, so the run was unwinnable anyway" that the data could not support. It now leads with the map change and refuses to print those lines at all when the rosters are different records. A diagnostic that emits a plausible wrong number is worse than one that emits nothing.

- [x] **Map colours and pickup appearance in the player guide, with captured screenshots (2026-08-17).** The backlog asked to *"explain mapcolors and drop color/appearance in user docs. should include screenshots of dropped keys/items"*. New [`doc/user/colors-and-pickups.md`](../user/colors-and-pickups.md): legends for the corner minimap, the automap and the exported map PNG, plus what a dropped item and a key look like — backed by 13 committed screenshots under `doc/user/img/`, the first images this repo has ever carried in its docs.

  **The pickup half mattered more than it sounds, because appearance is the entire signal.** Every pickup in the game is the same two nested squares at waist height; there is no health cross and no ammo box. Colour is the only thing distinguishing health from swap from five ammo pools, and it was written down nowhere. The map half fixes two specifics that actively mislead: acid is orange on both maps and green in the world (deliberately — `renderMinimap`'s own comment says it must never be confused with the green exit pulse), and an uncollected key is gold on the minimap whatever colour the key is.

  **Writing it surfaced a shipped bug, fixed first: every key in the world drew blue.** `collectKeyBillboards` takes `gateColors` with a `[]` default for gate-less fixtures, and the engine's only call site omitted it — so every key resolved through the `?? 1` fallback regardless of its gate, while the door, both map dots, the HUD pip and the locked-door toast all had it right. beta-7's changelog had promised the key sprite by name as part of that set. The existing sprite tests could not have caught it: they all pass their own `gateColors`, so the defect lived entirely in the wiring, which is why the regression test for it is at engine level and asserts the key's *bezel* tones — the faces are shared with the HUD pips, so a face assertion passes with the bug present.

  **Three stale doc claims corrected alongside**, all verified by running the code rather than re-reading the prose: `mechanics.md` said a key-locked door is blue (there are four colours, never reused within a level); both `mechanics.md` and `hud-and-ui.md` quoted the toast as "You need a key!" when it names the colour; and `level-design.md` said a level locks "at most six doorways" when the cap is four *rooms* (`MAX_GATE_ROOMS = GATE_COLOR_COUNT`, no override at the call site) — wrong in the number and the unit, in a bullet that already said a key opens every door of its room.

  **The screenshots are regenerable, not hand-taken**: `npm run report:doc-screenshots`, documented in [`testing.md`](testing.md#documentation-screenshots). Three things about it are worth carrying forward.

  - **Four of the eight pickup kinds are unphotographable by playing.** `rollLoot` filters rockets/smg/gas out until the matching weapon is owned, and the generator decides static pickups from the *previous* level's carryover — so a fresh level 1 cannot contain them however the fixture is written, and IDKFA grants weapons long after generation. Hence four mutating `debug*` test hooks, the first in an object that was otherwise purely observational. They are gated exactly as every other hook is and vanish from a production build; the standing rule written next to them is that the balancing bot must never call them, because a bot that can conjure ammo invalidates every economy number it reports.
  - **The camera never walks — it turns on the spot and the subject is spawned in front of it.** That avoids the turn-burst-versus-movement-duration trap entirely, and needs no cheats: IDDQD would have stamped a "cheated run" badge into every HUD in every shot.
  - **Output is byte-stable across runs**, which was verified rather than assumed (a re-run reported 12 of 13 unchanged, and the 13th was one whose crop had deliberately changed). The load-bearing part is seeding `Math.random` before any module loads — `?seed=` pins gameplay rolls but *not* `textures.ts`'s per-texel jitter, which is the thing that would otherwise churn every image on every regeneration.

  **Two Playwright traps cost real time here and are commented at both sites, because each presents as an unexplained hang rather than an error:** `waitForFunction` polls on requestAnimationFrame by default, which the virtual clock owns, so it needs `polling: 100`; and the briefing's 1200ms dismiss lock is measured against `performance.now()`, so it must be pumped past in *virtual* time — sleeping in real time leaves it armed forever and Space is silently swallowed for the rest of the run.

  **The fixture's trailing blank lines are load-bearing and measured.** The player spawns in the *corner* of room 0 (`pickSafeSpawn` → `farthestCorner`), and a corner can end up walled off by the pillars scattered through large rooms — which is what the fixture produced unpadded, leaving no room to line items up. `linesOfCode` feeds the layout seed, so blank lines re-roll the level while changing nothing it contains; the padding was chosen by generating and scoring every value in a range, not guessed.

- [x] **Gamepad reload, interact and sprint (2026-08-17).** Found while investigating the reload-feedback report, fixed separately. Three keyboard actions had no gamepad binding at all, and the most serious was not the one that surfaced it: **`F` interact was unreachable on a pad, so lore terminals could not be read and fake walls could not be opened — secret rooms were unreachable for a gamepad player entirely.** Reload and sprint were the other two. `X` reloads, `A` interacts, `L3` sprints while held.

  **A doc comment had claimed the reload binding existed for as long as reloading has.** `InputSnapshot.reload` read *"Reload requested this frame (`R`, or the gamepad's X)"* while no `GAMEPAD_BUTTON_X` constant existed and `pollGamepad` never touched `reloadQueued`. Nothing tested it, because the tests covered the buttons that were wired rather than the ones the docs promised — the gap is only visible by diffing the keyboard's action list against the pad's.

  **Sprint routes through `isDown("ShiftLeft"/"ShiftRight")` rather than a new `InputSnapshot` field**, because that is what the engine already asks and what `RECORDED_KEYS` already carries across the multiplayer wire and into replays — so a pad sprint is recorded and networked for free. Deliberately *not* written into `this.keys`: a real Shift release would then clear the pad's state too, and vice versa. `captureSnapshot` switched from reading `this.keys` directly to `isDown`, which is the line that makes a gamepad sprint survive playback — the `botRotSpeedMul` bug (a simulation input the replay never recorded) is the shape this avoids.
- [x] **The reload was already animated; it was just invisible (2026-08-17).** The backlog item read *"auto-reloading is barely player-aware … only a hint in the hud bar. should have anim, sound and maybe even a toast"*, and its premise was wrong: a viewmodel dip **and** four per-weapon reload sound sequences shipped in the same commit as the mechanic itself. The report was still correct — a player who has played this game for months had never seen the animation — so the work was finding out why, not building what the item asked for. **Recorded here because the next reader of a line like that would otherwise re-derive the same false conclusion.**

  **The animation was never broken.** Traced in a real browser by patching the live 2D context and watching the vertical extent of drawing in the weapon's column band across a reload: it slides y=400 → 479 → 400 on a clean sine, exactly as `viewmodel.ts` intends. Two things made it imperceptible, and only one of them was about motion.

  **The weapon is drawn almost the colour of the floor.** The strip it occupies averages luminance **30/255**, and `OUTLINE_COLOR` was `#0a0a0d` — luminance ~10. A dark outline, on a dark weapon, on a dark floor. That constant's own doc comment says it exists to give the flat fills "a defined edge against the dark backdrop"; it could not do that job at that value. Lifted to `#4a505a`, which is what made the silhouette — and therefore any motion it makes — legible at all.

  **The downward dip was already spent, and this was measured before turning the knob.** `HUD_HEIGHT` is 58 and the weapon clears the bar by only ~72px, so travel beyond that goes into screen the HUD already covers. Sampling the fraction of the weapon's visible strip that changes: **59px of dip → 13%, 73px → 12.5%, the full 96px → 11.7%.** Flat, and mildly *decreasing*. The user asked to "make the dip dip more"; deepening `RELOAD_DOWN_PX` would have shown nothing. The sideways component is the half that moves inside visible area, so that is what grew — `RELOAD_SIDE_PX` 24 → 96, since 24px read as a twitch.

  **The sound was mixed to be missed.** The four reload voices peaked at 0.13–0.22 against a gunshot's 0.5, and `audio.test.ts` asserted that on purpose (*"well below its own shot, so it reads as mechanism"*). It did read as mechanism, and it was also inaudible under the gunfire it always follows. Raised ~1.6x, and the assertion re-argued rather than renumbered: the ceiling moved 0.5 → 0.8 of a shot with a floor added beneath it, so it cannot drift back to silence.

  **The regression guards pin magnitudes now.** `viewmodel.test.ts` asserted only that the dip moved *down and across* — a direction-only assertion, which is precisely what let a technically-correct animation be invisible for months at 100% coverage. It now pins the sideways travel, and `audio.test.ts` pins a peak floor on all four voices, not just the pistol's.

  **No toast**, by user decision: ghidra reloads after every rocket and the shotgun every two shells, so a per-reload toast would fire constantly on exactly the weapons that reload most.

- [x] **Two more gore levels, Excessive and Absurd (2026-08-17).** `count` is not the axis that gates a tier above Extreme, and that is the whole design. A hit spawns `(3..5) x count` (`engine.ts`'s `damageEnemy`), so at Extreme's 16x a hit is 48–80 particles and **four to six hits already saturate `MAX_BLOOD_PARTICLES = 300`** — raising `count` alone adds no blood, it evicts the tier's own older blood sooner, and in sustained combat a higher-count tier can read as *less* gory than a lower one. The cap and the stain clock are therefore what move, and `maxParticles` became a per-tier field rather than a global constant. Excessive: 300→600 cap, 6s→8.25s stains, count 16→30. Absurd sets `stainDuration: Infinity` — FIFO eviction at 1800 becomes the only thing that ever removes a stain, which is also what bounds the tier — count 45, plus a new `settledSize` axis (1.6x) applied to landed particles only.

  **The same lesson bit again one revision later, which is why it is worth reading twice.** The tiers first shipped at count 20/24 with caps 400/1200; asked to give each "half again as many particles", the obvious edit was `count` alone — 20→30, 24→36. Measured over a fixed 12-hit fight, Excessive then held **333** live particles against **343** before the change: the 400 cap was the binding constraint, so a 50% spawn increase was worth *nothing on screen* (slightly negative, within noise). Raising the cap alongside it (400→600) put the same fight at **529**, i.e. the +50% actually arriving. Absurd, which had cap headroom, moved 662→890 on the spawn bump alone and got 1200→1800 for the long-fight ceiling. **The knob named "particles" and the number of particles you can see are different things at every tier above Extreme** — measure the live count, never reason from the multiplier.

  **Absurd then went to count 45** (user call, "can still be more absurd"). Measured on the same 12-hit fight: 890→**1082** live, cap never reached. On a level-length 60-hit fight it holds **1707** and **first touches the 1800 cap at hit 20** — so from roughly a fifth of the way into a sustained level onward, the cap governs and further `count` only churns the FIFO faster.

  **Asked for more a third time, the answer turned out not to be more particles at all — and the advice given one round earlier ("the cap is the lever now") was wrong.** Rendering four candidate configurations and measuring the fraction of the canvas actually painted red: cap 1800→3000 moved *drawn* squares 1201→1235 and coverage 23.3%→**23.4%**, and 1800→4500 only reached 24.3%. Past a point the extra blood lands on floor that is already red — it costs frame time linearly and buys nothing visible. `settledSize` was equally spent, for a different reason: `renderBlood` clamps every particle at 6% of canvas height, and at `settledSize` 1.6 **everything within 5.3 tiles is already pinned at that clamp**, so raising the multiplier only widens the pinned band. The binding constraint was the clamp itself. Making it per-tier (`settledClamp`, 10% for Absurd only, airborne particles still on the global 6%) took a 40-hit fight from 17.7% to **45.7%** coverage — the floor becomes a solid sheet. **Three axes, and the only one that moved the picture was the one being treated as a safety rail rather than a knob.**

  Two guards keep that safe: the split is *settled vs airborne*, so the thing that grew is a stain lying at `z = 0` and the thing that produced the original "mountain of blood" — a big square in flight near the camera — is explicitly excluded and still clamps at 24px; and `BLOOD_MAX_SIZE_FRACTION` had to move above `GORE_MULTIPLIERS` in the file, since the table now references it and a `const` read before its declaration hits its temporal dead zone at module load. That is the same failure the original `GORE_KEY` bug had, caught here by reading the file rather than by a test.

  **`size` was deliberately not touched at either tier, and `BLOOD_MAX_SIZE_FRACTION` stayed global.** That is the axis behind the shipped "mountain of blood" bug (see the 2026-07 entry below): an oversized particle *near the camera* was the failure, so `settledSize` scales floor stains only and multiplies *into* the existing clamp rather than around it. Verified: a point-blank settled splat at Absurd renders at exactly the same 24px (6% of a 400px-high canvas) as one at Extreme.

  **Measured against the real functions in a real browser**, the recipe the original bug fix used, because playing headless can't reliably land shots (pointer lock). Twelve hits half a second apart, then ten seconds of nothing: Extreme 273 live particles → **0**; Excessive 343 → 0; Absurd 662 → **609**, i.e. the floor stays painted. Draw calls during the fight: 220 / 294 / 550 squares. **Perf: fine at 60Hz on real hardware, unmeasured at 120Hz.** The harness could say almost nothing here — a headless `s3-stress` vs the new `s3-splatter` cell reads 5.00ms vs 5.10ms busy, a 0.10ms delta at n=1 at or below the ~0.1–0.2ms calibration floor, and headless is precisely blind to the raster cost this change adds (up to 1800 `fillRect`s at up to 40px). The session machine had no display, so the headed run never happened. What settled it was the user playing the shipped tiers on their own 60Hz setup and reporting no problem. That clears the 16.7ms budget; it does not speak to 8.3ms, and 120Hz-viability is a target this project takes seriously enough to have built a whole render-quality tier around (see the Classic/Sharp entry). If Absurd ever gets a framedrop report, that is the gap.

  **`loadGoreLevel`'s hand-written name list is gone**, derived from `GORE_MULTIPLIERS` instead (`Object.hasOwn`, so a stored `"constructor"` can't pass). That list was the one line in a change of this shape that `tsc` cannot see: a missed entry saves fine and silently reverts to Normal on the next reload — the same silent-persistence class as the original `GORE_KEY` TDZ bug. Also added the fallback the engine never had: a replay segment stores `gore` as a bare string and hands it straight back at playback, so an unknown tier yielded `undefined` and threw on the first `.count`. No storage-schema bump (a widened domain is not a changed value shape), no `balanceHash` change and no `defaultHighscore` regeneration (gore is exempt by construction), and no determinism risk — blood draws from `Math.random()`, not the seeded PRNG, on purpose.

  **`s3-stress` and `c3-stress` stayed pinned to Extreme** rather than being re-pointed at the new ceiling: their comments claimed to measure "the particle ceiling", which this change silently falsified, but re-pointing them would break budget histories that predate the new tiers. The new `s3-splatter` cell runs the identical macro at Absurd, so the pair reads directly as what the top tier costs. Multiplayer still hardcodes `DEFAULT_GORE_LEVEL` (`sessionEngine.ts`) and ignores the preference entirely — pre-existing, untouched here.

- [x] **The file tree belongs to the tab its workspace was loaded from (2026-08-16).** One `#file-tree` outside every panel, fed by one set of workspace globals, meant the tabs only picked a *loading method*: load the Demos campaign, switch to GitHub, and the demo's files were still listed below. Each workspace *source* now has its own pane and its own status line — Local and Continue share one, since both go through `pickWorkspace()` — and `#workspace-name` follows the visible tab, which is what stops a failed GitHub load from relabelling the campaign you are mid-run in. Verified in a real browser as well as in vitest: the reported symptom was seen by eye, and the suite cannot see layout.

  **The notes item's own "each remembered independently within the session" was built, shown, and rejected on sight** (user call, same day). Keeping the demo campaign loaded under Demos while a repo loaded under GitHub meant two tabs each presenting a loaded workspace when only one was what the engine played — "way better, but … only the github one is actually loaded". Both trees were genuinely live (a `RemoteFileHandle` fetches lazily, a `FileSystemFileHandle` stays valid for the session, so clicking either worked), which is exactly why it read as a lie rather than as an obvious bug. A load now clears every other slot, so a tree on screen always means *the* loaded workspace. That deleted the whole cross-tab-adoption path — `adoptWorkspaceSlot`, its "a file click switches the active source" rule and the campaign-save question that came with it — and left one writer of the `workspace*` globals, `commitWorkspaceSlot`, with ~40 downstream readers untouched.

  **The open design question ("what does switching tabs mean for the running game?") resolved as display-only** (user decision), and survived the change above intact: switching tabs swaps what you look at and never disturbs the level. Settings and Multiplayer own no workspace and say so in that spot rather than showing someone else's tree. A marker on the tab holding the loaded workspace says which source the game is running from from anywhere. It went through three rounds of user feedback: a dot read as a record indicator, trailing the label read as a footnote marker, and once it was a play triangle the obvious next question was what it does when the game is paused — so it became a transport readout (▶ / ❚❚) off the engine's existing `onFreezeChange` edge, which incidentally gave that wiring its first DOM-observable signal (the pause test previously asserted only "did not throw"). **U+23F8 PAUSE renders as a solid block at this size** — indistinguishable from the rejected dot, and no better at 13px — so the glyph is two `❚` bars, checked in a browser rather than assumed.

  **The marker shipped wrapping the tab labels, and the verification is why.** "❚❚ GitHub" broke onto two lines; the browser check that preceded it had loaded the demo campaign, so it only ever measured "Demos" — the shorter label — and reported no overflow. Re-measured properly (every tab count × every marked tab × both states against an unmarked baseline) the damage was wider than the report: at four tabs only `❚❚ GitHub` wrapped, but with Continue showing *every* marker state wrapped. Cause and fix in `decisions.md`; the short version is that a flex item's min-content width is its longest *word*, so the space between glyph and label let flexbox wrap the tab rather than grow it. That measurement also turned up a pre-existing bug it had nothing to do with: six tabs never fit the strip at all and the Settings gear was cut off past the sidebar edge, now a wrapped second row.

  **The Settings tab was `flex: 1` for a single glyph** (user call, on the same screenshot). It only *looked* wide when the strip had slack — in tight rows it already shrank — so `flex: 0 1 auto` (shrink, never grow) is the fix, and the gear went 12px → 17px because a gear at label size reads as a speck rather than a control. Both of those would have grown the strip's height, so `.tab-btn`'s `line-height` is now pinned at the 15px it computed to anyway, and the marker's optical lift moved from `vertical-align` to `position: relative` — measured, `vertical-align` grows the line box and cost 1px on every marked state. Row height is now 29px across every tab count and marker state that fits one row.

  **One behaviour did change, and it is unreachable by hand.** Every loader now claims its own tab synchronously before its first `await`, which makes "the status and tree a load writes are the ones on screen" an invariant instead of an accident — and incidentally stops `clearCampaignSave()`'s bounce off the Continue tab from yanking the view mid-load. In the app each loader's button only exists inside its own panel, so it is a no-op; one multiplayer-gating test drove `#select-workspace` while the Multiplayer tab was selected and was updated to assert what it is actually about (the Host sub-tab bouncing to Join). Coverage held at the same 99.9/99.53/99.76/100 across both rounds.

- [x] **All settings moved into a dedicated Settings tab, with a Classic/Sharp render-quality choice (2026-08-16).** The Gameplay/Audio/Texture-Pack sections left the always-visible sidebar header for a sixth launch-bar tab (gear glyph), returning that vertical space to the file tree; every control id survived unchanged, so unit tests and most harnesses were unaffected (the three scripts touching in-panel controls click `#tab-settings` first, and the session bootstrap restores the previously selected tab afterwards).

  **Render quality is ONE paired choice, deliberately.** Sharp = 1280×800 internal + the half-res floor-cast, auto-paired structurally in `renderScene` (`AUTO_HALF_RES_FLOOR_ENABLED`, threshold >640 wide) — the frame-budget audit measured the pairing at ~5.5ms busy (locked, 120Hz-viable) and the unpaired 1280×800 at 14.5ms with dropped frames, so the bad combo is unreachable in the app and stays measurable only via `perf:bench --flag floorhalfauto`. Resolution applies at engine construction only (zBuffer is sized from the canvas there); shot/aim resolution stays on the fixed 640, keeping replays and mixed-quality multiplayer deterministic — pinned by an engine test proving crosshair highlight and an actual shot agree on the same dead-center enemy at both sizes, and that billboards draw past x=640 (the zBuffer really covers the wider canvas).

  **The intro tour's blanket "never switch a launch tab" rule was relaxed to switch-declaratively-and-always-restore** (user decision): steps carry `activateTabId`, tabless steps re-activate the tour's initial tab, and `stop()` restores it on Done/Skip/Escape alike. The tour walks the settings panel (name, difficulty, render quality, textures) with the render-quality WHY doc-pinned (retro/640/120Hz/next-level) — the framing that keeps the option from reading as an arbitrary graphics toggle, since it is also the answer to the open 120Hz framedrop report.

- [x] **The chosen online texture pack is remembered (2026-08-15).** The backlog asked for it in one line — *"if user set a online-wad, store it in prefs and restore automatically (add a button to clear wad textures / resetting the pref)"* — and picking a pack survived exactly until the next reload, which made it read as a per-session novelty rather than a setting.

  **What is stored is the catalog id**, never a URL and never the WAD: `codeenstein-wad-pack` holds a short name from a list the app already ships. It is validated against the live catalog on read rather than trusted, because a pack can be dropped from `ONLINE_WAD_CATALOG` (a license re-check, a dead upstream) in a build someone already has a preference for — a stale id has to read as "no preference" rather than as a fetch of a file nobody serves.

  **The asymmetry needed a decision rather than silence.** A local `.wad` cannot be remembered, because a `File` is not a durable handle. So picking a local file now *clears* a remembered online pack: otherwise the next reload would quietly restore the online pack over the file the player just chose, which reads as the choice being ignored.

  **Restore is fire-and-forget, and a failure keeps the preference.** The pack is cosmetic, the fetch is a real round-trip for tens of megabytes, and nothing about launching a level depends on it — so a failure leaves the built-in textures up and says so, exactly as a failed manual pick does. The preference survives it because the usual cause is being offline, which is not a reason to forget what someone chose.

  `TextureManager.resetToDefaults()` backs the revert button and is a pointer swap, not a rebuild: `loadFromWad` always swapped `active` and left `defaults` alone, so the originals were never discarded.

  **No `STORAGE_SCHEMA_VERSION` bump, and the rule is now written into `storageSchema.ts`**: that constant is for a changed *value shape*, and a new key is not one — nothing written earlier needs interpreting differently, and the key's absence already means "no pack".

  Verified in a real browser against a real 99 MB WAD, 10/10 — pick, revert button appearing, preference landing, automatic restore on reload, revert, and staying reverted across another reload. Two user docs asserted the opposite and were corrected: `hud-and-ui.md` called the pack session-only, and `privacy.md`'s table of stored keys did not list it.

- [x] **`generate:default-highscore` exits when it is done (2026-08-15).** It wrote its output, closed its browser, then hung indefinitely at 0% CPU — measured at ~13 minutes of work followed by 16 of nothing before it was killed, which reads as "this job takes 30 minutes" and had been recorded that way. `ensureDevServer` spawns vite with piped stdio, and those pipes are live handles: node will not exit while they are open. The server was released only from a `process.on("exit")` handler, so the handler waited for an exit that waited for the handler. Every `process.exit(1)` path was unaffected — an explicit exit *does* run exit handlers — which is why this only ever showed on **success**, the one path nobody watches to the end. Fixed by stopping the server explicitly where `main()` finishes, the same shape `run-balancing-telemetry.mjs` already used at both of its exits. Verified by reproducing it both ways rather than by reasoning, and the other consumers were audited rather than assumed: `ensureDevServer` has exactly two callers, and the `plan-order` server the generator starts indirectly already tears down in a `finally` — which is what makes a one-line fix sufficient.

- [x] **Weapon reloading shipped, and the 2026-07-16 backlog item that scoped it moves here (2026-08-15).** Magazines for pistol/shotgun/gdb/ghidra (9/2/45/1), `R` for an early reload, auto-reload on empty, with a procedural animation and four new reload sounds. The item's research held up; what it did not anticipate is below.

  **The shotgun needed its own ammo first, and that turned out to be the load-bearing half.** It spent **4 bullets a pull out of the pistol's pool**, so "mag: 2" had no coherent meaning and a real magazine would have stranded pistol ammo inside a gun that cannot fire it. The user's call — give it dedicated `shells` — dissolved both problems and is worth having on its own: the pistol and the shotgun are the two *starting* weapons, so one shared pool made them rivals from the first minute of every run. It now spends 1 shell, damage and pump cadence untouched. Third time this repo has split a pool (gdb off `bullets`, Friday Hotfix's `gas` from day one); the shotgun was the last weapon still sharing.

  **Conservation is the part that needed care.** Loaded rounds have left the reserve, so everything that *reports* or *persists* ammo must add them back or a magazine's worth evaporates at every level boundary — a save, a multiplayer transition, the score's ammo bonus. One helper (`pooledAmmo`) owns that; the HUD subtracts them out again for the familiar `9 / 70` split.

  **Two rules fell out of the model rather than being chosen.** A weapon switch **cancels** a reload, deliberately breaking the neighbouring "cooldown survives a switch" rule — that one exists so the shotgun's pump cannot be switch-cancelled, and a reload is the opposite case; it needs a companion `reloadingWeaponIndex` because multiplayer reconciliation can change `weaponIndex` with no local input. And loaded rounds **return to the reserve at a level transition**, which is what keeps `EngineCarryover` (it rides the multiplayer wire *and* sits inside every recorded replay) free of per-weapon fields.

  **`R` reloads, interact moved to `F`, fullscreen to `Alt`+`Enter` — the first modifier-aware binding in the codebase.** Nothing anywhere read `altKey`/`ctrlKey`/`shiftKey`/`metaKey` before. Verified by a real physical keypress, because no harness here can: synthetic events cannot tell you whether a chord is eaten before the page sees it, and Playwright cannot see fullscreen at all (see [Testing](testing.md)). That is the `Ctrl+W` lesson, and it is why the binding was chosen deliberately rather than by taste.

  **Old replays stopped offering a Watch button (user's observation, mid-review).** They were still offered and failed *on click* with "recorded under different game balance" — worse than not offering them. `computeBalanceHash` cannot answer this at render time because it folds in the per-level enemy roster and the board has no maps, so a replay now also records a map-independent `simulationHash`. **Absent counts as a mismatch there**, the opposite of `balanceHashMatches`: that function decides whether to refuse a replay already asked for, this one decides whether to *offer* it, and a hash-less recording predates the guard — which now means it predates magazines. Storage schema versioning was considered and rejected as the wrong lever: it governs the shape of stored keys, not the rules of the game, and the scores themselves are still valid.

  **The bot had four ways to break, none of them in the item's single "bot-side gap" bullet.** It recorded a *dispatched keypress* as a shot, which would have put its cadence permanently out of phase with the engine — silently, in every future capture. It read only the reserve when deciding it was dry, so it would have walked in to melee holding a loaded gun. Its mine give-up counter burned during reloads (a 2s gdb reload alone exhausts the 40-tick budget). And a reload set neither `fire` nor `fireOnCooldown`, which `detectAnomalies` reads as a stall — the flag that already means "aimed and willing, held back by cadence". `BotPlayerState` is now exported and shared instead of hand-written in four places; one of those copies already carried a comment warning about exactly that drift.

  **The bundled board was regenerated, and `verify:replay` is what forced it.** The three shipped replays predate magazines, so the new render gate hid their buttons — and that verifier exists to fail loudly on a board with no usable payload. Back-filling was considered and rejected: `--backfill-rot-speed` worked because that value was *recoverable* from write order, whereas a `simulationHash` is not — those runs really were played under different rules, and stamping the current hash would restore the button and hand the player a desyncing replay. Regenerated for real; `verify:replay` then passed in 239s, frame-exact per level with the whole carryover ladder intact.

  **A generator bug found on the way, fixed separately.** `generate:default-highscore` wrote its output and then hung indefinitely at 0% CPU — ~13 minutes of work reading as 30. `ensureDevServer` spawns vite with piped stdio (live handles that keep node alive) while the server was released only from a `process.on("exit")` handler, so the handler waited for an exit that waited for the handler. Every `process.exit(1)` path was unaffected, which is why it only ever showed on success. See the entry for it below.

  **Still unmeasured, deliberately**: every supply number here (starting shells, drop sizes, the loot-weight split, the four reload durations). Parked to a night run — the design, the sizing and the trap that rules out a cheap single-arm comparison are in `notes`.

- [x] **Boss key (F9) shipped, and the backlog item that scoped it moves here (2026-08-15).** One press covers the page with a plain code editor showing the source file the current level was generated from, highlighted; the same press brings it back. The item's central constraint held all the way through: **a browser cannot minimise its own window** — `window.minimize()` does not exist, `moveTo`/`resizeTo`/`close` apply only to windows the page itself opened — so the disguise happens inside the page.

  **Settled with the user before building:** `F9` (a function key, because the disguise is a focusable code surface and a letter key would be swallowed by it); **Escape swallowed** while it is up, so only F9 dismisses; live **everywhere**, launch screen included, since that screen shows the game's own branding and file tree; and in co-op it **hides without pausing**.

  **Four things turned out to be load-bearing, none of them in the original scoping.**

  - **The listener must be on `window`, capture phase.** `InputController` binds keydown to the *canvas*, so a canvas-scoped boss key works exactly once: the overlay takes focus and the second press never arrives.
  - **Fullscreen has to be left synchronously in the handler.** `F` fullscreens the canvas alone, and only the fullscreen element and its descendants paint — a DOM overlay renders as literally nothing. The item had this half right (`exitFullscreen()` needs no gesture) and the other half missing: *re-entering* does need one, and the dismiss keypress is it, so that call cannot be awaited behind anything.
  - **The pause is a real blur, not a synthesised one.** Focusing the overlay blurs the canvas, which is the path alt-tab has always taken, so nothing is injected into the input stream and a run being recorded stays honest. No engine API was added.
  - **Enter and Space have to be swallowed too, not just Escape.** `GameHud` keeps a *window*-scoped listener for Enter/Space/Escape, so Space behind the disguise would have dismissed a pending level-start briefing and started the level underneath it.

  **Three claims in the backlog item were wrong, and each cost a change it did not budget for.** *"`bgm.ts` has `pause()`"* — it had `stop()`, which is never called anywhere in `src/`, so music played straight through every pause the game has; `pause()`/`resume()` are new, and wiring music to *ordinary* pauses was deliberately left alone as its own decision. *"The data is already on hand"* for the level's source — it was not: the text lived as a local and died when the launch function returned, and `ParsedFile` keeps no copy, so `launchLevel` now takes it. And the line references (`engine.ts:~2635`/`~2772`) were stale; the real gate is 2658-2661, 136 lines ahead of `levelTime += dt` at 2796 — the *claim* they supported was still true.

  **A co-op guest cannot show its own level.** It receives only a serialised `GameMap` — no path, no `ParsedFile`, no text — so it shows a bundled stand-in, and shipping source over the wire would be a protocol change. Co-op also cannot pause at all: `localInputSampler.sampleAndReset()` drains *and zeroes* blur/escape/click before they reach the engine, because lockstep cannot be frozen unilaterally.

  **On verification, which is the part worth keeping.** Unit tests pin the state transitions, and they earned it — they caught the new highlighter silently dropping the `/*` of every block comment, i.e. rendering a file with characters missing. A real-browser Playwright run with genuine CDP key events covered overlay, source file, pause, Escape-swallowing and the title/favicon round-trip, 13/13. **The fullscreen round-trip could not be covered by either**, because Chromium under Playwright does not honour the Fullscreen API at all — see the new paragraph in [Testing](testing.md#what-the-test-suite-structurally-cannot-catch). The user verified windowed and fullscreen by hand; both work, which also retires the item's `Ctrl+W`-shaped worry about whether F9 reaches the page at all.

- [x] **The "~30% ranged miss rate is two defects" backlog item is closed and moved here (2026-08-15).** It ran from 2026-08-11, and both defects are resolved in opposite directions: **defect 2 (the aim is stale)** shipped as the 2-tile-gated `BOT_AIM_LEAD` on 2026-08-14, and **defect 1 (a fixed angular fire tolerance)** was settled on 2026-08-13 as **no measurable effect** — by the powered three-arm re-run with a real null control, which is precisely the protocol the item was still listing as the thing to do when it was read a day later. Both entries are below. Nothing lived only in the backlog: the mechanism, the three withdrawn hypotheses and the guard-resolution correction are in those entries, in `decisions.md`, and in `balancing-telemetry.md`'s guard-floor paragraph.

  **The residual, stated so it is not mistaken for an open thread.** Gated leading buys +2.8pp at 6-8 tiles and, by construction, nothing closer; what still misses is dominated by the bot's own facing error — **0.0859 rad at 0-2 tiles, above even Casual's `fireAngleEps`** — which is a property of keyboard-quantised aim, not of prediction. That is already tracked as the first of the three instrument-shaped design choices under the deathmatch-opponent item in `notes`. A third fix aimed at the predictor is the specific thing this item's own history argues against.

- [x] **Two stale backlog items removed rather than moved (2026-08-15).** The **Stage 6** item in `notes` had been a pure correction for three days — "this is not open work, do not rebuild it" — duplicating `decisions.md`'s *Approaches Measured and Rejected* verdict and the Stage 6 entry below. A backlog item whose entire content is "this item should not exist" is the shape that file's own header warns about. And the lane bullet under the parked MP campaign said `pc-tobi-laptop-guthand.lan` "is listed but the runner resolves only three hosts, so it sat idle and is worth investigating": it is **commented out** in `ssh-hosts.env`, and has been since 2026-08-12 23:39 — *before* the 2026-08-13 capture that bullet describes — while `readHostList()` (`scripts/lib/sshRunner.mjs`) filters `#` lines. Three is the correct count and there is nothing to investigate; the item's own 2026-08-12 "four hosts, none commented out" audit line had gone stale within hours of being written. Corrected in place.

- [x] **Numeric env knobs fail loudly instead of becoming `NaN` (2026-08-14).** All **53** numeric `CODEENSTEIN_*` knobs were a bare `Number(process.env.X ?? default)`. `Number("abc")` is `NaN` and `NaN` propagates silently: `CODEENSTEIN_MULTIPLAYER_PORT=abc` did not fail, it listened somewhere unpredictable, and `CODEENSTEIN_CAPTURE_ATTEMPTS=6o` (the o/0 slip) produced a capture whose denominator — and therefore every rate — was `NaN`, discoverable only hours later. Exactly **two** validated, and both guarded a value whose corruption would have been *invisible*; that was the argument for doing the rest, not against it.

  New `scripts/lib/envNumber.mjs` with optional `integer`/`min`/`max`, used by all ten tooling scripts: ports bounded to 1..65535, counts and durations to integers >= 1 (>= 0 where zero is meaningful), replay speed to a positive fraction. `CODEENSTEIN_TELEMETRY_SEED`'s hand-rolled `0..0xffffffff` check is now expressed the same way and is no longer a special case.

  **It trims before testing for emptiness, and that is the subtle half.** `Number("")` and `Number("   ")` are both **0**, not `NaN` — so a variable a shell left blank is the one bad value a plain `Number()` gets wrong *without* leaving a `NaN` behind to notice. Everything else at least fails loudly downstream eventually; a silent zero does not.

  **`scripts/multiplayer-server.mjs` carries its own copy rather than importing, deliberately.** That file is a single dependency-free script — `docker/signaling/Dockerfile` copies it alone, with no `node_modules` and no build step, and says so in its own comment. An import would have broken the deployed image *at runtime* while passing every local test. Both copies carry that warning so a later DRY pass has to notice it.

  **Deploy note for the signaling backend**: a server already running with a typo'd value now fails at its next restart rather than continuing with a wrong number. That is the intent, and it is the one behavioural change here.

  Verified by running the entry points rather than only the tests — the class of check `notes` already records learning the hard way. The server rejects `PORT=abc` with exit 1 and still starts and serves on a valid port; the capture rejects `ATTEMPTS=6o` with exit 1 and, with a valid value, proceeds to its own clean-tree refusal.

- [x] **The aim lead ships, gated at 2 tiles — the first attempt lost to re-aim cost, not to bad prediction (2026-08-14).** `BOT_AIM_LEAD` had been off since 2026-08-11 with the diagnosis "linear extrapolation of a *curving* path errs most close in", and the backlog recorded that settling it needed engine-side position logging that did not exist. Built it, and the diagnosis was wrong.

  **New instrumentation.** `shot` events now carry `tgt` — the target's position now plus its two preceding frames — and `sx/sy/sdx/sdy`, the shooter's position and facing. Two previous frames rather than one, because the predictor being scored estimates velocity from the *previous* displacement, so one frame is not enough to reconstruct it. Held in an engine-side array index-aligned with `enemies` rather than as fields on `Enemy`: that type is plain data shared with the map layer and serialized for replays, and widening it would put diagnostics into the replay digest for no gameplay reason. Allocated only when an event log is attached, so normal play pays nothing.

  **The predictor was never the problem.** Scored against where enemies actually ended up, leading is *more* accurate at every range — it beats no-lead on **93.8%** of shots and cuts positional error 38-60%:

  | range | ang err no-lead | ang err lead | gain | re-aim demanded | ratio |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
  | 0-2 | 0.0328 | 0.0248 | 0.0080 | **0.0366** | **4.6x** |
  | 2-4 | 0.0162 | 0.0106 | 0.0056 | 0.0074 | 1.3x |
  | 4-6 | 0.0085 | 0.0044 | 0.0041 | 0.0050 | 1.2x |
  | 6-8 | 0.0069 | 0.0033 | 0.0036 | 0.0045 | 1.2x |

  **The cost is the re-aim.** Bearing rate goes as `v_perp / dist`, so the same world-space lead is a far larger angle up close: at 0-2 tiles it demands **4.6x more turning than the accuracy it buys**. The bot's aim is keyboard-quantised, and its measured facing error at 0-2 is already **0.0859 rad** — above even Casual's `fireAngleEps` — so it is firing mid-correction and the lead hands it more correction to do. That 4.6 / 1.3 / 1.2 / 1.2 ratio profile tracks the original arm's measured damage (**-8.9 / -4.4 / -3.9 / -1.5pp**) closely enough to be the same phenomenon.

  **Fix and result.** `BOT_AIM_LEAD_MIN_DIST = 2.0`, read off that cliff rather than tuned; setting it to 0 reproduces the 2026-08-11 arm exactly, which keeps the retry single-variable against the original. Three arms of 80 on staged curl, Casual+Gamer x hard, with two identical `lead-off` arms as a measured null control:

  | range | pooled off | gated | delta | null-control delta | z |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
  | 0-2 | 97.9% (18,212) | 98.0% (10,654) | +0.1pp | -0.0pp | 0.60 |
  | 2-4 | 78.7% (12,206) | 78.5% (6,805) | -0.2pp | -0.7pp | -0.39 |
  | 4-6 | 71.2% (16,106) | 72.3% (8,628) | +1.2pp | +0.3pp | 1.94 |
  | 6-8 | 66.5% (11,105) | **69.4%** (5,772) | **+2.8pp** | +0.3pp | **3.69** |

  Guard clean: levels-per-attempt **8.86** against a pooled 8.35 with a 0.35 null spread. **0-2 being unchanged is a mechanism check rather than a null** — the gate disables the lead inside 2 tiles, so anything else there would mean the gate was not working. And it **replicates**: an earlier run of the same three arms gave **+3.6pp (z=6.34)** at 6-8 on a different substrate. Default flipped ON.

  **A process failure worth more than the result, because it nearly shipped a wrong conclusion.** That earlier run was on the *wrong substrate*. `stage-campaign.mjs` refuses when the tree is not safe for `git checkout -B`; it refused because `engine.ts` was uncommitted at that moment; and its output went to `tail -1` unread — so all three arms played the 17-level demo campaign while everything downstream said curl. It surfaced only because `levels/attempt` read **16.5**, which is impossible on a 15-level campaign. That run's guard was therefore saturated and worthless, which is the exact failure mode this file already documents twice. **Two cheap checks now, and both belong in any staged capture: `ls demo-campaign | wc -l` immediately after staging, and an ssh check that every lane is on the expected sha *and* carries the expected level count.** The lane half matters on its own — an earlier smoke capture emitted no `tgt` at all because a lane was running a stale checkout, and note that `CODEENSTEIN_CAPTURE_LOCAL_ONLY=1` does **not** prevent lane use, it only suppresses the refusal.
- [x] **The "Bot wedges" backlog item is closed and moved here (2026-08-14, user's call).** It ran from 2026-08-02 to 2026-08-14 and both of its halves are now resolved: the wedge rate was re-measured to zero across three repos and three distinct fixed mechanisms (entry below), and the oscillation thread's motivating number turned out to be smaller than the harness's own noise floor (entry below that). What follows is the content that existed *only* in the backlog item, preserved because it is not reconstructible from the code.

  **The two root causes, both fixed 2026-08-06.** The **loot treadmill** (`44b01c0`): the last hop now targets the drop's own coordinate rather than its tile centre (the engine collects within 0.5 of the drop, and centre-to-corner is 0.707); dynamic drops get an abandoned set and attempt counter keyed by coordinate, so an uncollectable one is written off instead of re-picked forever; the target is committed to while still on offer instead of re-chosen every waypoint; and a per-level `LOOT_BUDGET_FRACTION` bounds the total, since kills create drops and the pool otherwise refills faster than it drains. **serilog level 9 went 88,791 decisions / 14,268 tiles -> 2,823 / 535**, loot 87% -> 35%. Its **successor** was a **C# parser bug**, not navigation: an unmodified interface method defaulted to `private`, so 136 methods became 140 lockable rooms — **640 doors, 321 keys** — and `planRoute` gave up at `MAX_ITERATIONS`, returning `{ok:false, legs:286}` with legs populated even on failure. Levels failing to plan went **1 -> 0** across all 105 staged levels.

  **The pre-fix headline, retired**: 121 of 405 runs (30%), serilog 60/60, two repos at 0% completion. Those describe 2026-08-06, and on 2026-08-10 they steered a "what next" decision straight into work that was already done. That is the reason this entry spells out that they are historical.

  **Level 17 was never a problem, and the general lesson is the durable part.** Measured 2026-08-07: the alarming ">30-minute unbounded grind" reading was a **god-mode artifact**. God mode removes the death that terminates an attempt, so the grind was really "this bot never dies". With a real loadout level 17 is reached by 7 of 18 runs, all 7 die there, median 89s — 11% of a run that gets that far, well behind levels 6, 10 and 11. **A god-mode diagnostic measures the bot's navigation, never its cost, because the thing that bounds a real attempt is death.**

  **The original 2026-08-02 oscillation investigation, kept for its localisation even though its headline number did not survive.** It was **not the corridor vocabulary**: feature-sited oscillations went *down* (5 -> 4), and 13 of 22 were inside rooms the layout rework never touched — same count, same sizes, total room floor identical (4653 -> 4658 tiles). What did change is that rooms went from 65% to 81% of walkable space because corridor floor halved. Two sites were pinned: an open stretch of an 18x18 labyrinth room with no enemies or props anywhere near (`stage02_bootstrap.sh` ~(37,31)), and a one-tile corridor with an armed spike trap in the adjacent tile (`stage07_service.rb` ~(21,33)). **Its "15 of 22 are circling, not ping-pong" claim was later inverted** by a 905-finding sample (80% ping-pong) — see the oscillation entry below — and its reference to an `allWaitingOnSpike` exclusion is stale: that `.every()` call was replaced by the fraction-based `SPIKE_WAIT_DOMINANCE` in `a6abdf1` on 2026-07-31, *before* the investigation ran.

  **Still open, and deliberately not closed with this item:** curl's **58/58 level-1 wedge** is a different bug with its own entry — the browser and the route planner disagree about which file is level 1, so the bot drove a route for a map it was not standing on. And ripgrep's Casual arm clearing **3%** is a difficulty question, not a wedge; the acceptance criterion "curl runs without excluding `.sh`" was also never re-checked.

- [x] **Oscillation: `ARRIVE_EPS` swept both ways and refuted as the lever, and the number that motivated the whole item turns out to be inside the noise (2026-08-14).** 5 arms, 404 attempts, staged curl, Casual+Gamer x hard. Archives `balancing_capture_osc_{baseA,baseB,tight05,tight08,wide}`. All five passed `verify:event-log`.

  **The null control is the headline.** `baseA` and `baseB` are identical code:

  | arm | ARRIVE_EPS | n | osc tk/1k | vs base | x floor | levels/attempt |
  |---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
  | baseA | 0.15 | 80 | 4.69 | -1.26 | 0.5 | 8.68 |
  | baseB | 0.15 | 80 | 7.21 | +1.26 | 0.5 | 8.72 |
  | tight08 | 0.08 | 84 | 4.44 | -1.50 | 0.6 | 8.45 |
  | wide | 0.30 | 80 | 19.96 | **+14.02** | 5.6 | 8.61 |
  | tight05 | 0.05 | 80 | 56.59 | **+50.64** | **20.1** | **7.40** |

  Noise floor from the identical pair: **2.52 tk/1k** on oscillation, **0.05** on levels-per-attempt.

  **So the item's motivating measurement does not survive.** It reported the layout rework pushing oscillation `4.4 -> 6.6 ticks/1k` — a 2.2-point move from an n=1 reading. Two arms of the *same code* here differ by 2.52. That finding was never distinguishable from noise, and the item that grew out of it was chasing a number, not an effect.

  **`ARRIVE_EPS` is already in the trough of a steep U**, which is a genuine negative result rather than a null: 0.08 is inside noise, 0.30 is 3.4x worse, and 0.05 is 10x worse *and* breaches the guard (levels-per-attempt 8.70 -> 7.40, 26x the guard's own noise). The hypothesis in the backlog — "bot navigation tuning (turn rate vs walk speed vs arrival epsilon)" — is refuted for the arrival-epsilon third. **Do not re-tune it.** Moving one knob in both directions without improving the total is the wrong-lever-class signature this project has been burned by before.

  **A mechanism for the 0.05 collapse, offered as hypothesis not measurement**: one movement step is `ENGINE_MOVE_SPEED * stepMs = 0.16` tiles (the figure `combatPolicy.mjs`'s own burst-cap comment already cites), so a 0.05-tile arrival radius is smaller than the bot's step granularity and arrival becomes luck — it orbits the waypoint until the tick budget forces a replan. That would also explain why `TIGHT_ARRIVE_EPS = 0.05` is safe where it is actually used (doors, exit: single targets with their own generous budgets) and catastrophic as a global default.

  **What the next attempt should start from, measured on 905 findings rather than 22.** The item says "15 of 22 are circling, not ping-pong". Pooled across every curl/serilog/ripgrep capture on disk it is the **inverse**: **80% ping-pong** (`signFlips` >= 70%), **18% circling**. The bot sits a **median 0.14 tiles** from its nav target — **55% of findings are inside `ARRIVE_EPS`** — while carrying a median heading error of **1.37 rad**. So it is steering hard at a point it is standing on, and the instability lives *below* the arrival threshold where widening or tightening that threshold cannot reach it. Oscillation is also strongly repo-dependent (serilog **2.90**, curl **5.45**, ripgrep **10.97** tk/1k), so "the oscillation rate" is not one number.

- [x] **Attrition sized: regular + Edge Case do 88-100% of enemy damage, and Elites are individually lethal but numerically a rounding error (2026-08-13).** The backlog item had deliberately refused to size itself until the bot fixes landed, because the pre-fix numbers were contaminated by a bot walking 14,000 tiles for loot and knife-trading Elites. Those fixes landed 2026-08-06; this reads **~1,000 post-fix runs** already on disk across staged curl, serilog and ripgrep. No new machine time.

  | | curl | serilog | ripgrep |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|
  | normal | **72.7%** | 70.2% | 56.9% |
  | edgeCase | 15.7% | 29.8% | 43.1% |
  | elite | **11.6%** | none staged | none staged |
  | **regular + edgeCase** | **88.4%** | **100%** | **100%** |

  **The hypothesis survives its own re-measure, which is the part worth noting.** curl's Elite share is **11.6%** post-fix against **10.0%** in the pre-fix sweep — so the contamination this item spent a week waiting out barely moved this metric. The claim was right for reasons that did not depend on the bot's defects.

  **Per-enemy is where the mechanism shows.** curl damage-per-spawn: Elite **17.4**, normal **3.6**, Edge Case **0.7** — an Elite is ~5x deadlier than a regular and ~25x an Edge Case. Against 1,536 Elites, **46,309** normals and **49,151** Edge Cases. So "the full stop, not the attrition" is now measured rather than inferred: Elites are dangerous per encounter and irrelevant per campaign.

  **One of the two metrics the item asked for is uninformative, recorded so nobody re-runs it.** "The share of deaths where no Elite was present at all" comes out at 0.5% on curl — but only because nearly every staged curl level *has* an Elite, so the number measures staging rather than lethality. Damage share discriminates; roster presence does not. serilog and ripgrep answer it trivially at 100% and 98.4%, having no Elites at all.

  **Unasked-for and the most actionable thing here: environmental damage varies enormously by repo.** 5.0% on curl, 7.6% on serilog, **29.7% on ripgrep** — mines 16.3% plus acid 12.1%. A third of all damage taken on the repo whose Casual arm clears 3% comes from level furniture rather than from enemies. Any look at ripgrep difficulty should start there and not at its roster.

  **Schema traps, verified before use rather than assumed** — both would produce quietly wrong shares. `by[]` is populated *only* for enemy sources (its empty count matches `trapSpike + hazard + trapMine + selfRocket` exactly), so enemy-vs-environment has to come from `src`; and `by[].amt` is a consistent **2/3** of `amt` in this hard-difficulty data, i.e. a pre-scaling quantity, so the two must never be mixed in one ratio. curl's figures pool the walking-distance and angular-gate A/B arms, which is defensible only because both were measured neutral.

- [x] **The bot wedge re-measured: curl 0 in 787, serilog 96.7% full-campaign completion against a pre-fix 60/60 wedged (2026-08-13).** The backlog had carried this as "a campaign, not an afternoon" since 2026-08-06, and the `121 of 405 runs (30%)` headline with it. It does not survive. ripgrep is still running at time of writing and is appended below when it lands.

  **curl cost nothing — the answer was already on disk.** Eight captures from the flag A/Bs had banked **797 attempts** on staged curl, so the rate came out of existing data rather than a new run. Per-run outcomes reconstructed from the event logs: **787 runs died, 0 ended any other way, 0 wedges.** The 10 missing attempts reconcile exactly against 10 crashes (below), so nothing is unaccounted for.

  **serilog: 116 of 120 completed all 15 levels (96.7%)**, 3 profiles x hard, zero crashed attempts, 130 minutes on four lanes. L1-L13 cleared by all 120 attempts; L14 117/120; L15 116/117. Per profile Casual 38/40, Gamer **40/40**, Pro 38/40. Only 4 runs failed to finish for *any* reason, so the wedge rate is **<=3.3% by construction** whatever those four turn out to be. Pre-fix it was **60/60 wedged, 0% completion**, and the backlog's acceptance bar — "comparable to sinatra's 88%" — is met.

  **Both results have a named mechanism, which is what makes this more than a correlation.** serilog is C#, and its wedge was the C# parser bug: an unmodified interface method defaulted to `private`, turning 136 methods into 140 lockable rooms — 640 doors, 321 keys — so `planRoute` gave up at `MAX_ITERATIONS`. curl's was the separate level-1 planner/browser disagreement. Two distinct fixed causes, two clean measurements. **ripgrep exercises a third** — the locked-door/gate blockage that put it at 83% wedged on L8, capped since by `MAX_GATES = 6` — so it is the one remaining cell that could still surprise, not redundancy.

  **Three caveats, none of which rescue the old number.** The staging is **re-derived, not restored**: re-staging curl today picks different files at four slots than the 2026-08-09 archive did, and serilog's is almost certainly not whatever produced `60/60` — so this is "the wedge rate now", not a strict before/after on a fixed population. It is the **capture harness**, which `diagnose-level-wedge.mjs:7` says does not reproduce the wedge — but that claim is itself pre-fix, and this harness has demonstrably reported wedges before (ripgrep L8 at 83%), so it is not a blind instrument. And curl's own historic 58/58 level-1 wedge was always filed as a *different* bug, so curl was never the strongest test case for this question.

  **Two measurement traps found while doing it, both of which produce a confident wrong zero.**
  - **`failureReasons` is not the wedge counter.** It records only *crashed* attempts. The `died`/`stuck` outcome never reaches the cell JSON at all, so anyone computing a wedge rate from `failureReasons` gets zero for free. Outcomes have to be reconstructed per `rid` from the event log — a run with no `playerDeath` that never reached the final level.
  - **`stall` anomalies are anti-correlated with wedging.** serilog throws **19.9 stalls per run** and completes 96.7%; curl throws **9.5** and completes **0%**. Whatever `stall` detects is transient and does not block progress; used as a wedge proxy it inverts the ranking.

  **Incidental: 10 attempts crashed on `#launch-demo-campaign` "resolved to ... element is not visible"**, all inside a single invocation (`gatecasual_offB/Casual-hard-003`), self-healed by the orchestrator's retry. Not the first-run tour blocking the button — that would hit attempt 1 of every invocation, and it hit exactly one. Filed as a harness-flake signature; the "resolved but not visible" wording is the useful part if it recurs.

  **ripgrep, the third mechanism: 0 wedges in 105 runs, and its wall turns out to be a skill gradient rather than a blockage.** This was the cell most likely to surprise — its historic failure was the locked-door/gate blockage that stopped **16 of 18 runs at L8, 83% wedged, 0 completed**, capped since by `MAX_GATES = 6`, and neither curl nor serilog exercises that path.

  | profile | runs | died | cleared | **stuck** | clear % |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
  | Casual | 40 | 39 | 1 | **0** | 3% |
  | Gamer | 40 | 20 | 20 | **0** | 50% |
  | Pro | 25 | 2 | 23 | **0** | 92% |
  | **total** | **105** | 61 | 44 | **0** | 42% |

  **3% -> 50% -> 92% across the skill ladder is the finding**, and it is the opposite of a blockage: a wedge does not care which profile is driving, and this cares enormously. An early Casual-heavy sample read as "L7 is a 9% wall for everyone", which was a sampling artefact of the order the lanes happened to run — worth remembering that the per-profile split is where a wedge and a difficulty spike become distinguishable.

  **Stopped deliberately at 105 of a planned 120**, because the marginal 15 attempts were worth ~0.3pp: `0/105` bounds the true wedge rate under **2.8%** at 95%, `0/120` under 2.5%, and Pro was running at 0.16 attempts/min because it *clears* the campaign and a full 15-level clear is slow. Recorded as 105 rather than presented as the plan.

  **One analysis trap the stop itself created.** Killing the orchestrator mid-flight orphans in-progress lane runs, and an in-progress run looks exactly like a wedge — no `playerDeath`, never reached the last level. The counts above are therefore restricted to **banked invocations only** (an events directory with a matching cell JSON), which excluded exactly the two invocations that were in flight (`Pro-hard-003`, `Pro-hard-006`) and reconciles to the same 105 the cell files report. Anyone re-running this must apply the same filter or they will invent wedges that are really just an interrupted capture.

  **serilog is also a warning about substrate choice**: at 96.7% completion and 14.94 of 15 levels per attempt it is saturated at the *ceiling*, exactly as the demo campaign is, and would have been useless as an A/B substrate. curl sits mid-band. Two real repos, opposite failure modes — so the recipe recorded in the entry below needs its caveat honoured: **pilot every candidate**, never assume "real repo" implies "discriminating".

- [x] **Two unproven bot flags measured against a real repo, and the substrate question answered on the way (2026-08-13).** `BOT_WALKING_DISTANCE_THREATS` and `BOT_ANGULAR_FIRE_GATE` both shipped ON and explicitly unproven, because the demo campaign clears 100% with zero deaths and cannot discriminate anything. This is the staged-real-repo capture the backlog had been asking for. **400 attempts total, ~2.5h, four lanes.** Archives `balancing_capture_ab_walkthreat_{off,on}` and `balancing_capture_ab_anglegate_{off,on}`.

  **The substrate had to be found by pilot, because every per-repo number on record was stale.** Stage C's 0%/94%/96% column predates the Elite cap by one day; the 2026-08-09 mid-band figures (curl 41%, wolf3d L9 30%) predate the angular gate, the level-1 planner desync fix, the teleporter-pad give-up and the pellet-cone model — the same window over which demo-campaign Pro/hard went from 1-of-42 clearing L15 to 20 of 20. A 20-attempt pilot on staged curl found the live shape: **L1-L8 at 100%, L9 at 45%, L10 at 33%, nothing past L11.** So curl discriminates, but **campaign completion is 0% and was excluded as a metric in advance** rather than reported later as a null. wolf3d was piloted too and dropped: it runs at ~0.29 attempts/min against curl's 1.96, roughly 7x the cost for the same question. Note the staging is **not** reproducible from the 2026-08-09 archives — re-staging curl today picks different files at slots 7/10/12/14, so none of these numbers compare to the old 24/58.

  **`BOT_WALKING_DISTANCE_THREATS`: a null, from a mechanism that fired constantly.** Casual/Gamer/Pro x hard, 40 per combo per arm.

  | cell | OFF | ON | delta | Fisher p |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | L9 pooled | 69/119 (58%) | 69/118 (58%) | +0.5pp | 1.000 |
  | L10 pooled | 33/69 (48%) | 38/69 (55%) | +7.2pp | 0.496 |

  Levels-cleared-per-attempt moved -0.11 / +0.07 / +0.08 on Casual/Gamer/Pro — mixed signs. **The largest single movement is Pro's L10 at +20.4pp (p=0.231, n=23 vs 22), and it is worth naming because it becomes the noise-floor estimate for everything below**: this harness produced a 20pp swing at n~20 from a lever otherwise measured flat, and Casual's L10 moved -4.2pp on the same level in the same run. This is a null *from a mechanism that fired*, which is what distinguishes it from Stage 6: the static sweep re-run against the staged curl levels puts the answer-change rate at **16.1% of positions with an in-radius candidate** and **33.2% on level 9**, the level the difficulty lives on. The lever was pulled thousands of times per run and nothing moved.

  **`BOT_ANGULAR_FIRE_GATE`: availability finally confirmed, effect still not established.** The standing note said settling this needs "a test case that actually contains long shots", and curl is that case — measured from the walking-distance capture, **45.4% of Casual's 18,003 shots exceed the 3.6t binding radius and 23.5% of Gamer's 17,113 exceed 5.8t**. **Pro was excluded by construction**: only **19 of its 17,528 shots (0.1%)** exceed 9.6t, so a Pro arm buys 80 attempts of guaranteed noise. Binding radii were verified directly off `angularHalfWidth` rather than taken from the note.

  | cell | OFF | ON | delta | Fisher p |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | Casual L9 | 12/45 (27%) | 20/40 (50%) | +23.3pp | **0.043** |
  | Casual L10 | 5/12 (42%) | 13/20 (65%) | +23.3pp | 0.277 |
  | Gamer L9 | 21/40 (53%) | 21/40 (53%) | 0.0pp | 1.000 |
  | Gamer L10 | 8/21 (38%) | 7/21 (33%) | -4.8pp | 1.000 |

  **The nominally-significant Casual L9 cell is NOT being claimed as a win, and the reason is a control the capture supplies for free.** Below its binding radius the gate is *identical code* in both arms, so that zone is a built-in null control. Hit rate moved **+2.4pp below the radius (z=5.57)** and **+2.5pp above it (z=3.28)** — the difference-in-differences is **+0.1pp**. If the gate worked by refusing shots geometry cannot land, the binding zone had to improve *more* than the zone where the arms run the same code, and it did not; the hit-rate gain is a global shift between arms, not something the gate did. Gamer, at 23.5% binding, moved exactly **0.0pp** at L9. And p=0.043 is one cell out of twelve tested across the two A/Bs, against a harness whose own noise floor in this very session was a 20.4pp swing at comparable n. Suggestive, unconfirmed.

  **FOLLOW-UP, same day: the Casual L9 cell was re-run properly and it does not survive. `BOT_ANGULAR_FIRE_GATE` has no measurable effect.** Three arms of 120 on the identical staging (verified byte-identical to the run above, so the cells sit side by side): **`offA` and `offB` are the same code on both sides and exist to measure this substrate's noise floor directly** instead of borrowing one from the walking-distance A/B.

  | comparison | A | B | delta | Fisher p |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | **null control** L9 `offA` vs `offB` | 50/120 (41.7%) | 52/119 (43.7%) | +2.0pp | 0.794 |
  | **null control** L10 `offA` vs `offB` | 26/50 (52.0%) | 23/52 (44.2%) | -7.8pp | 0.552 |
  | treatment L9, OFF pooled vs ON | 102/239 (42.7%) | 59/122 (48.4%) | +5.7pp | 0.316 |
  | treatment L10, OFF pooled vs ON | 49/102 (48.0%) | 29/59 (49.2%) | +1.1pp | 1.000 |

  Levels-cleared-per-attempt: `offA` **8.63**, `offB` **7.95** — a **0.68 spread from identical code** — against the treatment's pooled 8.28 -> 8.72 (+0.44). The treatment moves *less* than the control does.

  **What actually happened to the +23.3pp.** The earlier ON arm (20/40, 50%) is reproduced almost exactly here (59/122, 48.4%). The OFF arm is not: 12/45 (26.7%) then, **102/239 (42.7%)** now. Those are the same configuration, and they differ by **+16.0pp at p=0.048** — so the "significant" result was one unlucky OFF draw, and the run-to-run variance of this harness is itself significant at the same threshold. **Treat any single-cell p just under 0.05 here as unsupported unless a null control arm ran alongside it**; that is the transferable lesson, and it is cheap insurance at roughly a third of a capture's cost.
