  Mechanism level agrees, with the control finally in place: hit-rate difference-in-differences (binding zone minus the identical-code zone below 3.6t) is **+0.82pp for the treatment against +0.45pp for the null control** — same order, over 44k-49k shots per arm. The gate binds on 45.4% of Casual's shots and still moves nothing.

  **Both flags stay ON, and what changes is the label, not the default.** Straight-line is the dimensionally wrong measure for a travel decision and a fixed angular tolerance is dimensionally wrong for a shot; neither shows harm at this n. They are no longer "unproven" — they are *measured neutral on curl/hard*, which is a different and weaker claim than "they work". The angular gate's settling run is the follow-up above: it was done, and the answer is no effect. **Both flags were then kept, deliberately, by the user on 2026-08-13** — the rationale is recorded under *Two Measured No-Ops, Kept Deliberately* in `decisions.md`, and the short version is that a null says the wrong quantity did not happen to cost anything here, not that it was the right quantity. Do not re-open this as "two dead flags nobody removed". **Neither result bears on the deathmatch-opponent case** for either flag — "an opponent that tracks you through a wall reads as cheating" is a claim about a human observer and no bot harness measures it.

- [x] **`pickThreat` measures an occluded enemy by walking distance — the walking-distance audit's second and last site, closed (2026-08-12).** `dist` was `Math.hypot`, and `pickThreat` both *gated* on it (`.filter(e => e.dist < profile.engageRadius)`) and ranked on it, so an enemy 7 tiles away through a wall and 25 by corridor read as "close" and could be picked over one genuinely to hand. Same defect and same measure as `driveToExit`'s blocker choice, fixed by `rankExitBlockers` on 2026-07-31; that entry is below and this closes the pair.

  **Only occluded candidates are re-measured, and that is the design rather than a shortcut.** A visible enemy is engaged by shooting it, which costs no travel, so for those straight-line distance is not an approximation of the right answer — it *is* the right answer. This is the one substantive difference from `rankExitBlockers`, whose blockers must all be walked to.

  **The `sqrt(2)` budget slack is what keeps it a narrow fix.** A 4-directional step count overestimates real travel by up to `sqrt(2)` — `|dx| + |dy|` against `hypot(dx, dy)`, peaking at 45 degrees — so gating raw step counts on `engageRadius` would also have rejected occluded enemies standing in *open floor* on a diagonal, which has nothing to do with the defect. Budgeting `engageRadius * sqrt(2)` cancels that worst case exactly: open floor can never lose a candidate the old gate kept, only genuine detours around geometry can. Derived, not tuned. A unit test pins a case that is inside `engageRadius` at 8.49 tiles and 12 steps away, i.e. one a raw-step gate would have dropped.

  **Distance comes from one bounded flood fill, not a path per enemy.** `pickThreat` runs up to three times per decision at 20 decisions/sec, so a BFS per candidate per call was not affordable; one fill bounded at the budget answers every candidate at once (~340 tiles at today's radius) and is skipped entirely when every candidate is visible, which is the common case. It is deliberately *more permissive* than `bfsPath`: only structural walls block, so closed doors, hazards and spikes are all passable. That asymmetry is the point — `rankExitBlockers` plans a route the bot must really walk and has to be honest about its cost, whereas this only decides whether a candidate is worth considering, and every generosity keeps a candidate the old gate would also have kept. It also keeps `pickThreat` free of an opened-doors argument it has no way to obtain, and the module free of an import (it must stay liftable into `src/engine/`).

  **Availability measured before efficacy, because the last two mechanisms shipped here fired 0.99% and 0.00% of the time.** A static sweep over all 17 generated demo-campaign levels — every floor tile reachable from spawn as a hypothetical player position, every enemy assumed aggroed — compares the two answers directly: of **9,422 positions with an in-radius candidate, 22.0% change**, split **7.9% where the threat is dropped entirely** and **14.2% where a different enemy is chosen**. Per level the change rate runs 2.0% (`stage16_hardware.h`) to 50.5% (`stage06_pipeline.py`). Raw cases on stage06 are the intended geometry exactly: an enemy 9.0-9.5 tiles away straight-line, occluded, and 17-24 tiles by corridor. This independently corroborates Stage 6's own finding that a walking-distance gate rejected 27% of its candidates and 43 of 45 on `stage07_service.rb` — a different denominator, but `stage07` is among the highest here too, at 29.3% dropped.

  **What that number is not.** It is an upper bound on a map-geometry question, not a rate: assuming every enemy is aggroed inflates the candidate pool well past reality, every tile is weighted equally regardless of whether the bot ever stands there, and the 742 "dropped" positions include many adjacent tiles looking at the *same* enemy. It establishes that the geometry is abundant and the mechanism will fire — not what it is worth.

  **Shipped ON and unproven, deliberately, and it should keep that label until an A/B says otherwise.** `BOT_WALKING_DISTANCE_THREATS` defaults true for the same reason `BOT_ANGULAR_FIRE_GATE` does: straight-line is the dimensionally wrong measure for a decision about travel. No behavioural A/B has been run, and the demo campaign cannot supply one — it clears 100% with zero deaths, so its survival metrics discriminate nothing. This wants the staged-real-repo substrate the backlog already calls for.

- [x] **`hitFraction` now models the multi-pellet cone the way the engine resolves it (2026-08-12).** `expectedDamagePerShot` claimed a flat 7 of 7 shotgun pellets out to 4 tiles; the engine delivers 7 at 1 tile and 2.96 at 4, so the shotgun was over-valued **2.3x** at 4 tiles against pistol/gdb everywhere in `pickRangedWeapon`.

  **Derived from the engine, not fitted to it.** A throwaway probe fired the real shotgun at a centred enemy across a distance sweep and counted pellets from damage dealt. `pelletHitFraction` then integrates each pellet's own hit probability over the engine's uniform deviation — pellet `i` lands with probability `|[-hw-o_i, hw-o_i] ∩ [-D, D]| / 2D`, no free parameters. Derived vs measured, in pellets of 7: 1t 7.00/7.00, 2t 5.61/5.63, 3t 4.00/3.92, 4t 3.00/2.96, 6t 2.00/2.04, 8t 1.54/1.59.

  Two errors underneath it: `pelletOffsets` spreads pellets across **±spreadPx** while the model used `spreadPx / 2`; and the engine scales cone deviation by `zBuffer[column]` — the depth *behind* the target, which saturates in an open room — where the model used the target's own distance.

  **The control that isolated the second error, kept because it is the evidence and not just the conclusion.** The same sweep was run twice: once in an open room (the wall ~34 tiles behind the target, so `rangeFraction` saturates and every shot takes the full ±38px) and once with the wall immediately behind it (deviation ≈ 0). If the deviation were driven by the *target's* distance, as the old model assumed, the two would be identical. They are not:

  | d (tiles) | 0.6 | 1 | 1.5 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | far wall (open room) | 7.00 | 7.00 | 6.63 | 5.63 | 3.92 | 2.96 | 2.38 | 2.04 | 1.84 | 1.59 |
  | near wall | 7.00 | 7.00 | **7.00** | **7.00** | 4.45 | **3.00** | **3.00** | 2.25 | 1.77 | 1.68 |
  | old model | 7 | 7 | 7 | 7 | 7 | 7 | 5.6 | 4.7 | 4.0 | 3.5 |

  Two tiles goes 5.63 → 7.00 pellets purely by moving the wall behind the target, and five tiles 2.38 → 3.00. The near-wall row also matches a plain count of pellet offsets inside the sprite, which is what the geometry predicts once the deviation is removed — so both terms are confirmed independently. The open-room row is the honest modelling target, because the bot cannot cheaply raycast the wall behind each candidate target. Before trusting any of the sweep, the harness was checked for a distortion that would have invalidated it: `resolveShot` projects with the `SCENE_WIDTH`/`SCENE_HEIGHT` constants, not the 200x150 test canvas.

  **The A/B: behaviour moved as intended, performance did not move at all.** Weapon selection over 50k+ ranged shots per arm went shotgun 6.3% -> 3.6%, pistol 22.6% -> 28.2%, ghidra unchanged. But across **four combos at n=60 (240 attempts per arm)** every metric's sign is mixed — `enemyAccuracy` -9.4/+8.4/+2.7/+4.6%, `dmg.enemyRangedPerSec` -11.1/+6.9/-1.7/+4.3% — with only `levelTimeSec` consistently signed and then by 0.0-0.7%, far below readable. Guards passed on all four, and on all four of an earlier n=20 pass and a full-campaign pass.

  **Shipped on correctness, and recorded as a null rather than dressed as a win** — same call as `BOT_ANGULAR_FIRE_GATE`. The model's job is to describe the engine and it now does; the performance null is not evidence either way, because the test case cannot produce evidence (see the campaign-exhaustion entry).

  **The single-pellet path was deliberately left alone.** The first cut made it a flat 1, which removed the pistol's long-range degradation and destroyed the only thing modelling gdb's tighter cone (`maxConeDeviationPx` 20 vs 38) — two existing tests caught it. Pistol and gdb measure at exactly 1.00 damage-per-hit versus prediction, so that path had no business moving inside a shotgun fix; the mixed semantics between the two paths are a pre-existing wart, called out in code rather than silently rebalanced. Three other tests were updated and none was a relaxation: the Friday Hotfix "plateau" was the old clamp masking real decay, its 0.5 falloff ratio measured the pure range ramp because the cone contributed nothing, and the 7-tile elite scenario equipped ghidra — which *wins* once the shotgun stops being over-valued, so "keep what you have" was the correct answer the assertion misread.

  The superseded investigation follows, kept for its measurement of the defect:

    - **The decomposition that settled it.** A `shot` event and every `damageDealt` it caused share a timestamp, so a shot's total damage is recoverable and the shots can be split into those that dealt nothing and those that connected. "Model too generous" and "the bot misses" then separate cleanly: `dmg/hit ÷ predicted` tests the model's own claim on shots that *did* land, while `dmg/shot ÷ predicted` additionally carries the misses.
    - **Single-pellet weapons: the model is exact, and the shortfall is aim.** Pistol lands **22.0** damage per connecting shot against 22.0 predicted (ratio **1.00**); gdb **12.0** against 12.0 (**1.00**). Their whole gap to prediction is the **~30% of shots that miss entirely** (pistol 29-35%, gdb 29-31% across 0-8 tiles). That is a bot-accuracy number, not a model number — and it is worth its own item: the bot misses roughly one ranged shot in three.
    - **Multi-pellet weapons: the model is wrong, on shots that hit.** Shotgun at 0-2 tiles connects on **95%** of shots and still delivers only **66.8** damage against **175** predicted — ratio **0.38**, i.e. **2.67 of 7 pellets** land where the model claims all 7. It is not a miss problem; the shot connects and simply does less than claimed. The gap narrows with range only because the model's own prediction falls faster than reality: 0.42 at 2-4t, 0.47 at 4-6t, 0.67 at 6-8t. Friday Hotfix shows the same shape at **0.61-0.64** (3.7 of 6 pellets).
    - **So the defect is `hitFraction`**, the multi-pellet term: `halfWidthPx / scatterPx` clamped to 1, which at close range credits *every* pellet because the sprite is wider than the scatter. The engine's real per-pellet raycast evidently does not agree. The single-pellet path never exercises that term, which is exactly why it is perfect.
    - **Consequence, and why it matters beyond one branch**: the shotgun is systematically over-valued against pistol/gdb everywhere in `pickRangedWeapon`, not just in the cluster branch — which is precisely the effect the old ghidra item mistook for a short-circuit. Correcting `hitFraction` would change weapon selection across the board, so it needs the 3-combo A/B protocol at n=12, and it is a *bot-behaviour* change: do not land it inside another measurement.
    - **The 2.6x is a lower bound.** One blast's damage to a *second* enemy counts toward that shot, which inflates the measured side; the true per-target figure is worse.
    - **The sequencing worry is resolved: this calibration target is still good (2026-08-11).** Fixing the bot's aim first would have moved the number this item is fitted to, since the ratio is measured on shots that *connected*. But the aim lead shipped **off** (measured as a regression — see the miss-rate item), and the one aim change that did ship, `BOT_ANGULAR_FIRE_GATE`, measured as a null. So the bot that produced the 1.17M-shot corpus and the bot at HEAD aim the same, and `hitFraction` can be recalibrated against the existing measurement without a re-capture.
    - **ghidra cannot be measured this way and its rows are not evidence.** A rocket has flight time, so its damage lands at a *later* timestamp than the shot and the join cannot see it — hence the nonsensical 100% miss rate on its rows. Any ghidra calibration needs a different join (nearest-later damage within a flight-time window), which is unbuilt.
    - Small-n rows (shotgun 8+, pistol/gdb 12+, Friday past its 6.5-tile max range) show ratios above 1 purely because the model's fog falloff collapses its prediction; ignore them.

- [x] **First-run intro tour, and a version stamp on everything persisted (2026-08-12).** The backlog asked for both together: *"UI intro, with popouts from elements… only to be run if no localStorage is present. while we are onto it also add a version tag to the localStorage."* Shipped as two PRs, because the stamp is useful on its own and the tour is a UI feature with design choices attached.

  **The stamp** (`src/storageSchema.ts`). Eight keys were persisted across `main.ts` and `highscores.ts` with no version, so a shape change to any of them would have been indistinguishable from current data. Nothing migrates yet and there is no empty `switch` pretending otherwise — version 1 *is* the first stamped shape. Three decisions each had a plausible wrong answer: first run is "no owned key at all" rather than "no version stamp" (an existing player has seven keys and no stamp, and reading that as a first run re-shows the intro to someone mid-campaign); storage stamped *newer* is never migrated downward (two tabs on one origin with different deploys is ordinary); and a corrupt stamp reads as `future` rather than 0, so migrations are skipped rather than re-run over data that may already be current. Every storage access is guarded individually — the first cut guarded only acquisition, and Safari private mode throws from `getItem`, which its own test caught.

  **The tour** (`src/ui/introTour.ts`). Nine popouts anchored to real sidebar controls, walking it top to bottom, worded from `doc/user/getting-started.md`, `multiplayer.md` and `hud-and-ui.md` so the tour and the written guide cannot drift. Scope is the cold-start screen, and that is a constraint rather than a preference: at a genuine first run the file tree is empty and no level is running, so the HUD/minimap material has nothing to anchor to. It never switches a launch tab to reveal the panel behind it — that is switch-and-revert on persistent UI state — so steps anchor to the tabs themselves.

  **The tour broke every Playwright-driven harness, and CI is what found it.** The verify suite, the balancing bot and the default-highscore generator all load the page with an empty `localStorage`, which is a first run by definition, so the tour opened and its backdrop swallowed the clicks they were making: five red browser legs, all `page.click('#tab-demo')` timing out. Gated on `navigator.webdriver` — the mechanism this project already used to silence audio for robots — with `isAutomated()` extracted from `engine/audio.ts` into `src/automation.ts` so there is one copy. Chosen over a URL flag precisely because it needs no cooperation: only some of those harnesses pass `?testHooks=1`. The manual button stays ungated.

  **Three defects that only driving a real browser could find**, none of them visible to unit tests that stub every rect: visibility was tested with the `hidden` attribute while this app hides controls with `style.display = "none"`, so the multiplayer popout parked itself on an invisible tab; targets below the fold were never scrolled into view, rendering highlights at y=1240 and y=1113 in a 900px viewport while the popout was clamped into view, pointing at nothing; and the multiplayer copy said "only the host needs the workspace" when joining needs no workspace at all — only the Host sub-tab is workspace-gated.

  **It also leaked into the test suite, which is how the pattern first showed.** `main.test.ts` clears `localStorage` in `beforeEach`, so once the tour existed all ~200 of its tests imported `main` as a first run and scheduled a focus-stealing overlay that could fire during the *next* test — two tour tests passed alone and failed together. The shared setup now stamps the schema so an ordinary test is a returning player.

  Verified: 2,926 src tests, 520 scripts tests, coverage gate 99.51% branches, `tsc --noEmit`, plus a real browser at 1400x900 confirming all nine highlights land on their control and inside the viewport, that finishing means a reload does not re-show it, and that the button replays it.

- [x] **In-room teleporters are an exception now, not the rule (2026-08-12).** The backlog complaint: a `goto` used for "harmless" error handling — C's `goto out;` — put both pads of a teleporter pair in the same room, warping the player a few tiles across the room they were already standing in. Disorienting rather than a shortcut, and asked to be kept as an occasional joke about `goto` being evil rather than removed.

  **It was not occasional, and the demo campaign badly understated it.** The campaign has exactly two teleporter pairs and both are in-room, which reads as a small curiosity. Measuring the corpus instead: in-room pairs are **68-100% of every pair generated** — curl 17/17, doom 20/20, vim 445/544, git 251/369, quake 37/53 — and a single vim level produced **39** of them.

  `placeTeleporters` now allows `MAX_IN_ROOM_TELEPORTER_PAIRS = 1` per level and skips the rest. Cross-room pairs, the ones that genuinely shortcut a level, are never capped. After: worst level goes **39 → 1** on vim, **19 → 1** on git, **14 → 1** on quake, **10 → 1** on doom, **4 → 1** on curl.

  **Cross-room pairs actually go slightly up** — git 118 → 121, vim 99 → 114 — and the reason is worth knowing rather than rounding to "unaffected": a skipped link never calls `findPropSpot`, so it stops reserving floor spots, and cross-room links that previously failed to place a second pad now find one. More of the interesting kind, as a side effect of removing the boring kind.

  **The cap is tested before `findPropSpot`, which is what makes this free.** A skipped link draws no rng at all, so levels that keep their pair generate exactly as they did before — and because both demo-campaign teleporter levels carry exactly one in-room pair, nothing there is skipped. `verify:replay` passes against the **existing** board with no regeneration, which is the proof that generation is byte-identical rather than an argument that it should be.

  One kept rather than zero is deliberate and load-bearing: `verify:campaign` requires at least two levels demonstrating the mechanic, and the campaign's only two teleporter levels are both in-room. A cap of zero would have deleted the mechanic from the showcase campaign entirely.

  Verified: 2,888 src tests (3 new/rewritten teleporter cases, including one asserting a link that fails to place does not spend the cap), `tsc --noEmit`, `verify:campaign` (both goto/teleporter checks, same two levels), `verify:replay` (full 17-level board, unchanged).
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-11: `media.peerconnection.ice.loopback` does not work either — the Firefox CI skip stays.** It was the one option the skip's ruled-out list never mentioned and the best fit on paper (loopback candidates are sufficient when both peers are two contexts in one Playwright process on one machine, and it sidesteps the default-route discovery heuristic instead of satisfying it). Tested where it had to be tested — on CI, with the pref set and the four Firefox multiplayer steps un-skipped: the host generated a session code, so signaling is fine, and the guest then died on "Data channels were not received within 15000ms". Reverted.
  - **The local test was worthless and that was established before trusting it.** Firefox passes `verify:multiplayer-connect` on this dev box *with and without* the pref, because UDP egress works here — the control was run first for exactly that reason. This box has never been able to reproduce the runner's no-routable-default-route shape, and the 2026-08-03 correction to that effect is now confirmed rather than assumed.
  - **What was NOT measured, and is the first thing to do if anyone reopens it**: whether the pref changed candidate gathering at all. `installIceDiagnostics()` is opt-in behind `CODEENSTEIN_MULTIPLAYER_DEBUG_ICE=1` and off in CI, so "still fails" is all this shows — not "still gathers zero". Turn it on before trying a sixth option. Full write-up in `doc/dev/testing.md`'s cross-browser section.

- [x] **A TODO's encounter is now a property of its own comment text, not of the PRNG stream (2026-08-11).** `placeTodoEncounter` drew `rng()` and split it into thirds, so which TODO became a trap, a mine or a "Bug" enemy rode the shared per-level stream — and was therefore re-rolled by any change that shifted it, however unrelated. `verify:campaign` asserts all three outcomes appear somewhere in the showcase campaign, and the coloured-keys change had already deleted the trap outcome outright, rescued only by authoring another TODO. The outcome is now `fnv1a(comment.text) % 3`.

  **The backlog's own proposed fix would have made it worse, and measuring first is what caught that.** The item said to cycle the outcomes instead of rolling them, "which guarantees all three whenever a level has three". Counting the campaign's actual TODO terminals — `1,0,0,1,0,1,0,0,1,0,1,0,0,1,0,0,2`, eight in total — **no level has three, and only one has two**. Cycling from a fixed start would have given a trap to all seven single-TODO levels and stage17 a trap and a mine, producing **no Bug enemy anywhere** and failing the very check it was meant to protect. The item's quoted ~8% fragility was also stale: it was computed against 9 encounters, and at the 8 the campaign now carries the chance of losing an outcome was ~12%.

  **Hashing the text removes the coupling rather than shrinking it.** The mix stops being a lottery ticket re-drawn on every unrelated edit and becomes a fixed property of the campaign's own source comments — and an author who wants a different outcome edits that comment's wording, which then stays edited. Predicted before implementing (trap 4 / mine 3 / Bug 1) and confirmed against the real generator afterwards: **trap 4, mine 2, Bug 1**, plus one TODO whose encounter is not placed at all (it was unplaced before this change too). All three coverage checks pass.

  `fnv1a` was extracted from `seed.ts`'s `seedFrom` rather than copied, for the reason `constantMirrors.test.mjs` exists: two copies of a hash are two things that can drift.

  **Two existing tests failed, and they were exactly the right two** — both pinned an outcome to an *empirically-found seed*, which is precisely the coupling being deleted. Rewritten to select by comment text, plus a new test asserting one comment yields the same outcome under four different seeds, which is the property the change exists for.

  Regeneration was mandatory and was proven so before being spent: `verify:replay` was run against the *old* board first and failed ("ran out of recorded input"), confirming it catches the shift rather than assuming it would. The regenerated board (Pro 61,730 / Gamer 59,276 / Casual 55,479, all 17 levels) then replays clean end to end. Verified: 2,887 src tests, 514 scripts tests, coverage gate 99.51% branches, `tsc --noEmit`, `verify:campaign`, `verify:replay`.

- [x] **The planner stopped guessing which levels the game plays (2026-08-11).** `planLevels` enumerated `demo-campaign/` with a sorted `readdirSync` and handed the bot a route for slot 1. The game does not play slot 1: it starts at `findEntrypoint`'s pick and then walks *forward* in tree order (`advanceToNextLevel` -> `findNextParsableFile`), so every file alphabetically before the entrypoint is never reached at all. On curl the planner's slot 1 was `01_projects_OS400_make-docs.sh` while the engine loaded `03_docs_examples_pop3-stat.c` — 205 differing tiles, a bot driving a route for a map it was not standing on, and 58 runs lost before `planEngineMatch` existed to notice.

  **The fix deletes the second definition rather than syncing it.** `campaignLevelOrder(tree)` in `main.ts` composes the real `findEntrypoint` and the real `flattenParsableFiles` into the sequence the game will play; `__codeensteinCampaignTestHooks.getLevelOrder()` exposes it; `readCampaignLevelOrder` asks for it before planning. There is now one answer to "which file is level 1" and the planner consumes it. It throws rather than falling back to filename order if the hook is unreadable — a fallback would silently reinstate exactly the bug, and a run planned against the wrong maps is void, not degraded.

  **Sized honestly first, and the honest size changed the approach.** The backlog called this "a cleanup rather than a hazard" and proposed extracting the browser's entrypoint rule into a shared module. Reading it showed a four-stage cascade in a 4,265-line `main.ts` leaning on 26 references to `workspaceIsRemote`/`workspaceIsDemo` and a memoised `flattenParsableFiles` — a day-plus refactor under the `src/` exactness bar. Asking the browser instead is a hook plus a round trip, and removes the duplication rather than sharing it.

  **Nothing needed regenerating, and that is verified rather than reasoned.** The demo campaign's `main.c` already sorted first, so its order is byte-identical either way — confirmed by probing the new hook against the real campaign (17 files, same sequence) — and `npm run verify:replay` replayed the baked board through all 17 levels to a matching final score in 233s. Staged real-world campaigns are where this changes behaviour, which is the case that lost the runs.

  **The coverage gate earned its keep twice.** The first cut put a `workspaceTree` null-check inside the hook closure, leaving an untested branch; moving it into `campaignLevelOrder` (which takes `TreeNode | null`) removed the branch and is better code besides. The gate then still failed by 0.01pp on the `entry ? … : 0` ternary — reachable only for a remote non-demo workspace, which no unit test constructs and the planner never serves, so it is annotated with that reasoning rather than left as a silent gap.

  Verified: 3 new `main.test.ts` cases (including the entrypoint-not-first case the demo campaign cannot exhibit), 2,886 src tests, 514 scripts tests, `tsc --noEmit`, coverage gate, an end-to-end telemetry smoke, and `verify:replay`.

  **Follow-up the next day: the "still open" third was already closed, and had been since 2026-08-07.** This entry (and the backlog item behind it) said `stage-campaign.mjs`'s solver "models only `findEntrypoint`'s `bestOverall` branch". It does not, and three checks settle it: `order()` picks `sorted.find(withMain) ?? sorted[0]` (`stage-campaign.mjs:262`), which *is* `bestWithMain ?? bestOverall`; `complexityByFile` scores with the browser's own `complexityScore`, and the zero-complexity filter uses that same number; and the `findEntrypointByName` stage cannot fire for a staged campaign at all, because staging renames every file `${slot}_${path}` and `findEntrypointByName` compares exact lowercase basenames — `03_src_main.c` never equals `main.c`. The stale claim sat in `notes` for four days and was copied forward into this entry without being checked. What genuinely remains is a *drift* risk: the solver re-derives the rule where the planner now asks for it, so the two can silently diverge if either side changes, and nothing would fail.

- [x] **The bot's stale aim was fixed, measured, and the fix reverted — the lag is real, the correction for it was worse than the lag (2026-08-11).** `report:aim-error` had split the ~30% ranged miss rate into a dimensionally-wrong fire gate (defect 1) and an aim one decision window stale (defect 2), and called defect 2 the dominant term. Both fixes were built; only defect 1's survived contact with an A/B.

  **Reading the engine sharpened the diagnosis before anything was written.** The item said a shot resolves "0-50ms" after the positions it was aimed at. It is not a range: `simulate()` runs `updateEnemyAi(dt)` (`engine.ts:~2804`) and only then `updateFiring(dt)` (`~2868`), while the bot samples enemy positions only once a pump has completed — so the lag is **exactly one engine frame**. That happens to be 50ms under `run-balancing-telemetry.mjs` (where `recordStepMs == stepMs`), which is why the report's "97% of a sprite half-width per window" arithmetic came out right, but it is ~16.7ms under `generate-default-highscore.mjs`. `Bot#aimLeadMs` derives it per harness rather than assuming.

  **Four arms, not two, and that is what made the result readable.** A 70-second smoke showed the arms diverging but with the candidate *worse* (75.9% vs 81.1% raw hit rate, n≈180, ~1.4σ), so the two switches were run separately — `base` / `lead` / `gate` / `both` x the four standard combos, `LEVEL_LIMIT=8 ATTEMPT_CAP=20 CONCURRENCY=6 QUALIFYING_TARGET=999`, 16 runs, 2h00m. Fused into one arm the regression below would have been unattributable.

  **The aim lead is a regression, replicated across both pairs that flip only it** (`base->lead` and `gate->both`), single-pellet weapons only since a pellet cone forgives exactly the error under test:

  | target | range | lead off | lead on | delta | z |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | edgeCase | 0-2 | 64.4% (459/713) | 55.5% (432/779) | **-8.9pp** | -3.51 |
  | edgeCase | 2-4 | 55.3% (1159/2097) | 50.9% (1062/2087) | **-4.4pp** | -2.84 |
  | edgeCase | 4-6 | 49.5% (770/1556) | 45.6% (646/1418) | **-3.9pp** | -2.14 |
  | edgeCase | 6-8 | 52.4% (930/1774) | 50.9% (772/1516) | -1.5pp | -0.86 |
  | normal | 2-4 | 89.0% | 90.2% | +1.1pp | 1.84 |
  | normal | 4-6 | 80.1% | 78.7% | -1.4pp | -2.27 |
  | normal | 6-8 | 70.1% | 71.7% | +1.6pp | 2.13 |

  Every Edge Case sign negative, monotonically worse the closer the target; normals move by nothing coherent. Pooled over everything it reads as a clean null (78.7% base vs 79.0% lead), which is exactly how a real effect hides when the archetype it acts on is a minority of the sample — the pooled number was the first thing computed and the least informative thing produced.

  **Why it fails on the archetype it was built for.** A lead's angular size and a sprite's angular size both carry the same `1/dist`, so their ratio is `speedMultiplier / spriteScale` — 4.0 for an Edge Case against 1.0 for a normal. That ratio was the argument *for* the fix; it is equally the argument against it, because an Edge Case eats four times the penalty when the predicted direction is wrong. And it is wrong often: `updateEnemyAi` returns early inside `ATTACK_RADIUS` (`enemyAi.ts:~182`), so an enemy closing at full speed one window stops dead the next to bite, and a lead built from the previous window walks the aim off a now-stationary target. That is the 0-2 bucket, where the penalty is largest.

  **The angular fire gate is a null and ships anyway, on principle** — every cell inside noise, largest |z| = 1.4, signs mixed, guards clean. That is roughly what the geometry predicts for this test case: the gate binds only past 3.6 tiles (Casual) / 5.8 (Gamer) / 9.6 (Pro, i.e. beyond `engageRadius`), and the demo campaign's shots are mostly closer. Underpowered where it acts rather than shown inert. Kept because a fixed angular tolerance is dimensionally wrong against a target whose width falls off as `1/dist`, and because the bot is destined to become a deathmatch opponent taking longer shots than this campaign offers — but recorded here, and in its own doc comment, as a null rather than a win.

  **The guard half of the protocol was unfalsifiable, and that is the finding with the longest reach.** All four arms cleared **20/20 at every level with a 0.000 death rate**, so `qualifyRate` and the per-level conditional death rate could not have breached their pre-registered thresholds whatever the change did. The demo campaign at `LEVEL_LIMIT=8` currently validates win metrics only. Any future bot A/B needs a deeper level limit or a repo that actually kills the bot, or its guards are decoration. **[Retracted the same day — see the correction below.]**

  Verified: 154 tests in `combatPolicy.test.mjs` (16 new, covering both switches' on and off arms), full `scripts`/`src` suites, `tsc --noEmit`. Archive, including the run script and log: `balancing_capture_ab_aimlead_democampaign/`.

  **Correction, same day.** Two mechanism claims above are wrong and are retracted here rather than edited away. *"It is wrong often, because `updateEnemyAi` returns early inside `ATTACK_RADIUS`"* — the early return is real, but `ATTACK_RADIUS` is **0.5 tiles**, and counting the archived shots shows only **1,007 of 71,259** (1.41%, or 7.1% of the 0-2 bucket) were fired inside it. That cannot move a bucket by 8.9pp. And *"a future attempt needs the enemy's current velocity via `getEnemies()`"* is a no-op experiment: `run-balancing-telemetry.mjs` never passes `recordStepMs`, so `subStepMs == stepMs` and one pump is exactly one 50ms engine frame — the difference between consecutive snapshots already *is* that frame's displacement, so the engine's own figure is the identical number. Both claims were plausible, neither was checked before being written into a commit message, a doc comment, `notes`, `decisions.md` and a PR description; the arithmetic that refutes them took two minutes. The measured result — that the lead regresses, replicated, Edge-Case-specific, decaying with range — is unaffected. The mechanism is simply open, and the next step is to score the predictor rather than build a third fix on a fourth guess. **Correction to that next step, made before acting on it:** it cannot be done with the captures already on disk. The event log records no enemy-position time series — `shot` rows carry `dist` and `targetArch` and nothing else — so scoring predicted against actual positions needs engine-side per-decision logging and a fresh capture first. "The data is on disk and no new capture is needed" was the fifth wrong claim of the day and the first one caught before it cost anything.

  **Second correction, same day, and it is the one with the widest blast radius: the "unfalsifiable guards" finding above is also wrong.** The claim was that a 0.000 base death rate left `qualifyRate` and the conditional death rate unable to breach "whatever the change did", so `GUARDS: pass` "carried no information at all" and the protocol validated win metrics only. Running `checkRollback` against synthetic diffs on that exact baseline refutes every part of it: against a zero-death, 100%-qualify base, **5/20 deaths at a level (+25pp) breaches, 4/20 attempts failing to qualify (-20pp) breaches, 5/20 runs lost between levels (+25pp attrition) breaches, and a single stuck breaches** — while 4/20 deaths (+20pp) and 3/20 non-qualifying (-15pp) pass. The guards were live throughout; they caught nothing because nothing regressed. What a zero base actually costs is *resolution*: at `ATTEMPT_CAP=20` the detection floor is 5 deaths, 4 non-qualifying runs, or 1 stuck, so a pass bounds a large regression and says nothing about a small one. That is weaker than "survival is unaffected" and far stronger than "no information".

  Two further things were missed while asserting it. `computeSurvivalCurve` over all 16 archived combos confirms the zero base is genuine rather than an artifact of the qualify bar — `died=0`, `stuck=0`, `unattributed=0`, `reached` 20 at every level (one exception: base Pro/hard reads `[20,20,20,20,20,19,19,19]`, one run ceasing to contribute after L5 with no recorded death, stuck or crash). And `abReport.mjs` already documents the real blind spot and already covers it: `conditionalDeathRate` is incomplete above the qualify bar, which is exactly why `attritionRate` exists — "the one survival signal that stays honest above the qualify bar" — and `checkRollback` already tests it at the same tolerance. The gap was described in the module's own doc comment, and the finding claimed to have discovered it.

  **Three wrong mechanism claims in one day, all the same shape**: a plausible explanation written straight into a commit message, a doc comment and a PR description without the two-minute check that would have refuted it. All three are retracted in place. The working rule taken from it — check a mechanism claim against the arithmetic or the data *before* it is written down, not after it is published — is recorded here because the pattern, not any individual error, is the thing worth remembering.

- [x] **C sources can finally have locked doors — and the cause was not the C parser (2026-08-11).** The backlog item read "the locked-door/key mechanic never appears for C sources at all… almost certainly visibility detection finding no private/protected members in C". Half right: `cParser.ts` never mentioned `visibility`, so every C entity came back without one. But fixing only that would have changed nothing, because the *map* rule was the real gate.

  **Two changes, one on each side of the parser/map boundary.**

  1. **The parser reports the fact.** C has no access modifiers, but it does have the concept and spells it as linkage: `static` at file scope means internal linkage, reachable only from this translation unit, while a non-static function is the API other files call through a header. `hasStaticStorageClass` maps that onto `visibility: "private"`. Deliberately does not recurse — a `static` inside a body is a function-local variable's storage duration, an unrelated construct, and must not mark its function private.
  2. **The map layer decides what it means.** `isLockableRoom` required `kind === "method"`, which quietly scoped the entire mechanic to languages that nest their callables inside classes. Encapsulation is not a property of class membership: a `static` C function and an unexported Go function are as unreachable from outside as a private Java method, and both arrive as `kind: "function"`. Now any private/protected **callable** locks.

  **The second half was the bigger bug, and it was not only C.** Go already marked lowercase `function_declaration`s private (`goVisibility`), so Go was locking a lowercase *method* while leaving the lowercase *function* beside it wide open. `function || method` is how the rest of the codebase already spells "is a callable" — `pickSpawnAndExit`, both parsers' entity filters and `main.ts`'s entrypoint search all use exactly that pair; `isLockableRoom` was the only place that said `method` alone.

  **Measured before/after with `npm run report:gate-budget`.** Demo campaign: `stage09_worker.go` **0 → 2** gated rooms, `stage16_hardware.h` **0 → 1**, every other level byte-identical; levels gating at least one room 9 → 11, and the budget distribution did not move (p50 2, p90 4, max 4 — still inside `MAX_GATE_ROOMS`). On real C: wolf3d's `WOLFSRC` now gates on 1 of 12 levels, curl's `lib` on 4 of 12 — each of those four hitting the cap of 4, which is the 2026-08-10 cap earning its keep on a repo carrying **3,086** `static` definitions across 373 `.c` files.

  **`demo-campaign/main.c` still gates nothing, and that is correct** — it contains no `static` functions at all. Level 1 is unchanged. The demo campaign's C representation of the mechanic comes from `stage16_hardware.h`.

  Regenerating `defaultHighscore.ts` was mandatory, not tidiness: door and key placement draw from the level's shared `mulberry32` stream, so adding a gate shifts every later draw while `balanceHash` stays silent. `verify:replay` was run *before* regenerating to confirm it would actually catch this — it failed at exactly level 9, the Go level, with levels 1-8 replaying perfectly.

- [x] **A key opens its room, not one doorway — coloured keys shipped 2026-08-10.** `openDoorAhead` spent one key per *doorway*, so a locked room with six mouths cost six keys for one space, and opening a door you had already come through from the other side charged you again. `MAX_GATES` (2026-08-08) bounded the damage by pricing rooms in doorways and preferring one-mouth rooms, which penalised a shape that now costs nothing extra.

  A key belongs to a **gate** (one locked room), carries a colour, and opens every door of that gate. It is **permanent inventory** — never spent — so pushing a room's second mouth is free.

  **The cap was measured, not guessed.** `npm run report:gate-budget` (new, and the before/after instrument) counts gated *rooms* per level, which nothing reported before. Two independent samples agreed exactly: demo-campaign (17 levels, 9 gating) and laravel's `Support` (25 levels, 15 gating), both p50 2, p90 3, **max 3**. `MAX_GATE_ROOMS = 4` therefore never gates less than today's ceiling, leaves one slot of headroom, and equals the palette so no level reuses a colour. Room attribution is pure geometry over the room rect, *not* `roomMouths` — that helper only matches tiles which are still floor, so it returns nothing once doors are placed.

  **Measured after**: demo `stage06` went from **5 keys to 2** for the same two rooms, `stage03` 4 → 2, and keys now equal gated rooms exactly on every level.

  **The trap this had to avoid**, recorded in `doorsKeys.ts` since 2026-08-08: issuing one key per room while the engine still charged per doorway makes `assertAllRoomsReachable` report rooms unreachable — correctly. Generator and engine therefore moved in one commit.

  **Gate identity is a side table, not new `Tile` values.** `Tile` is a closed union mirrored by *five* hand-maintained solid-tile sets (`player.ts`'s `isWall` plus four script-side copies, including one in `combatPolicy.mjs` that is easy to miss), so four more values would multiply all of them. `src/map/gates.ts` turns `GameMap.gates` into an `Int16Array` behind a `WeakMap`, built once per level and never invalidated — door tiles only ever go `3 → 0` and never *gain* a gate, so a stale entry is unreachable by construction.

  **`placeKeys` searches harder rather than dropping a gate.** A skipped key used to strand one doorway of a room that had others; with one key per gate it strands the whole room. So the fallback relaxes the *convenience* exclusions in tiers — enemy tiles, breakup rooms, then sharing a tile with another key — while never relaxing the exception-zone exclusion, which is a measured gameplay decision (a key past the `try` gauntlet turns an opt-in detour into a mandatory damage tax; it was landing there on 4 of 7 zone levels and produced 36 `healthDrainFrozen` events in one scan). A room is un-gated only if even that fails, or if it turned out unreachable — which makes **"every gate has exactly one key" a total invariant** and retires `pathing.ts`'s documented "evidently rare, but not structurally impossible" hole.

  **`assertAllRoomsReachable` got simpler *and* stricter.** No key counting: a gate opens only when its own key has been collected. The fungible counter let a surplus key from gate A pay for gate B whose own key was never found — exactly the generator bug the assertion exists to catch, and invisible until now. One new test pins that deadlock; another pins order-independence, which is a real property (keys are permanent and each gate's key sits in the region reachable before it opens, so gate opening is a confluent closure) and is what the route planner leans on.

  **Coop: a key is granted team-wide.** One key per gate would otherwise strand every teammate but whoever walked over it, and `routePlanner` runs per-bot with no model of a teammate's inventory, so a session would *wedge* at the door rather than merely slow down. That deleted the key loot-drop category outright — `"key"` is gone from `LootKind`, `lootApply`, `lootColors` and both death/disconnect drop paths — rather than leaving dead code behind.

  **The locked-door hint now pings *this* gate's key** (`keyForGate`, was `nearestReachableKey`). Pointing at the nearest key of any kind is confidently wrong once only one key opens the door being pushed, and `null` — "it is behind something else" — is the honest answer.

  **Colour lives in the engine, not the map**, exactly as `StyleSetId` does; each renderer keeps its own table by value, following the convention `raycaster.ts` and `automap.ts` both state explicitly. Violet, not yellow: amber already means "keyless switchboard door", and `textures.ts` records keeping the two door kinds tellable apart as a standing constraint. Worth being honest that this *reclassifies* doors — `types.ts` lists the styleset-invariant "gameplay signal" textures and doors are **not** among them, so "a gate colour must not depend on the level" is a new argument, not one the existing rule already made.

  **Two incidental findings.** `verify:campaign`'s TODO-outcome coverage turned out ~8% fragile to *any* rng-shifting change (9 independent 1-in-3 rolls); this change landed in that 8% and deterministically dropped the trap outcome until a 10th TODO was added — filed in `notes` as its own item. And several `??` fallbacks written defensively during this work were removed again: they were unreachable and cost coverage, which is the repo's own "an unreachable branch is a coverage liability" rule.

  **Cost**: `defaultHighscore.ts` had to be regenerated. `balanceHash` covers only the enemy roster, and enemies are generated *before* doors and keys, so it stays **silent** while every shipped replay diverges — `verify:replay` is the only real check.

- [x] **Players can name themselves, once, for the whole game. SHIPPED 2026-08-10.** Started as a multiplayer-only field (see the entry this replaced, folded in below); the user's own framing corrected it mid-build: the name is wanted for **single-player highscores** too, and to say which bot profile set each shipped default entry — which is what the backlog's "player name config, for highscores" item had asked for all along. So it is a general setting, persisted, not a multiplayer lobby field.

  **`codeenstein-player-name`, alongside gore/difficulty/volume**, with the same never-throw load/save shape. Sanitized on read as well as write, because storage is not a trusted source either — a value left by devtools or an older build gets the same treatment a freshly-typed one does. Saved on `input`, not `change`: a text field's `change` only fires on blur, and hosting a session immediately after typing must already see it.

  **The highscore entry carries `playerName` only when one was set.** The fallback ("Player", greyed) is applied at render, not baked into stored data — otherwise naming yourself later would leave your older runs permanently stamped with the fallback, and an entry recorded before the setting existed would differ from a new unnamed one for no reason.

  **The shipped board names its bot.** `installPlayerName` sets the profile name in localStorage before each generated run, so a regenerated board carries "Casual"/"Gamer"/"Pro" for free. The already-shipped board was **back-filled** (`--backfill-player-name`) rather than regenerated: same write-order-plus-`PROFILES_HASH` argument as `--backfill-rot-speed`, saving a ~33-minute wedge-prone regeneration to add a cosmetic label. `verify:replay` was run afterwards and the board still replays frame-for-frame.

  **A pre-existing bug surfaced the moment the rows became distinguishable**: the shipped fallback board was rendered in bot-profile order, which happens to be *ascending* by score, so the rank column told every first-time visitor that the worst run was #1. A real board has always been sorted at record time; the fallback never was. Fixed at display time in `loadHighscoresForDisplay`, deliberately not in the file — the generator's back-fill modes and `verify:replay`'s entry indices both rely on the file staying in `PROFILES` order.

  **The highscore panel tests indexed cells positionally**, so one new column broke seven of them at once; they now resolve a cell through the table's own `<thead>`. That is the fix for the next column too.

  The multiplayer half, built first and still true: Built from the plan parked in `player-names-state.md` (deleted with this entry, per the scoped-state-file convention). There was no per-player identity concept anywhere in the codebase before this — the existing "Display name" field names the *session* in the public lobby listing, which is why the new field sits above the Host/Join subtabs rather than inside either panel.

  **One field, not two.** The plan called for an input in both the Host and the Join panel; a single one above the subtabs is who you are in either role, and two inputs for one value can disagree. The session-listing name stays exactly where it was.

  **The handshake is now two-phase, and the reason is worth keeping.** Every guest's `session-init` has to carry the *complete* `displayNames` map, and the host fans its per-guest setup out concurrently — so a single-pass exchange cannot know guest B's name while building guest A's message. Phase A (per guest, concurrent) exchanges build-version and the guest's name; the host merges every reply plus its own; phase B (per guest, concurrent) sends `session-init` and the chunked map. Both phases still *begin with a host send*, so the guest stays purely reply-driven and the eager-send race `sessionSetupGuest.ts` documents at length is not reintroduced. `runHostSessionSetup` split into `runHostSessionSetupPhaseA`/`PhaseB`.

  **The fallback is the old behaviour, in one place.** `resolvePlayerDisplayName` (`src/engine/playerNames.ts`) resolves a chosen name or the capitalized roster id — which is precisely what `multiplayerResultRows` computed inline before — and it is applied once, where a name enters the engine (`PlayerState.displayName`), so nothing downstream re-derives it. A name is untrusted input from another peer, so the same boundary strips control characters (including the bidi overrides that would reorder text drawn around the name) and caps length at 24.

  **Two things the unit suite structurally cannot see, both checked live.** Names are exchanged by a real handshake between two real browsers, so `verify:multiplayer-netcode` now asserts each peer sees the *other's* chosen name (one of them deliberately non-ASCII), and `verify:multiplayer-multiguest` asserts all three peers agree on all three names with one guest choosing none — N=3 being where the phase barrier actually earns itself. Both needed a `getPlayerDisplayName` read-only hook, the same shape as the existing `getPlayerStatus`.

  **The label was drawn to `drawEnemyOverlay`'s conventions and then deviated from them once, deliberately.** Same 10px monospace, centering, translucent plate and `textAlign` restore, and the same "only label a sprite whose centre column is not wall-occluded" rule. But the plate is sized to the *measured text* rather than to the sprite: rendering it showed a distant teammate's name spilling off a few-pixel-wide plate onto bare wall. An enemy's plate is sized to its HP bar, which is a gauge whose width means something; this one exists only to keep text readable.

  **What is deliberately not here**: the name is not persisted. Retyping it every session is mildly annoying, but the backlog's own "player name config, for highscores" item wants a stored name *with* a localStorage version tag and migration story, and inventing half of that here would be the wrong half.

- [x] **A truncated GitHub tree, and a spent rate limit, are now visible to the player. FIXED 2026-08-10.** `fetchGithubTree` detected the API's `truncated: true` and `console.warn`'d it, which was the whole of the signalling — and `consoleSidebar.ts` patches `console.log` **only**, so the one place a player might have seen it never showed it. The campaign then played happily from whatever subset arrived.

  Fixed on both halves the backlog item asked for: the line moved to `console.log` so the in-game console mirrors it, and `TreeNode.truncated` (set on the root, by `fetchGithubTree` alone) drives a persistent `.tree-notice` above the file tree — persistent because the incompleteness outlives the load, and a load-time message is exactly what gets missed. Wording matches `troubleshooting.md`'s advice, which was already good: clone it and use the **Local** tab.

  Rate-limit failures were the same class and went with it. Requests are unauthenticated, so a burst of browsing starts failing, and `Failed to fetch repository tree (403 Forbidden)` reads like a private repo. A `403`/`429` whose `x-ratelimit-remaining` is `0` now reports the limit, the minutes until reset, and that a local folder works meanwhile. A `403` **without** the spent budget stays a plain access failure — mislabelling a real permissions error as "wait a few minutes" would be the worse bug.

  **One thing only a browser could have shown, and it changed the implementation.** The header-gated check is exact against `api.github.com`, which sends `Access-Control-Expose-Headers: … X-RateLimit-Remaining, X-RateLimit-Reset …`. `raw.githubusercontent.com` sends **no** `Access-Control-Expose-Headers` at all (verified against both hosts with `curl -H Origin:`), so from a page its rate-limit headers are unreadable however present they are on the wire — the same gate there is not conservative, it is *dead*. That path (lazily-fetched file content, so it fails **mid-session**, levels into a campaign) therefore reads a bare `403` as a rate limit: a file in a public repo answers `200` or `404`, and there is no permissions dimension for a `403` to come from. The first Playwright pass fulfilled the mock response without the expose-headers header and the message did not fire — worth keeping, because a mock that is more permissive than the browser would have shipped a check that never runs.

  Verified in the real app, not only in jsdom: Playwright intercepts GitHub's API, and the marker renders above the tree, the console sidebar mirrors the line, and the rate-limit message reaches `#github-status` with its reset estimate.

- [x] **`fetch-online-wads.mjs` degrades instead of taking `npm run dev`/`npm run build` down. FIXED 2026-08-10.** It ran as the `predev`/`prebuild` hook and `process.exit(1)`'d on any download, extract or parse failure, so a clone with no network — or one external host being down, or a catalog entry gone 404 — could neither start the dev server nor build. Nothing in the catalog is load-bearing: procedural default textures ship, `public/wads/` is gitignored, and a missing pack already degraded cleanly at runtime.

  It now warns per entry and exits 0. `--strict` (or `CODEENSTEIN_WADS_STRICT=1`) restores the old behaviour, and CI runs the fetch with it ahead of `npm run build` — so a dead catalog entry is still caught there rather than silently shipping a shorter list, which is the trade the backlog item asked to decide.

  Three adjacent defects fixed with it. A download failure escaped to the top-level catch and aborted the whole run rather than one entry; a source URL that had already failed was re-attempted for every later entry sharing it (three catalog entries share one Freedoom zip); and **an unparseable download stayed on disk**, where the skip-if-present check meant it was never re-fetched — the failure was permanent and the game would serve a broken pack forever, directly contradicting the script's own "a partial fetch self-heals on the next run" doc comment. It is deleted when `loadWadTextures` rejects it.

  Verified by temporarily adding a 404 URL and a non-WAD URL to the catalog, since a clean run skips everything already on disk and would have exercised no failure path at all: both failures fire, default exits 0, `--strict` and the env var both exit 1, and the unparseable file is gone afterwards.

- [x] **Every shipped replay played back wrong — the bot's rotation multiplier was a simulation input the replay format didn't carry. FIXED 2026-08-09.** "Watch Replay" on any of the three bundled entries pointed the view where the run never looked, shot at nothing and died in seconds. It played; it never errored.

  `createPlayerState` read `rotSpeedMultiplier` from the `?botRotSpeedMul` URL param under `isTestHooksActive()`, and `generate-default-highscore.mjs` records at 2.0/3.5/5.0 per bot profile. A player watching has neither the param nor test hooks, so playback ran at 1.0 and every recorded turn under-rotated by 2-5x. `ReplayLevelSegment` carried `filePath, bonusLevel, gameplaySeed, difficulty, gore, astHash, balanceHash, frames` — every simulation input except that one — and playback is blind input replay, so it could not reproduce what it was never told.

  Pre-existing but made fatal by the turn-split (`6e45951`): a turn used to be held ~50ms and still rotated ~0.13rad at 1x, drifting slowly; a ~3ms burst at 1x rotates ~0.008rad, so the aim was simply gone. Reverting the turn-split would only have hidden it.

  **The board did not need regenerating, contrary to the parked handoff's "they cannot be salvaged".** `main()` pushes `keptEntries` in `Object.entries(PROFILES)` order and `PROFILES_HASH` pins that those profiles haven't moved, so entry *i* was provably played by profile *i* — the value was recoverable, not lost. `generate-default-highscore.mjs --backfill-rot-speed` stamps all 51 segments in seconds, and `verify:replay` is what proves the mapping right rather than assumed. That avoided a ~33-minute regeneration that is itself wedge-prone at demo-campaign L6.

  **No `replayCodec.ts` change was needed either**, also contrary to the handoff: `packBoardForStorage` builds its header as the whole board JSON with only `frames` emptied, so per-segment scalars round-trip for free. The `PACKED_BOOLS`/`bin1:`-bump rule applies only to per-frame `InputSnapshot` fields.

  Two adjacent record/playback asymmetries were found in the same path and fixed with it:

  - **Every replay recorded on easy or hard was refused outright** as "recorded under different game balance". `computeBalanceHash` snapshots the roster synchronously before its first `await`; recording hashes *before* `new RaycasterEngine`, playback hashed *after* — and the constructor rescales `hp`/`maxHp` in place by the difficulty multipliers, one of the four hashed fields. Normal is 1x, so the normal-only bundled board never showed it, and every existing replay test used normal.
  - **Playback never ran `primeForPlay()`**, so it skipped the spawn-tile reveal live play gets from `start()`. Latent rather than active — `markVisitedAround` floors to the tile, the player cannot leave the spawn tile in one frame, and `mapCompletionBonus` is a threshold — but a genuine asymmetry in the "100% Clear" numerator. Fixed with `startReplayDriven()`, deliberately *not* `startExternallyDriven()`, whose wall-clock FPS measurement the replay viewer is excluded from.

  **Nothing could have caught any of it**: there was no replay-playback verification at all. `astHash` guards level content and `balanceHash` guards "same game", but nothing asserted a replay still *plays back* as recorded, and CI was fully green with the board broken. `npm run verify:replay` (`scripts/verify-replay.mjs`) closes that: it clicks the real "Watch" button, drives playback under the virtual clock, and asserts `endReason === null`, `framesConsumed === framesRecorded` per level, and the carryover ladder — `advanceToNextLevel` writes `priorScore: stats.score` into the next level's carryover, so a recorded payload is its own per-level expected-value table and needs no fixture. It reproduced the bug before the fix (level 1 died after 1,351 of 2,893 frames, scoring 806 against a recorded 1,898) and passes frame-for-frame after. This also closes the scope note in `verify-campaign-playthrough.mjs`, which verifies payload structure and explicitly does not re-simulate.

  Caveat recorded in the script: score parity holds under `?testHooks=1` only, since `PLAYER_STATS_ENABLED` is `false` and the accuracy bonus reads `p.telemetry`. Simulation parity — frame counts, win/death, the ladder — is unconditional, which is why the frame-count check leads.

- [x] **An Elite fight has never been won. 1,332 spawned across Stage C, 2 died (2026-08-08).** Both kills were `normal`-difficulty ~2,000 HP enemies taking 22-24s; **zero on `hard`, at any HP**. Measured from the existing event logs with the new `killRateByHpBand` — no new capture.

  | max HP | spawned | killed | rate |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|
  | <250 | 857,568 | 223,637 | 26.1% |
  | 250-499 | 16,931 | 3,551 | 21.0% |
  | **500-1,999** | **0** | 0 | — |
  | 2,000-2,999 | 664 | 2 | **0.3%** |
  | 3,000-4,999 | 655 | 0 | **0.0%** |
  | 5,000+ | 13 | 0 | **0.0%** |

  - **This retires the "encounter geometry" hypothesis, which had the wrong premise.** The four "survivable" contrast levels are not levels with winnable Elite fights — **all 514 of their cleared runs left the Elite alive**, as did 2 of wolf3d L8's 3 lucky clears. There has never been a won Elite fight to compare a lost one against, so no amount of comparing room shape, cover or sightlines could have found anything. Elite HP, level number and `clearRatio` failed to separate fatal from survivable because none of them was the variable.
  - **The 500-1,999 void is the mechanism**, and it is the cliff `enemies.ts:22-38` already documents: `ELITE_HP_MULTIPLIER` stacks on *un-split* HP, so complexity 39 is a 4-enemy / 976 HP pack (244 each, well inside the killable band) and complexity 40 is one 2,000 HP enemy, well outside it. Nothing is ever generated in between, on any repo.
  - **What *does* separate fatal from survivable, cleanly: whether the planned route walks inside `AGGRO_RADIUS` (7.5t) of the Elite.** Verified by regenerating all 7 levels offline and matching them against the capture's recorded static block, so these are provably the maps the bot played:

    | | route enters aggro? | closest approach |
    |---|---|---:|
    | wolf3d 8, curl 12, laravel 14 (**fatal**) | **YES** | 0.0t, 0.0t, 3.0t |
    | ripgrep 13, codeenstein3d 12/13/15 (survivable) | no | 13.4-44.1t |

    Zero overlap and a wide margin. On wolf3d 8 and curl 12 the route runs straight *through* the Elite's tile. curl 12 is forced twice over: its Elite's `home` also contains the exit (so `exitRoomHasAliveEnemy` gates it) **and** blocking that rect disconnects spawn from exit — in a 316-tile map, the smallest in the corpus.
  - **This is diagnosis, not the fix. Routing around Elites is explicitly not an option (user, 2026-08-08): they must be killable.** The metric's job is to predict *which* levels a fix must improve, and to verify it afterwards. Note an Elite chases at `MOVEMENT_SPEED` 1.7 against the player's 3.2, so it can always be outrun — "avoidable" was never the same as "safe".
  - **Fix shipped**: split the pack and cap the HP (`ELITE_MEMBER_HP_CAP = 350`, `ELITE_MAX_MEMBERS = 8` in `enemies.ts`), so every generated enemy sits inside the measured killable band while an Elite room keeps its 2x budget. Corpus-wide the largest enemy the generator can emit drops from **50,400 HP to 525** (Hard). Only the anchor keeps `Enemy.elite`: the damage multiplier is per enemy, so flagging the whole pack would multiply the room's DPS by its size.
  - **The cap is in *base* HP but the evidence is in *runtime* HP — this is the easy mistake and I made it first.** 500 base reads as "the measured band" but becomes **750 on Hard**, past every observation. 350 base is 525 on Hard, the top of the band the data covers. Related: the largest enemy ever killed *on Hard* is **338** (112,311 kills); the 2,200 figure is a `normal`-difficulty kill.
  - **VERIFIED 2026-08-09, four repos, 360 attempts, every cell at target.** The extrapolation held: 525 on Hard was above anything ever killed there, and it dies.

    | repo | largest killed (hard) | Elite kills | Elite TTK p50 | 500-999 HP band |
    |---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
    | wolf3d | 338 -> **500** | 0 -> 20 | 3.1s | 0/0 -> 57/562 (10%) |
    | curl | 338 -> **525** | 0 -> 62 | 2.4s | 0/0 -> 61/199 (31%) |
    | laravel | 338 -> **525** | 0 -> 21 | 4.0s | 0/0 -> 139/328 (42%) |
    | vim | *never captured* | 5 | 4.6s | 79/416 (19%) |

    **Combined 336 kills of 1,505 spawned (22%) in a band that previously contained zero enemies** — the `0/0` is the structural void, not a small sample. Every previously-fatal level is passable: **wolf3d L8 0/78 -> 40/44**, **curl's forced-Elite level 0/60 -> 24/58** (its room holds the exit *and* disconnects spawn from exit), **laravel L14 0/5 -> 8/41** with 8 runs completing a full campaign for the first time. Reproduce with `scripts/lib/eventMetrics.mjs`'s `killRateByHpBand` / `damageTakenByAttacker`, both surfaced by `balancing:events`.
  - **Read the damage-share numbers carefully — I nearly conflated two of them.** The 81-99% figures were *per fatal level*; their comparable after-values are wolf3d L9 40%, curl L9 26%, laravel L14 24%. Campaign-wide shares are a different and much smaller number (22%->7%, 18%->13%, 12%->3%). Also **laravel's before arm is n=5 hard runs**, since Stage C stopped it early — a far weaker baseline than wolf3d's 78 or curl's 60.
  - **Three A/Bs later: the remaining levels are not a balance defect, and the search is closed (2026-08-09).** wolf3d L9 was the test bed (highest melee share, least bot-confounded). All three arms, hard, 15x3 attempts each, against the verified baseline of **12/40 cleared**:

    | arm | L9 cleared | **total dmg taken** | melee | ranged | kills | time |
    |---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
    | baseline | 12/40 (30%) | **122** | 66 | 48 | 4 | 30s |
    | `enemyAimSpreadDeg` 0->3 | 12/40 (30%) | — | — | — | 4 | 35s |
    | `STANDOFF_MIN_TARGET_HP` 500->400 | 5/39 (13%), p=0.099 | **127** | 45 | 82 | 4 | 31s |
    | `CRITICAL_HEALTH_FRACTION` 0.2->0.35 | 13/42 (31%), p=1.00 | **126** | 60 | 65 | 4 | 30s |

    - **Total damage taken is invariant.** Every lever moved damage *between* melee and ranged and reduced none of it, because the total is exposure-time x ambient DPS and exposure time is set by time-to-kill (~30s for 4 kills), not by positioning. That is the shared wrong assumption behind all three: they were defensive levers against a quantity no defensive lever controls. Do not re-run them.
    - **Why standoff backfired, specifically**: `STANDOFF_DISTANCE` is 5 and `RANGED_RANGE` is 8, so backing out of melee parks the bot inside the band where hard enemies shoot at **0 degrees spread and cannot miss**. Melee damage fell 66->45 and ranged rose 48->82. Retreating on hard is a bad trade at any trigger, which the critical-health arm independently reproduced (ranged 48->65).
    - **A real interaction the Elite cap created, worth knowing even though fixing it did not help**: `STANDOFF_MIN_TARGET_HP` is 500, and the cap put **1,246 of 2,235** big enemies at exactly 500 or below (`500 > 500` is false), silently disabling standoff against most pack members. It had always fired when Elites were 3,000+. A balance constant landing on a *bot* constant is a class of interaction nothing checks.
    - **Conclusion: L9 at 30% and L10 at 17% are not walls.** The wall was L8 at **0/78**, now **91%**. What remains is a campaign that turns attritional deep into hard, played by a bot taking up to 53% of its damage while not fighting. The only levers left — time-to-kill, or hard's `damage: 1.5` — are design choices about how hard Hard should be, not defects. Per the user: *"hard is supposed to be hard"*.

  - **The walls that remain are NOT Elite walls, and this is the Stage D finding.** At every level still clearing under 50%, `normal` enemies dominate and the Elite is incidental: vim L9 **normal 91% / elite 7%** (80 enemies), curl L10 normal 78% / elite 16% (58 enemies), wolf3d L10 normal 75% / elite 11%. laravel L14 is the only one where the Elite is still a real share (24%), and it has the smallest roster (21). So the next balance question is **roster density and attrition**, which the Elite ceiling does not touch and was never meant to. Note most of these levels were *never reachable before*, so they are first observations rather than regressions.
  - **Follow-up worth one pass**: pack members carry the elevated HP but not the `elite` flag, so they render as ordinary enemies that take ~3x longer to kill than a normal one. Mechanically intended; visually it reads as an unexplained sponge. A tint/scale for "Elite pack member" without the damage multiplier would fix it — that needs a third flag or an `Enemy` field, which is why it is not in this change.

- [x] **FIXED 2026-08-09 (`6e45951`), guard re-run below. The turn key inherited the strafe's widened hold, so aim corrections overshot 3-22x. Root-caused from the curl wedges.**
  - **Guard result — curl, normal, 45 attempts per arm, same binary via `TURN_SPLIT_PHASES`:**

    | criterion | OFF | ON | reading |
    |---|---:|---:|---|
    | nav-warns | 4,327 | **0** | mechanism fixed, unambiguous |
    | shots/run | 29 | **35** | +21% — the aim now settles, so the trigger actually pulls |
    | damage taken/run | 16 | **16** | dodging preserved; the 2026-07-29 failure did NOT recur |
    | deaths | 20 | 18 | — |
    | levels cleared | 96.0% | 96.6% | — |
    | wedge rate | 3.46% | 2.59% | p=0.47, **not significant** |
    | `enemyAccuracy` | 0.2383 | 0.2671 | **breached** its guard (+12% rel.) |
    | `qualifyRate` | 100% | 100% | **saturated — could not detect harm** |

  - **Kept despite the breached guard, deliberately.** `enemyAccuracy` is a proxy for "the bot still dodges"; damage taken per run is the quantity itself and is *identical*. Enemies land a higher fraction of fewer shots because fights end sooner. Overriding a pre-registered guard on a more direct measurement is a judgement call and is recorded as one.
  - **Two reasons this test is weaker than it looks.** `qualifyRate` sat at 100% in both arms — on curl/normal it had no headroom, so "no drop" is not evidence of safety, and damage-taken is carrying that argument instead. And the wedges, the whole motivation, improved only directionally. A hard-difficulty or multi-repo arm would settle both.
  - **Scope, corrected from the first write-up.** `intent.durationMs` *is* the decision length, so an unwidened aiming decision was already short and correct. This only ever affected decisions where something widened the burst — which is the combat-stall state, since the stall-strafe fires *because* the bot has stalled. The remedy was producing the aim failure that kept the stall alive.
  - **Still open, and cheap: threats have no give-up.** Mines abandon a target after `MINE_TARGET_GIVEUP_TICKS` 40; a threat gets `COMBAT_STALL_TICKS_THRESHOLD` 40 -> a strafe and nothing else. `combatStallTicks` also resets whenever position changes by 0.01 (`posKey` is 2 decimals), so the strafe it triggers resets the counter that triggered it. And `pickThreat` uses `visible` only as a *sort* key, so an aggroed enemy behind a wall is still selected as the threat.

- [x] **DONE 2026-08-09 (`a687d14`). Flamethrower (Friday Hotfix): give it real reach and replace the hard cutoff with distance falloff (2026-08-05).** Shipped as full damage to `fullDamageRange` 2.5, decaying to zero at `maxRange` 6.5, via `rangeDamageScale` in `weapons.ts`. The plateau ends short of the old 3.5 cutoff on purpose, so it is a redistribution rather than a buff to the highest-DPS profile in the table. Both bot mirrors updated *and* the curve mirrored into `expectedDamagePerShot` — the number alone would have left the bot scoring 48 a pull at 6 tiles where the engine lands ~12. **Acceptance is still unmeasured**: the item asks for pellets landing in the 4-6t bucket and kill share moving off 5.4% *without* total gas damage per level rising, which needs a capture against the same seed set. Today `maxRange: 3.5` (`weapons.ts:272`) is a **binary gate**, not a range curve: in `resolveShot` a pellet whose target is beyond it becomes `{kind: "miss"}` outright (`engine.ts:4495-4499`), so the weapon deals full 8-per-pellet damage at 3.4 tiles and literally nothing at 3.6. A flame jet should thin out, not stop at a wall.
  - **The precedent is already in the codebase.** `rocketDamageAt` (`rockets.ts:116-121`) is exactly the shape wanted: `max(FLOOR, 1 - distance / radius)` scaling the damage, zero past the radius. The flamethrower wants the same treatment with a plateau — full damage out to roughly today's 3.5, then decaying to zero somewhere around 6-7 tiles.
  - **Why the hard cap exists, and why falloff serves that goal better.** `weapons.ts:252-259` records that cone spread alone let it connect from further out "than a real flamethrower should ever reach", so the cap was added as the thing that actually enforces reach. Falloff enforces the same intent more honestly: reach is bounded by damage becoming negligible rather than by an invisible wall, and the tight-jet-fanning-at-the-tip visual (`FlameStream`) already implies decay the mechanics don't deliver.
  - **The data says it is locked to the wrong band.** Over the 2026-08-04 capture: **11,874 trigger-pulls, 64.6% hit rate, 5.4% kill share**. Hit rate by engagement range runs 76% (0-2t) / 66% (2-4t) / 33% (4-6t) — but that last bucket rests on **30 pellets out of ~71,000**, because 3.5 tiles cuts it off before it gets there. Meanwhile fights against *regular* enemies happen at a median of **4.6 tiles**, outside its reach entirely, while Edge Cases sit at 2.7t. The weapon is confined to the band that holds the 10-15 HP trash.
  - **It is already the highest-DPS weapon in the game, so the falloff is what pays for the range.** 48 damage per trigger-pull at `fireIntervalSec: 0.1` is ~480 DPS, and `ammoPerShot: 2.5` exists specifically because a full gas pool would otherwise outdamage everything else. Extending reach *without* decay would be a straight buff to the strongest damage profile in the table — the two changes are a tuned pair, like the shotgun's cadence/damage pair.
  - **Touchpoints, including the one that fails silently.** `Weapon` needs the falloff fields (`weapons.ts:64`), `resolveShot` needs to attach a damage scale to the pellet outcome instead of discarding it, and the damage call site must apply it. Then the **bot's mirrored table**, which `adding-a-weapon.md` flags as the silent-failure step — 3.5 is duplicated in *two* places there: `FRIDAY_HOTFIX_MAX_RANGE` (`combatPolicy.mjs:318`) and `WEAPON_STATS[5].maxRange` (`:910`). Leave those and the bot keeps scoring the weapon as if it stopped at 3.5, which would quietly invalidate any A/B of the change.
  - **Known cost: this invalidates every replay.** `WEAPONS` is folded into `SIMULATION_BALANCE` wholesale (`balanceHash.ts`), so any damage-curve change alters the hash and `defaultHighscore.ts` must be regenerated — a ~35-minute bot run. Per the acid-decay entry below: **regenerate last**, after every other simulation change has landed, not in the middle.
  - **What would show it worked**: pellets actually landing in the 4-6t bucket (today 30 of ~71k) and a kill share moving off 5.4%, *without* total gas-pool damage per level rising — the falloff should redistribute its damage across range, not add to it. Re-runnable from the same seed set as the 2026-08-04 capture for a like-for-like comparison.

- [x] **Lane idle is 18-42% and it is almost entirely the tail — the fix is a smaller work unit, and measurement says that is nearly free.** Researched 2026-08-08 (not implemented).
  - **Measured idle, per Stage C capture**: ripgrep 21% (442 of 2,064 lane-min), curl 24%, serilog 18%, sinatra 26%, wolf3d **42%**, codeenstein3d **29%**. codeenstein3d is the important one: it is the first capture that ran **with** the tail-packing and yield-based-rate fixes, and idle went *up*, not down. Those fixes did not address this; do not read them as having done so. wolf3d's outlier is explained — four of its six combos retired early on the old invocation cap, leaving two cells for five lanes; that cap is fixed, so 18-26% is the honest baseline.
  - **The cause is unit granularity, not scheduling.** A work unit is one invocation = a chunk of attempts on **one** combo. Concurrency per combo is `ceil(remaining / CHUNK)` with `CHUNK = 20`, so once a cell needs fewer than 20 attempts it collapses to **one** unit — and at the tail, with two cells short, only two lanes can have work no matter how many are free. Everything else is idle by construction.
  - **The objection to fine-grained units is refuted by measurement.** Fitting 171 real invocations (banked attempts vs wall time) gives **fixed overhead 0.3 min** and **3.1 min per attempt** — so a *one-attempt* invocation costs 3.4 min, of which only **8%** is overhead. Browser and vite startup is ~18 seconds, not the minutes it feels like; `ensureDevServer` already reuses a live server, which is most of why. Splitting the tail into single attempts is therefore close to free.
  - **Options, cheapest first.**
    1. *Size the per-combo concurrency cap by the smallest useful unit, not `CHUNK`.* `ceil(remaining / MIN_CHUNK)` — or literally `remaining` at the tail — turns "3 attempts left, 1 lane allowed" into "3 attempts left, 3 lanes allowed". One expression; recovers most of the tail.
    2. *Deliberate overshoot at the very end.* Let free lanes race the last few attempts and accept a small surplus. At 3.4 min a spare attempt is far cheaper than a lane idling for one. Requires reporting the **real** per-cell `n` rather than the target — the analysis already does.
    3. *Cross-repo pipelining* — the structural one, already filed in the lane-parallelism item: a remote lane can start repo N+1 while the local lane finishes N, because each remote has its own checkout at `REMOTE_DIR` and `bootstrapHost` force-checks-out a sha. This is the only option that removes the tail entirely rather than shortening it.
    4. *Attempt-level pull scheduling* (a lane pulls single attempts and keeps a browser warm) — **probably not worth building.** Its whole advantage is amortising startup, and startup is 18 seconds. Option 1 captures nearly the same win for a fraction of the work. Recorded so nobody re-derives it as the obvious answer.
  - **The tail is worse than "some idle" — the last cell can land on the slowest lane and there is no way to take it back. Observed four times in one night (2026-08-08/09, the Elite-fix capture, 5 lanes).** Every repo ended the same way: 5 of 6 cells complete, one cell short by a handful of attempts, and that cell held by a single lane while every other lane sat idle with nothing it was permitted to take.
