Remaining lever: microsecond `dt` quantisation (~2x) — only safe if the live loop quantises too.

- [x] **shotgun needs firing interval, can be fired into semi-auto-mode :D — fixed in the engine, where it should have been all along (2026-08-01).** `Weapon.fireIntervalSec` already existed and `updateFiring`'s semi-auto branch already honoured it; the pistol and shotgun simply defined none, so their fire rate was whatever the input device produced. **Pure data fix, zero engine logic**: shotgun `fireIntervalSec: 0.85` (a pump cycle), pistol `0.15` (~6.6/sec). This closes the loop on the 2026-07-17 *"highscore bot presses fire key so fast, the pistol becomes a smg lmao"* item, which was fixed **bot-side only** (`profile.fireCooldownMs`) and left the engine — and therefore every human player — uncapped. The bot's per-profile rate survives as a *human-hand* limit stacked on top; the two now compose via `max` in both `scoreRangedWeapon` and the fire gate.
  - **The cap alone would have destroyed the shotgun, which is the non-obvious part.** At 0.85s it does 7x18/0.85 = **148 DPS** point-blank against the pistol's 22/0.15 = **147** — identical output for **4x the ammo**, from the same shared `bullets` pool, and only at contact range where the whole cone connects. Measured against the real bot scorer: the shotgun went from winning **45 of 45** sampled (profile x distance x HP) matchups to **6 of 45**. Fixed in the same change by taking `damagePerPellet` **18 -> 25** (~206 DPS, ~1.4x the pistol), so "devastating up close" survives the cadence. **The cadence and the damage are a tuned pair — retuning either means re-checking the ratio.**
  - **Two deliberate properties, now pinned by tests**: the cooldown lives on `PlayerState`, not the weapon, so tapping `1` and back can't switch-cancel the pump; and quick-melee (`Space`) bypasses `updateFiring` entirely, so a player caught mid-cycle always has something to swing.
  - **Audio**: `playShotgunPump` racks twice (two `triangle` clacks at 110->60Hz and 85->48Hz through narrow bandpasses, scheduled at +0.18s and +0.34s) so the pause reads as the gun cycling, not a dropped click. Lives inside `playShotgunBlast` rather than being engine-triggered — `viewKind` is the sound identity in `audio.ts`, and WebAudio scheduling off `ctx.currentTime` is sample-accurate where a frame-boundary trigger would jitter.
  - **`WEAPONS` joined `SIMULATION_BALANCE`** (widening `computeBalanceHash`'s values `number` -> `unknown`). Weapon tuning changes how a recorded input stream plays back while leaving `astHash` intact — exactly the silent-divergence class that guard exists for, and it was explicitly deferred in its own doc comment until "one of them is ever tuned". Folded the **whole table** in, not the two constants that moved, so future weapon edits need no list maintenance. Cost: it invalidates every pre-existing replay. Measured on the shipped board before regenerating — Casual 367 `fireQueued` frames / 117 closer than 0.15s, Gamer 324/107, **Pro 639/355 (median gap 0.058s)** — so those runs genuinely no longer reproduce, and refusing them is correct. **`defaultHighscore.ts` regenerated on the capped bot the same day**: Casual **47211**/11 levels, Gamer **55544**/11, Pro **56786**/12 — against the previous 48595/11, 50119/11, 69129/14. 86 generator checks passing, 0 failures, `PROFILES_HASH` `c7ae93fe` matching `profilesHash()`, still `gz1:`-compressed at 777KB (not the 84MB regression). **Pro's -17.9% and 14 -> 12 levels is the change's real cost showing up**, not noise on top of it: fights take ~14% longer for Pro, so it reaches the late campaign less often. Gamer's +10.8% is the same bimodality the previous regeneration documented (it has now come out 14/11/14/11/11 across five regenerations spanning 48774-64469) — the board is a max-over-qualifying-runs and remains a poor instrument for judging a code change.

- [x] **`defaultHighscore.ts` regenerated on the current bot, and the staleness guard is now ARMED (2026-08-01).** Board: Casual **48595**/11 levels, Gamer **50119**/11, Pro **69129**/14 — against the previous 46635/11, 64469/14, 52863/11. All three replays contiguous (12/12/15 segments), every segment carrying both hashes, worst level 17574 frames against the 21600 cap, 90 generator checks passing with no failures. `PROFILES_HASH` is baked in and matches `profilesHash()`, verified both ways: the test passes against the fresh file, and changing `ammoThrift` 0.2 -> 0.21 makes it fail with "re-run generate:default-highscore". **Pro's 69129 over 14 levels is the best Pro result ever recorded here** (previous best 66336, and that was before the Elite HP halving). **Gamer's -14350 is not a regression, and the whole board is a poor instrument for judging one.** The kept entry is a *maximum over 6 qualifying runs*, and Gamer's outcome is bimodal: it either gets through `stage12_render_engine.cpp` (the 2200 HP Elite level) and lands ~14 levels, or it does not and lands 11. Across four regenerations Gamer has now come out 14/11/14/11 spanning 48774-64469, and the split does not track code quality — run2 (before today's fixes) and this run (after a measured 12% combat improvement) both came out at 11. The evidence for the re-aim dodge is the n=20/side single-variable A/B, not this. Worth noting the improved bot did go deeper than any kept entry shows: one Gamer attempt reached **level 13** before getting stuck

- [x] **`defaultHighscore.ts` staleness is now detectable — but the guard ARMS at the next regeneration (2026-08-01).** The file is generated by playing the campaign with the bot's skill profiles, so any profile retune leaves it describing a bot that no longer exists — and nothing detected that: `astHash` covers the parsed source, `balanceHash` covers the enemy roster, neither knows anything about bot tuning, so every replay stays valid while the board silently misrepresents the bot. `profilesHash()` (`scripts/lib/profiles.mjs`) fingerprints all three profiles; the generator bakes it into the file it writes as `PROFILES_HASH`, and `profiles.test.mjs` recomputes and compares. Sorted keys, so reordering fields inside a profile (no behaviour change) does not trip it; profile *order* is preserved, because `curateMixedProfiles` reads key order as tier order. Verified by injecting the correct hash (passes), changing `ammoThrift` 0.2 -> 0.25 (fails with "re-run generate:default-highscore"), and restoring. **Currently in its unarmed state**: the shipped file predates the guard, so there is no baked hash to compare and the test takes a documented no-op path rather than failing — an assertion that cannot pass until an unrelated ~35-minute job runs would simply get disabled by whoever hit it. **It arms the moment `npm run generate:default-highscore` is next run**, which is already outstanding: 7 behaviour-changing commits have landed since the last regeneration, including the re-aim dodge (a ~12% combat swing), and the profiles themselves changed in Stage 2

- [x] **Dodging now works while re-aiming, and it is the biggest single combat win measured on this branch (2026-08-01).** User's observation, and it was right: *"just because the bot is dodging doesn't mean he should stop shooting — no human would stop shooting while dodging."* Reading the code showed the bot *does* keep firing while it dodges (the dodge block sits in the same `else` arm that sets `fire`), but there was exactly **one** dodge call site and it was in that fire branch — so a bolt was only ever evaded once the bot already had a firing solution (`|delta| <= fireAngleEps` **and** LOS). The module's own note puts the fire branch at "roughly 43% of combat decisions", so for the other ~57%, while re-aiming, the bot had **no evasion at all** and was simply a stationary target. Dodging was gated behind shooting. A directed dodge now also runs in the re-aim branch: it keeps the turn key (still converging on the shot) and adds the sidestep, and drops `KeyW` for the duration because forward + lateral engages `diagonalScale` and cuts the forward axis 29% — the 0%->72% regression mechanism. **Measured, single-variable (`DODGE_WHILE_REAIMING`), Gamer/normal, n=20/side:** `enemyAccuracy` **-12.2%** (0.564 -> 0.495), `dmg.enemyRangedPerSec` **-11.4%**, `dmg.enemyRanged` -13.8%, `minHealthReached` **+27.8%** (49.2 -> 62.9), `ttkNormal` -12.4%, `levelTimeSec` -2.7%, qualifyRate 18/20 -> 19/20 with level-1 deaths halved; guards pass and route metrics flat. **This corrects the Stage 3 conclusion.** "The dodge machinery may not be net-protective" was wrong — it is strongly protective, it was just unavailable in the majority of combat decisions, which is also why per-tier dodge *quality* tuning had so little to act on. **Distinct from the reverted "strafe only once aimed"**: that added *blind* oscillation to re-aiming and perturbed heading convergence every decision (-30pp qualifyRate); this triggers only on a genuinely inbound bolt. **Caveats:** measured on Gamer/normal only — wants the other combos before the full gate; and the `stall` anomaly rate rose sharply (24.9 -> 47.3 ticks/1k decisions, 9.0 -> 14.8 findings/run), which given every outcome metric improved is more likely the detector counting an actively-dodging bot as motionless (the same class as the spike-wait false positives) than a real wedge — worth checking with `activity`-tagged findings before trusting it either way

- [x] **Profile retune Stage 3 (per-profile evasion) MEASURED AND REVERTED — and it raises a prior question (2026-08-01).** Full write-up in `doc/dev/decisions.md`. Headline: promoting dodging/strafing to per-profile did **not** un-invert the two damage-avoidance axes, and `ttkNormal`'s smallest step fell 1.161x -> 1.118x, breaching its pre-registered 1.15x floor — revert, not tune forward, per the standing rule. The one thing that worked was the new deterministic `DODGE_REACTION_FLOOR_SEC` on the bottom tier: Casual's `enemyAccuracy` rose 11% (0.499 -> 0.556). **Root cause found by reading, and it corrects what this entry first said.** The initial reading — "dodging may not be net-protective" — was wrong. The bot *does* keep shooting while it dodges (the dodge block is inside the same `else` arm that sets `fire`). What it cannot do is dodge while **re-aiming**: there is exactly one dodge call site and it lives in the fire branch, so a bolt is only dodged when the bot already has a firing solution (`|delta| <= fireAngleEps` and LOS). The module's own comment puts the fire branch at "roughly 43% of combat decisions" — so in the other ~57% the bot has no evasion available at all and is simply a stationary target. Tuning dodge quality per tier therefore had almost nothing to act on. **Next experiment: allow the *directed* dodge in the re-aim branch.** Distinct from the already-reverted "strafe only once aimed" (that added *blind* oscillation to re-aiming and fought aim convergence every decision, -30pp qualifyRate); a directed dodge triggers only on a genuinely inbound bolt. Must drop `KeyW` while dodging — forward + lateral engages `diagonalScale` and cuts the forward axis 29%, the 0%->72% regression mechanism. **Open attribution gap I should close first:** Stage 2 was only measured at Tier-1 (n=8, 4 levels), so the ttk drop from the n=40 baseline's 1.161x cannot be cleanly split between Stage 2 and Stage 3. The committed state is Stage 2 and its Tier-2 number is unknown — a 3-profile n=20 run (~35 min) settles whether what is currently on the branch clears its own bar

- [x] **Profile retune Stage 2: knob signs fixed, ladder grader built (2026-08-01).** `ammoThrift` was 1.6/0.4/0.9 and `selfHarmAversion` 2.2/0.9/1.3 — both non-monotonic, i.e. Gamer was the *most* spendthrift and Pro the *more* self-harm-averse of the two. Now 1.6/0.4/**0.2** and 2.2/0.9/**0.5**, with Gamer anchored (it is pinned by `run-perf-benchmark.mjs`) and Pro moved, per the "widen both ends, hold the middle" decision. Casual also loses ghidra from `weaponPriority` — membership is a hard filter, so that is a real behavioural tier (a casual player never reaches for the rocket launcher) rather than a preference. All six ladder knobs are now strictly monotonic and pinned by `profiles.test.mjs`. **New grader:** `scripts/lib/profileSeparation.mjs` + `report-profile-separation.mjs`, reusing `abReport.mjs`'s `collectMetrics` so both reports share one metric definition; exits non-zero on a failing ladder. It bounds the **smallest adjacent step**, not the whole-ladder max/min — the distinction is the whole point, because Casual is cleanly separated from both others while **Gamer↔Pro** is the step that actually flipped run-to-run at n=5, and an ends-based bar cannot fail on that. Validated against the n=40 baseline, where it reproduces the known numbers and correctly fails. **Measured after Stage 2 (Tier-1: n=8, 4 levels — sign only, not a success declaration):** `ttkNormal` smallest step **1.161x → 1.317x** (Pro 0.755 → 0.614, i.e. `ammoThrift` 0.2 buying faster kills), `levelTimeSec` holding at 1.124x, and **both damage-avoidance axes still inverted** — as expected, since those are what the evasion axis in Stage 3 exists to fix. **Bug found and fixed in the process:** Stage 0's extraction used `export { PROFILES } from "./lib/profiles.mjs"`, which re-exports *without* creating a local binding — so `run-balancing-telemetry.mjs` itself crashed with `PROFILES is not defined` at startup while every importer of it still worked, and the unit suite stayed green because tests import `profiles.mjs` directly. Nothing ran the actual script. Caught only when the first Tier-1 run exited 1 three times

- [x] **Profile retune Stage 1 done; `rocketForDistantClusters` deliberately NOT deleted (2026-08-01).** The plan called for deleting both vestigial knobs. `coverageMode` is gone and that one was provably free: it was `false` for all three profiles, so `profile.coverageMode ? routeCoverage : routePlain` always evaluated to `routePlain` — a proof, not a measurement. It was also expensive: `planCoverageRoute` costs **1833ms against `planRoute`'s 538ms across the 17-level campaign, i.e. 77% of all route-planning time**, spent every `planLevels()` call on a route that was never read. `planCoverageRoute` itself stays in `routePlanner.mjs` (tested, real capability, now documented as having no live caller). **`rocketForDistantClusters` is deferred, because the plan's rationale for it is wrong.** The plan said deleting it hands rockets to the scoring loop "on their merits" — it does not. The knob gates one arm of a cluster bypass in `pickRangedWeapon`, and removing that arm falls through to `if (ownedWeapons.includes(1)) return shotgun` — *another* bypass, reached before the scoring loop. So for Gamer and Pro (both `true`) deleting it means rocket -> shotgun against a cluster at >=`ROCKET_CLUSTER_MIN_DIST` (5 tiles) whenever ghidra is owned with ammo and the aim is safe. That is a real behaviour change shipped under a cleanup label, and at 5+ tiles a shotgun is a poor substitute for a rocket. Options for whoever picks this up: (a) delete the whole cluster bypass so clusters really are score-selected — but `scoreRangedWeapon` scores a single target and has no cluster awareness, so that loses the behaviour rather than relocating it; (b) drop only the `profile.` condition so every tier uses rockets on distant clusters — changes Casual instead; (c) keep it and accept a knob that differentiates Casual only. Needs measuring, not a cleanup commit

- [x] **Stage 6 (pre-emptive engagement) measured and REVERTED (2026-08-01).** Full protocol: 4 combos (Casual/normal as a null control, Gamer/normal, Pro/normal, Pro/hard) x ATTEMPT_CAP=40 x 2 sides, ~3h, single-variable via `PREEMPTIVE_ENGAGE` against one dev server. **Guards passed on all four and all three stage-specific rollbacks were clear** (`combatVsExplorationRatio` max +2.4% against a +20% limit, `peakSimultaneousAggroed` never rose, `timeAtZeroRangedAmmoSec` 0 -> 0 everywhere) — and it still does not earn its place, because it changes nothing measurable. The null control is what makes that readable: Casual is gated off *and* had the switch off on the base side, so both its sides were identical code, and it still moved `enemyAccuracy` +5.2%, `minHealthReached` -29.5%, `stall` +14.4%, `trapSpike` +57.6%. Every feature-ON movement sits inside that. The decisive metric was `killsObserved`: control +0.2% against -1.0%/+0.3%/-0.6% on the three ON combos — if the bot were starting fights it otherwise skips, kills would rise, and they do not. **Then instrumented rather than assumed** (counters on the gate, 24 levels / 35,770 decisions): the gate opens on **0.99%** of decisions and is acted on in **0.66%**, ~10 per level. Root cause is structural and is the reason not to re-attempt this shape: the gate mirrors the engine's own aggro rule, so an eligible enemy is one the engine aggros on its next AI tick — a one-tick window by construction. Making it fire more means engaging outside the aggro radius, i.e. starting fights that would not have happened, contradicting the user's standing "don't run a corridor for an enemy that can't reach me" constraint. Full write-up in `doc/dev/decisions.md`. **Kept from the exercise:** the walking-distance gate proved load-bearing (rejected 27% of candidates, and 43 of 45 on level 7 — visible across a gap), `pickThreat` now receives `this.tuning` at every call site (an omitted argument would have made this A/B's own baseline run candidate behaviour), and Pro/normal stays in the A/B combo set

- [x] **`defaultHighscore.ts` regenerated once more, on the replay fix (2026-08-01).** Board: Casual **46635**/11 levels, Gamer **64469**/14, Pro **52863**/11. All three replays now verified contiguous by the new generator guard, 12/15/12 segments, every segment carrying both hashes, worst level 17085 frames against the 21600 cap. The failure path was exercised rather than assumed: this run logged one `[replay] "demo-campaign/stage10_kernel_module.rs" exceeded 21600 frames` **and** one matching truncation warning, i.e. an attempt did overflow and the new code truncated it instead of splicing — the previous behaviour would have shipped another silent gap. Scores remain noisy (Gamer 48774-64469 across five regenerations); nothing here is a bot regression

- [x] **The skipped replay level: the frame cap, plus a `filter` that hid it (2026-08-01).** Root cause of the Casual entry whose replay ran level 9 -> level 11. Two things had to line up. (1) `MAX_REPLAY_FRAMES_PER_LEVEL` is 21600 and the generator records at `RECORD_STEP_MS` = 1/60s, so the cap is **six minutes of a single level**; Casual is the slowest profile and `stage10_kernel_module.rs` one of the longest levels, so it overflows there intermittently, `LevelRecorder.record()` sets `overflowed` and `finish()` returns `null`. (2) `CampaignReplayRecorder.finish()` then ran `resolved.filter(l => l !== null)`, which **spliced the gap out of the middle of the sequence** — correct for the trailing-empty-level case it was written for, silently wrong for a middle one. Every surviving segment stayed individually valid (astHash, balanceHash, frames all fine), so nothing downstream could notice, and `startReplay` walks segments in order and simply jumped a level. **Why I first ruled the cap out, wrongly:** the overflow warning is a browser-side `console.warn`, and the highscore generator — unlike `run-balancing-telemetry.mjs` — never forwarded page console, so `grep exceeded` on the Node log returned 0. Proving it took adding that forwarding (now `CODEENSTEIN_CONSOLE_FORWARD=1`, same opt-in shape as the telemetry script), after which the log showed exactly one line: `[replay] "demo-campaign/stage10_kernel_module.rs" exceeded 21600 frames`, on precisely the file that goes missing. Two earlier hypotheses were disproven along the way and are worth not re-attempting: it is **not** a parse failure (`advanceToNextLevel` does silently skip a file whose `parseFile` returns null — a real latent hazard, since stage10 is the campaign's only `.rs` file — but the console shows zero `[parser] Skipping` lines and `[level] ... advancing to stage10_kernel_module.rs` firing on every attempt), and it is **not** `MAX_REPLAY_LEVELS` (that truncates the tail). **Fix:** `finish()` now truncates at the first unsavable level instead of filtering, so a payload is always a contiguous prefix of the run, and warns when it does. Plus a generator-side guard that asserts each kept entry's replay is a contiguous campaign prefix and refuses to write otherwise — verified against the shipped file, where it fails Casual with `got [... stage09, stage11, stage12]` and blocks the write. Both new unit tests confirmed red against the old `filter`

- [x] **`defaultHighscore.ts` regenerated after the bot fixes; the recorder bug found while doing it is now root-caused and fixed (2026-07-31, closed 2026-08-01 — see the entry above).** Regenerated on `f53de93` (nearest-key route ordering + loot-detour re-planning). Kept board: Casual **48884**/11 levels, Gamer **51945**/11, Pro **56347**/12 — against the previous 48076/11, 49506/11, 66336/14. **Do not read Pro's -15% as a regression:** the kept entry is a *maximum* over qualifying runs, and across four whole regenerations the same profile ranged 51059-61480 (11-12 levels) while Gamer ranged 48774-63089 (11-14). Run-to-run noise is comparable to the gap between any two boards, and the old Pro entry (66336/14) was itself a lucky tail. Generated with a larger sample than the default at the user's direction — new `CODEENSTEIN_HIGHSCORE_QUALIFYING_RUNS` env var (default unchanged at 3): the loop stops the moment N runs qualify, so at the default a profile used only **4 of its 40 permitted attempts** and the board was a best-of-3. At 6 each profile used 8 attempts. **Open bug found in the process:** the kept Casual entry's replay is missing its `stage10_kernel_module.rs` segment — 11 segments for the 12 levels the run actually played, so playback jumps from level 9 to level 11. It is specific to **Casual + stage10 and reproduced in 2 of 3 regenerations** (the third was clean, so it is luck, not determinism); Gamer and Pro record that level correctly every time. Not caused by today's changes. Playback is not broken — `startReplay` walks `payload.levels` sequentially and validates each segment's own `astHash`/`balanceHash`, all of which are present and correct — the level is simply absent. `ReplayRecorder.finish()` returns `null` both on frame-cap overflow and when a segment "never captured a single frame"; the cap is not it (zero `exceeded` warnings, worst level 14701 frames against a cap of 21600), so the zero-frame path is the suspect. Note stage10 is the level whose exit stays inert while an exit-room enemy lives, which used to fail ~58% of Casual attempts before `driveToExit` — probably the same neighbourhood

- [x] **more wall and floor variations, but each level should keep one styleset — DONE 2026-08-01.** Five stylesets (`stone`/`rust`/`tech`/`marble`/`techCool`), one per level, fixed for its duration; `stone` and `techCool` reproduce the old normal/bonus palettes exactly, so this adds three looks rather than re-theming. Varies wall/floor/door + ceiling + minimap wall color only — the five gameplay-signal flats (lore, acid, teleporter, spike safe/active) are built once and *shared by reference* across all stylesets, so "green means acid" can't drift per level. `GameMap.styleSet` is picked in `generation/styleSet.ts` from a **separate** `mulberry32` off `seedFrom(parsed)`, drawing **zero** values from the generator's own rng — exactness-gated in `mapGenerator.test.ts`, so no map layout, replay, or `defaultHighscore.ts` entry moved. Falls out for free: multiplayer guests regenerate the same styleset, and replay needs no new recorded field (it's a pure function of `(seedFrom(parsed), bonusLevel)`, both already available at playback). `TextureSet` lost `bonusWall`/`bonusFloor`; `renderScene`/`renderMinimap`/`renderExportMap` take a `LevelStyle` and every `map.bonusLevel ?` visual branch is gone. WAD side: `textureAllowlist.ts` is now grouped per styleset (20 slots, not 10), and a slot missing from a styleset's own list **borrows a sibling's** before dropping to programmer art — otherwise a sparse WAD makes the campaign flip between "real textures" and "defaults" level to level. Verified with new `npm run report:wad-stylesets` (styleset x slot matrix + per-candidate decode probe): all 5 stylesets resolve a **distinct wall and floor of their own on all 6 WADs** tested (5 catalog + a real Doom IWAD), zero dead allowlist names — `marble` needed `GRAY4`/`GRAYTALL` added, since shareware/HACX ship no `MARBLE*` and it was borrowing `stone`'s. `verify-wad-textures.mjs` gained a real-browser check that 8 campaign levels render ≥3 distinct palettes and that relaunching one renders pixel-identically. 2937 tests, coverage gate green.

- [x] **Loot detours now re-plan like route legs — the dominant wedge class in the scan (2026-07-31).** Root-caused by adding one field to the anomaly detail line: `activity`, which the trace already recorded and nothing printed. `branch` was useless (282 of 283 findings are `main`); `activity` split the same findings instantly — **9 of 13 idle stalls were `activity=loot`**, including both clusters I had previously attributed to door opening. The bot was never opening those doors: it walks *past* the doorway on a loot detour, and `maybeDetourForLoot` drove its waypoints with the bare straight-line `driveToward` while route legs had long since moved to `driveTowardWithReplan`. Shoved off the line, the straight line aims into the door frame for the whole 600-tick budget; the engine rejects the blocked axis and slides the free one, so **y stays pinned while x crawls** — `stage03_legacy_api.php` y=70.5 (door at y=71), x 23.53 -> 23.69 over 254 decisions. `driveTowardWithReplan`'s own doc comment describes this exact failure, captured at (38.8,56.3) on `stage06_pipeline.py`; the fix simply never reached this one call site. One-line change. **Measured across six scans:** the two clusters appear in **3 of 4 pre-fix runs** (4+6, 3+6, 4+3) and are **0 in both post-fix runs**; idle loot stalls **9 -> 0 -> 0**; qualifying **3/3 on all three combos throughout** (the guard that caught the previous attempt). Totals drift in both directions run to run (stalls 112-160 across runs) — the class-level numbers are the signal, not the totals. **Correction to the earlier entry above:** the door-staging observation is real and stays true — the staging point sits 0.15 tiles outside `openDoorAhead`'s reach for all four approach directions — but it was **not** the cause of these stalls, and the follow-up it proposed (verify the push opened the door, retry if not) was implemented and **measured to be a no-op**: across a full scan the retry path fired **zero** times, i.e. `getGrid` reported the door open after the first push every time. That hypothesis is dead; the code was reverted rather than kept as speculative recovery for a failure that does not occur

- [x] **"Door-approach" stalls were loot detours, and are fixed (2026-07-31).** **The door framing was wrong** — see the correction at the end of this entry. Chasing the residual idle stalls from the classification pass. **First correction: they are not 4 and 6 separate stalls, they are one continuous episode each.** The L3 findings are back-to-back decision ranges (1582-1658, 1659-1735, 1736-1812, 1813-1835) with **y pinned at 70.51** while x creeps +0.05 at a time; the detector split one ~254-decision episode into four because each 0.05 drift exceeded `TRACE_POS_EPS`. Same shape on L7 (492-661). Reading the four as independent events is what made them look like a frequency problem rather than one wedge. **Proven defect:** `openDoorAhead` (engine.ts) probes from the player's *exact* position `radius + 0.15 = 0.35` tiles along its facing, and the tile that probe lands in must BE the door tile; the bot's staging point is the plain centre of the tile in front of the door, **0.5 tiles out — 0.15 beyond the reach, for all four approach directions** (verified arithmetically for each). So the door has *never* opened on arrival at the staging point; it only ever opens because `holdForwardFine` walks the bot forward afterwards, which makes that nudge load-bearing rather than a safety margin. **Attempted fix, REVERTED:** move the staging point into the openable band. It regressed **qualifying runs 3/3 -> 0/3 on all three profiles** — the scan also ran 3.5x faster with 6x fewer findings, which is the tell, since fewer findings there meant a smaller denominator (runs dying early), not better behaviour. Cause of the regression is a genuine conflict: the band is only **0.15 tiles wide** (0.2 collision floor to 0.35 reach ceiling) while **one walk decision is 0.16 tiles**, and wall collision rejects an entire step rather than clamping it — so the bot cannot reliably land inside the band by coarse driving, and now got stranded *approaching* the staging point instead of at it. Revert verified: 3/3 restored. **Next step is the push, not the staging** — the coarse-approach-then-fine-push design is right (`holdForwardFine`'s doc comment already reasons about exactly this collision behaviour), so the fix belongs in making the push verifiably succeed: the bot now has `getGrid`/`getGridVersion` hooks, so it can *check whether the door actually opened* after pushing and retry, instead of assuming it did and walking into a replan loop when it didn't. Not attempted yet. **Still unexplained and worth reading first:** during the episode the bot drifts in **x while y stays constant**, i.e. it is holding forward while *not* facing the door — so `faceAngle` may not have converged, and note it falls through to full combat whenever `pickThreat` returns anything (this episode logged `threatDist=none` throughout, so that path alone does not explain it)

- [x] **The 70 "idle" stalls classified: ~72% were the anomaly detector calling correct spike-trap waiting a freeze (2026-07-31).** The scan report's honest-remainder figure (162 stalls, 92 with a threat in range = correct hold-and-fire, **70 idle**) turned out to be almost entirely a detector artifact. Three independent lines of evidence, none of them an aggregate over the detector's own findings: (1) **geometry** — 98.5% of idle stalls fall into just 14 repeat tiles, and **13 of those 14 sit immediately adjacent to a spike trap**; 11 of 14 freeze all three profiles at the same tile, which is map geometry, not tuning; (2) **tick-level trace** — 9 of 9 traced runs were **88-98% spike-blocked** with ~95% of ticks holding *no movement key at all*, i.e. the bot deliberately standing still; (3) **timing** — a trap is up for half its period, and the campaign's 45 traps give a blocked phase of **36-55 ticks**, which is exactly where the idle-stall durations land (median 34, p90 47, **max 55**). The bot waits one phase and never longer; it cannot do better without taking damage. **Mechanism:** the exclusion was `runSlice.every((r) => r.waitingOnSpike)`, but `waitingOnSpike` is computed from a probe 0.6 tiles ahead *along the current facing* (`combatPolicy.mjs:1698`), so it blinks off for a tick or two whenever the bot adjusts heading mid-wait — one such tick billed an entirely correct 34-tick wait as a freeze. Replaced with a dominance test at **0.8**, deliberately higher than the `mostlyFiring`/`mostlyEngaged` guards' 0.5 beside it: a bot genuinely *wedged* next to a trap measures ~50% (the trap keeps cycling underneath it), while a real one-phase wait never dilutes below ~0.88, so the high bar is what preserves the detector's ability to still catch a wedge next to a spike — pinned by a test both ways. Matched re-scan: **idle stalls 68 -> 19**. Caveat stated plainly: the two scans are separate stochastic runs (threat-present stalls moved 107 -> 141 on variance alone), so the count is directional, not exact — the tick-level and timing evidence is what carries the conclusion. Also fixed the trace dump to emit the longest run of *each* finding type per level rather than the longest overall, which is why only 1 of 5 idle stalls had rows on the first attempt. **The honest remainder is now ~2 real clusters, and they are a new class: door-approach stalls.** `stage03_legacy_api.php` (23.6,70.5) freezes **77 ticks, four times, identically** — above the 55-tick spike ceiling, nearest spike 12.9 tiles away, and **0.78 tiles from locked door (23,71)**; `stage07_service.rb` (70.6,22.5) freezes 38 ticks six times, one tile below branch door (70,21). Both Pro/normal in this sample. Not investigated further — this is the next thing to pull on, and it is a far smaller and better-defined target than "70 idle stalls"

- [x] **Level 3's key/door chain shortened — the level design was never the problem, the bot's pickup ordering was (2026-07-31).** The scan report flagged `stage03_legacy_api.php` as the campaign's distorting outlier (153-188s vs 29-103s, 24% of all findings, only 23 enemies) and recommended shortening the chain. Measuring first said otherwise. Level 6 has **7 doors and 7 keys and plans in a single leg** at 1.05x its shortest path, while level 3 has 8 door tiles / 4 gates and plans 13 legs at 3.73x — so gate *count* is not the cost. The cost was the order: `planRoute` collects every reachable key before opening the next gate, and it took them in `map.keys` array order, which is `placeKeys`' push order (gate-opening order) and says nothing about where the player stands. On level 3 that walked 94 tiles west to one key, **85 tiles back east to a key sitting 7 tiles from spawn**, then 114 west again to the first gate — 293 waypoints to do about 130 waypoints of work, all before the first door opened. `routePlanner.mjs`'s own doc comment had claimed "nearest reachable uncollected key" since it was written; only now is that true. Safe by construction: since the branch runs until no reachable key is left, the *set* collected before each gate is identical either way, so solvability cannot change — unlike the **door** order, which stays array-order deliberately (tile-order scanning deadlocked `stage13_batch_job.scala`, see `findReachableDoor`) and is now pinned by a test. Measured over the full 17-level campaign, planned route length: **level 3 529 -> 401 waypoints (-24%, 3.73x -> 2.82x)**, level 10 538 -> 426 (-21%), and **level 17 `the_monolith` 4610 -> 2924 (-37%)** — 51 keys, the biggest win and not something the report had flagged. Total **-25.3%**; every other level byte-identical, no plan failures, no `crossesHazard` changes, deterministic across repeated runs. First unit tests `routePlanner.mjs` has ever had (`routePlanner.test.mjs`, 4 tests, CI-wired via the `scripts/**.test.mjs` precedent); the nearest-key test was confirmed red against the pre-fix planner (`expected '10,1' to be '3,1'`), the other three pin invariants that must *not* move. **Route length is measured exactly; wall-clock level time is not** — that needs a `balancing:scan` to confirm, since level time also carries combat and stalls. An earlier attempt at the report's literal recommendation — biasing key *placement* in `src/map/generation/doorsKeys.ts` toward the gate each key opens — was measured and **reverted**: it moved level 3 only -2.6% and made level 7 **24% worse**, because placing a key near its gate ignores which side the player approaches from

- [x] **Pro/normal level-1 wedge root-caused and fixed (2026-07-31).** ~22s frozen at `(60.26,57.35)` in roughly 2 of 5 attempts. **Mechanism:** `#walkPathTo` BFSes with `LOOT_DETOUR_AVOID_TILES` (spikes + acid). From that tile on `main.c` the exit is **95 tiles away by plain BFS but NULL once spikes and acid are excluded** — every route crosses one. A null BFS returns `{state:"playing", reason:"stuck"}`, which `driveToExit` does *not* treat as fatal, so it fell through to `driveToward` — which steers a straight line with no pathfinding — and sprinted into a wall for the full `FINAL_APPROACH_TICKS` (80) budget, `EXIT_CLEAR_ROUNDS` (6) times over. 6x80=480 ticks, matching the 438-halt observed. The trace is unmistakable: 438 identical decisions, `delta=+0.001` (perfectly aimed), `navDist=52.74` (the exit, straight-line), `keys=[KeyW,ShiftLeft]`, position never changing. **Fix:** `pathAvoidingHazardsIfPossible` — strict avoid-set first, then retry allowing hazard terrain, the same two-tier shape `driveTowardWithReplan` already uses for doors. Refusing to plan is only correct when a better plan exists; that is true for an *optional* loot detour and false for reaching the exit. **Measured, n=16 Pro/normal attempts, single-variable via `BOT_HAZARD_ROUTE_FALLBACK`:** long stalls (>=200 ticks) **4 -> 0**, longest stall **441 -> 61**, exit-inert rounds **24 -> 4** (without the fix `driveToExit` burns all six rounds every time; with it the first resolves). The fallback fired 4 times in the fix-on run — i.e. the path under test actually executed. Also extended `TRACE_DUMP` to dump the longest finding of *any* type, not just oscillation: this wedge is a `stall`, so the oscillation-only dump captured nothing of it

- [x] **Balancing scan + highscore regeneration after the bot rework (2026-07-31 04:22).** Report: https://claude.ai/code/artifact/c21742a1-6d52-4dc1-9778-e19359a8adf9 — findings, interpretation and recommendations, incl. the profile-intent analysis. Scan at `8ce3f4b`: 233 findings (162 stall / 64 healthDrainFrozen / 7 oscillation), no crashes, no new anomaly class; Casual 5/5, Gamer 4/5, Pro 2/5 qualifying. **Headline: Pro/normal freezes ~22s at a fixed tile on level 1 in 2 of 5 attempts** — reproduced with all four of today's switches *disabled*, so it is pre-existing, not a regression; it went unseen because every A/B today ran Pro on *hard*. Level 3 remains the campaign's distorting outlier (153-188s vs 29-103s, 24% of all findings, only 23 enemies — the time is traversal). **Profiles: Casual is cleanly differentiated; Gamer and Pro have collapsed together**, and Pro is *worse* on TTK (0.95 vs 0.92) and route overhead (1.817 vs 1.799). Likely cause is ours: `rotSpeedMultiplier` (2/3.5/5) was a sharp differentiator *because the bot stood still to turn*, and since `ee7a658` it no longer does — the stop-to-turn A/B shows the compression directly (Casual gained 22.4% level time, Pro only 8.3%). Suggested: widen `fireCooldownMs`/`fireAngleEps`/`healthDetourThreshold` between Gamer and Pro instead of turn speed, and check whether Pro's `healthDetourThreshold: 0.25` is still justified. Caveat throughout: n=5, one difficulty — ordering is visible, tuning values are not

- [x] **`driveToExit` ranks exit blockers by walking distance now, and the combined "act on accurate information" A/B is settled (2026-07-31).** `driveToExit` sorted exit-room enemies by `Math.hypot` and hunted the nearest, so an enemy 5 tiles away through a wall (20 by corridor) was hunted before one 8 tiles down an open corridor — with a bounded per-round budget, that could burn the budget and report `stuck`. Replaced by `rankExitBlockers`, a pure exported helper ranking on BFS length over the now-live grid, using the same avoid-set `#walkPathTo` actually walks with; unreachable blockers sort *last* rather than being dropped (the exit stays inert while any homed enemy lives, so discarding one turns a hunt into a silent `stuck`), index breaks ties. **Combined A/B** (this + `BOT_LIVE_GRID`, n=12/combo/side): `qualifyRate` **+8.3pp on 2 of 3** (0.917 -> 1.000, flat on Pro), `ttkNormal` better 3/3, guards pass 3/3 — against `routeFollowOverhead`/`levelTimeSec` **up 3/3** (+2.5/+9.7/+6.4%). Coverage, kills and secret rooms all flat, so it is not visiting more of the map. **Attributed with `summarizeActivityDistance` rather than another A/B:** of the extra distance, **53% is `loot`** (+5.5t/level — pickups it previously believed were walled off; `pctRegularKillLootMisses` fell 5.2%/21.4% on Casual/Gamer), 41% is `route` (+1.8%, no mechanism identified — the honest open bit), and **`exitHunt` went 0.1 -> 0.0 t/level, i.e. the blocker change costs nothing measurable** — which isolates the cost to `BOT_LIVE_GRID` for free. Kept on that basis: the dominant cost is the bot succeeding at retrieval it used to fail, and the blocker fix is free. Magnitude is noisy between runs (+9.7% in the A/B vs +3.4% in the attribution run, same combo, same sign) — the attribution is trustworthy, the size is not

- [x] **Bot now sees the engine's live wall grid — the stale-door class is fixed at the source (2026-07-31).** The engine mutates its own grid mid-level (a door opens, a secret wall floods away) and bumps `gridVersion`; the bot planned forever against the *copy* it was handed at level start, so an opened door stayed a wall in every BFS it ran. That is the recorded `stage03_legacy_api.php` wedge, and `#noteDoorUnderFoot` exists only as a partial workaround — it can learn about a door the player personally stands on, never one that opened as a side effect or that a *teammate* opened. Found by auditing the engine's capability surface against what the bot actually calls: `PathField` (`src/engine/pathField.ts`) has been maintaining a live, grid-mutation-aware BFS distance field the whole time, and `scripts/lib/` had **zero** references to `gridVersion` or any grid hook. New `getGridVersion()`/`getGrid()` test hooks (grid copied row-by-row, never a live ref — `PathField` floods over that exact array); the bot folds the version into the read it already makes each decision and refetches the grid only when it moves. Verified end to end rather than by outcome alone: a unit test drives a real branch-door open and asserts the version bumps and the tile flips through the hooks, and a play run logged **8 grid refreshes** — i.e. the path actually executed. Gated by `BOT_LIVE_GRID` so it stays single-variable A/B-able. **Behaviourally unmeasured so far** — it changes what the bot's pathfinding believes, so it wants an A/B before being called a win. Unblocks the two walking-distance items below

- [x] **Stage 7 (backpedal retreat) shipped 2026-07-31 — the last movement stage of the rework plan.** `criticalHealth` used to spin to face away from a threat while holding `KeyW`, so for the first half of a large turn it *ran toward the thing chasing it* — the identical defect measured in the mine retreat. It now runs along the escape vector immediately via the 8-way pick, no turn, which also keeps the threat in view (a precondition for firing while retreating; firing itself deliberately left out as a separate variable). Full protocol, n=12/combo/side, single-variable via `NAV_BACKPEDAL_RETREAT`: `enemyAccuracy` **-3.5/-5.3/-3.6%** and `dmg.enemyRanged` **-4.9/-1.2/-2.9%** on 3/3 — both metrics the change targets; `qualifyRate` 1.000/**0.917->1.000**/1.000, and **zero deaths on the candidate side of all three combos** (Gamer's lone level-1 death, worth its whole 8.3pp, disappeared). Guards pass 3/3 and the stage's own tightened rollback (qualifyRate down >8pp, conditionalDeathRate up >10pp) is not breached anywhere. **Costs, honestly:** `ttkNormal` worse on 3/3 (+1.5/+4.8/+5.2%), plausibly because a bot that disengages properly stretches fights out — arguably correct at critical health, but a real cost. `minHealthReached` mixed (Casual -21.1% against Gamer +31.5%, Pro +15.1%) and weighted weakly: it is a *minimum*, so one unlucky run moves it, and Casual had zero deaths on both sides. On `diagonalScale`: the 72% regression came from bolting a lateral key onto a *forward* move, leaving the resultant 45deg off the escape line at 71% efficiency; here the octant is chosen to point along the escape vector, so worst case is half an octant (22.5deg, 92%), and a pure-lateral escape has no `diagonalScale` factor at all

- [x] **Bot stop-to-turn removed — the biggest measured win of the rework (2026-07-31).** The bot has always had full WASD and only ever walked forwards: at every route corner past `MAX_WALK_WHILE_TURNING_RAD` (0.35 rad) it added *only* a turn key and stood still until the heading converged. `planRoute` emits one waypoint per tile, so a winding corridor presents that every tile — measured **385 corners across the 8 demo levels, 1585 frozen decisions, 79.3s, ~13.4% of level time** (23.5% of all decisions were turn-only). **The enabling fact, which nobody had checked:** `moveForward` translates along `(dirX,dirY)` and `strafe` along `(-dirY,dirX)`, both scaled by the same `step`, with `diagonalScale` (1/sqrt(2)) applied per axis when both are held — so two perpendicular components have magnitude *exactly* `step`. Eight directions, all at full speed, sprint included, no turn required; worst-case direction error is half an octant (22.5deg, cos = 92%) versus 0% while frozen. Now picks the 8-way combination pointing at the target and keeps moving while it turns; safety checks scan along the **movement** vector rather than facing (`movementVectorFor`), which they never had to before. Full protocol, n=12/combo/side, single-variable via `CODEENSTEIN_TELEMETRY_TUNING='{"NAV_FULL_WASD":false}'`:

| combo | levelTimeSec | routeFollowOverhead | qualifyRate | minHealthReached | guards |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Casual/normal | **-22.4%** | -1.1% | 1.000 -> 1.000 | +4.3% | pass |
| Gamer/normal | **-15.5%** | -4.4% | 0.917 -> 0.917 | +2.6% | pass |
| Pro/hard | **-8.3%** | +0.7% | 0.917 -> **1.000** | +315% (9.1 -> 38.0) | pass |

Frozen decisions 23.5% -> 1.4%; `healthDrainFrozen` **halved on all three** (-48 to -51%) — that anomaly largely *was* the freeze, the bot standing motionless while taking damage. `enemyAccuracy` down on all three, `ttkNormal` down or flat. **Costs, small but consistently signed:** hazard damage up on 3/3 (0.076->0.290, 0.031->0.050, 0->0.045) — the predicted risk of strafing into terrain a facing-scan would not have caught; absolutes are a fraction of a HP per level and `minHealthReached` rose everywhere, so it is not costing survival, but the direction is consistent. `stall` ticks up on 2 of 3 (+28/+18/-3.5%). Oscillation moves are noise at 0.5-1.0 findings/run. **Every pacing number this bot has ever reported was inflated by the standing around** — treat pre-2026-07-31 `levelTimeSec` and `combatVsExplorationRatio` as biased

- [x] **Bot: the "oscillation" was mostly a bad detector, not a bot defect — diagnosis CORRECTED 2026-07-30. The recorded atan2 branch-cut root cause below is WRONG; read this first.** Reading one run's raw per-decision trace end to end (new `CODEENSTEIN_TELEMETRY_TRACE_DUMP=1`, plus `delta`/`navDist` on every trace record) contradicts the old diagnosis on every point:
  - **The sign does not flip every decision.** Measured across whole runs it flips on **14%** of decisions. The "flips decision to decision" claim came from a hand-picked five-line excerpt.
  - **The turn machinery is flawless.** A real captured turn: `+2.729 -> +2.274 -> +1.819 -> +1.364 -> +0.909 -> +0.454 -> 0.000` — exactly -0.455 rad per decision (50ms x 9.1 rad/s), landing dead on target. No overshoot, no branch-cut artifact. (The "overshoot" hypothesis floated in the attempt-2 revert is also wrong: all 44 `implausible rotation` warnings across six A/B runs are the *known* combat stall-strafe widening — computed burst 0.52ms, actual hold 50.0ms, 44/44 — which is rare and deliberately kept.)
  - **What the findings actually were:** (a) combat preempting navigation — the bot turns away from its waypoint to fight, gets strafed off course (`navDist` 0.24 -> 2.22), returns, re-engages (29-38% of ticks in 4 of 7 dumped runs); and (b) the bot's **ordinary gait** — `planRoute` emits one waypoint per tile, each corridor corner presents a 1.5-2.7 rad bearing change, and `MAX_WALK_WHILE_TURNING_RAD` (0.35) forbids holding `KeyW` above that, so the bot stops dead, turns in place for 3-6 decisions, sprints one tile, repeats. **28% of ticks inside flagged runs were frozen mid-turn.**
  - **Why both fixes "failed":** they were graded on a metric that was largely counting normal walking. Each slightly lengthened convergence, so more ticks accrued per turn and *more* windows qualified. That is the whole mystery of "two opposite interventions both made it worse".
  - **Fixed by** a route-progress exemption in `detectOscillation` (`6315051`): a run in which the bot reached >=2 nav targets is progress, not thrashing. Matched scan: **38 -> 4 findings**, other detectors unaffected (150 stall/healthDrainFrozen). Three of the four survivors cluster at one spot on `stage03_legacy_api.php` — (8.50,68.54), (8.18,68.44), (8.20,68.42) across separate attempts, `|delta|` flat at ~1.19, `navDist` stuck at ~1.2t, never arriving — a real localized wedge in the same y=68 corridor an earlier session hit, previously buried under 34 findings of ordinary walking.
  - **Open, and now the actual candidates:** (1) that level-3 wedge, which the detector finally isolates; (2) the stop-to-turn cost — the bot halts at nearly every tile boundary, so turning while moving is a real efficiency prize, though Stage 3's per-key hold durations tried something adjacent and was reverted for costing accuracy, so it needs the full A/B rather than enthusiasm.

- [x] **(superseded by the entry above, kept for its measurements) The bot can never aim: its smallest possible turn is 3-22x its own fire tolerance.** The wedges were the symptom; this is the mechanism, and it is general — curl only supplies the enemy density to hit it often.
  - **The numbers.** A decision is `VIRTUAL_STEP_MS` 50ms and `ENGINE_ROT_SPEED` is 2.6 rad/s, so the *smallest* turn a profile can make is `2.6 x rotSpeedMultiplier x 0.05`:

    | profile | rotMult | smallest turn | `fireAngleEps` | overshoot | nav-warns/invocation |
    |---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
    | Casual | 2.0 | 0.260 rad | 0.08 | **3x** | **0** |
    | Gamer | 3.5 | 0.455 rad | 0.05 | **9x** | 135-205 |
    | Pro | 5.0 | 0.650 rad | 0.03 | **22x** | 186-273 |

    So no profile can settle its aim by turning — it overshoots, corrects, overshoots back. It fires only when a *moving* enemy drifts through alignment by chance, which is why the bot uses ~18% of its available fire rate on the levels it loses.
  - **The instrument is blind to the worst-affected case.** `#checkRotationAnomaly` warns on `actual > Math.max(expectedMax, 0.3)`. Casual's full-window turn is **0.260 rad**, 0.04 under that floor — so it logs *zero* nav-warns while still overshooting its own tolerance 3x. Reading "Casual is clean" off the warn count is wrong, and that reading is what makes this look like a fast-profile problem.
  - **Root cause is one duration serving two keys.** `uniformIntent` assigns every key the same hold, and the combat branch then does `turnBurst = Math.max(turnBurst, moveBurstMs(10, false, ...))` — widening the *turn* to a movement-sized burst so the stall-strafe actually moves. `turnBurstMs` itself is correct; it computes 3ms and something else throws that away.
  - **The 2026-07-29 A/B tested the wrong fix, and its own comment says why.** Removing the widening killed the overshoot outright (8.3x -> 1.0x, nav-warns 15 -> 0) but raised `enemyAccuracy` on all three combos and dropped Gamer/normal `qualifyRate` 15pp, breaching a pre-registered guard — because without it the strafe key inherits a turn-sized burst and moves the bot ~0.02 tiles, so the bot stops dodging. That A/B could only trade aiming against dodging **because both ride one field**. It never tested decoupling them.
  - **The fix is already built and inert.** `segmentsFor` (`combatPolicy.mjs:1416`) splits per-key holds into dispatch phases and is fully wired into `applyAction` (`bot.mjs:1927`); `minPhaseMs` is **0** for the single-player bot, so a 3ms phase lands exactly. Its own doc comment: *"Today this always returns exactly one phase, because every branch still gives all its keys the same duration."* Give the turn key `turnBurstMs(delta, ...)` and the movement/strafe keys `moveBurstMs(...)`, and it emits two phases — turn ends early (no overshoot), strafe continues (dodging intact). No new mechanism, only per-key holds at the call sites.
  - **Re-run the 2026-07-29 guard**, since that is the experiment this would overturn: `enemyAccuracy` must not rise and Gamer/normal `qualifyRate` must not fall. Expect nav-warns to go to zero *and* strafe distance to hold — if strafe distance collapses, the phases are not landing.
  - **Also missing, and cheap on its own: threats have no give-up.** Mines abandon a target after `MINE_TARGET_GIVEUP_TICKS` 40; a threat gets `COMBAT_STALL_TICKS_THRESHOLD` 40 -> a strafe and nothing else, forever. Worse, `combatStallTicks` resets whenever position changes by 0.01 (`posKey` is 2 decimals), so the strafe it triggers resets the counter that triggered it. `pickThreat` also filters on `alive && aggroed && dist < engageRadius` with `visible` used only as a *sort* key — so an aggroed enemy behind a wall is selected as the threat and the bot stops routing to fight something it cannot see, hit, or be hit by (`hpFrac 1.00 -> 1.00` for thousands of decisions).
  - **Wedge shape, for recognising it again**: `branch=main`, `hpFrac` flat, position jittering ~0.05-0.1 tiles, `activity=route`/`route+replan`, a threat held at constant distance. Rates: curl **2.0%** of level-visits (26), wolf3d 0.29%, vim 0.21%, laravel 0.15%. By profile Casual 6 / Gamer 12 / Pro 15; by difficulty **normal 31 / hard 2** — on hard the bot dies before it can lock up.

- [x] **Bot: weapon-selection A/B re-run, and a deliberate decision to keep it despite a breached guard.** Re-ran the matched-scale A/B against the *current* melee rules (baseline `98c0e63` vs candidate `fbc86f7`, n=20/combo/side, three machines, each combo's two sides on the same host). The win reproduces almost exactly: `avgTtkByCategory.normal` **-30 to -38% on all three combos** across two independent runs (Casual -38.0%, Gamer -38.4%, Pro -30.5%), with `combatVsExplorationRatio` -10 to -19% and `levelTimeSec` flat. The cost is also real: pooled across both runs, **10 candidate failures vs 1 baseline** (110/120 vs 119/120, Fisher p ~= 0.006), *every* candidate failure `died@L1`, and Gamer/normal breached the pre-registered -15pp `qualifyRate` guard at -20pp. **Kept anyway, on the user's call, and the reasoning matters more than the threshold:** that guard was registered to catch "the bot can no longer do its job", and an ~8% level-1 failure rate does not do that — 92% of attempts still qualify and failed attempts are excluded from the aggregates, so the denominator stays healthy. Weigh the two error types by what they do to the *measurement* instead: systematic weapon mismanagement biases `avgTtk`/`enemyAccuracy`/`damageBySource`/`combatVsExploration` in **every** run, which is the original "the bot's play quality is a measurement problem" complaint; occasional death discards ~8% of runs and biases nothing. A bot that never dies on level 1 in 120 attempts while killing 35% slower than it should is *less* human-like, not more. **Open follow-up (upside, not a blocker):** the deaths are concentrated at level 1 rather than scattered across the campaign, which natural risk-taking would not do — that concentration suggests a specific behavioural flaw still to find. Note also a measurement inconsistency I have not explained: a 60-attempt `LEVEL_LIMIT=1` run of the same code showed **0** level-1 deaths, against 10/120 in the A/B; I over-trusted the smaller sample once already, so use `VERBOSE=1` at matched settings to get `logDeathDetail`'s fatal cause before trusting either

- [x] **Bot: the A/B's -5pp `qualifyRate` chased down to melee-driven mine deaths, and melee narrowed to "never sought out".** The weapon-selection A/B passed its guards but dropped `qualifyRate` exactly -5.0pp on all three profiles, always a single level-1 death out of 20 — too uniform across three independent runs on three machines to dismiss. Root cause: `shouldCloseToMelee`'s `oneSwingKills` clause. Level 1 (`main.c`) has **27 of its 32 enemies at <=15 HP** (all Edge Cases) and **6 mines**, so "walk up to a target one swing kills" fired for nearly every enemy on the level and turned it into ~27 blind approaches across a minefield. Every death was `fatal=trapMine` with 111-140 mine damage while the knife did all the killing. Measured at 60 level-1 attempts per condition, which is where this got interesting — two "fixes" made it *worse*, and the intuitive rule made it worse still: **swing only at what is already in reach, never approach = 0/60** (matches baseline); walk 3.5t = 2/60; walk 2.0t plus a spotted-mine check = 4/60; **never melee while any ammo remains = 3/60**. The spotted-mine guard cannot help because the mines that kill are *unspotted*, and the bot must not route around what it has not seen. And the strict "melee is always last" rule dies with the knife never drawn, for a reason `destroyMine` (engine.ts) states outright: a mine hit by gunfire detonates and "shooting one at point-blank still hurts whoever's close" — the shooter eats `mineDamageAt` its own position, so firing at contact range detonates mines onto the bot. In-reach melee is therefore load-bearing, not a preference. Shipped: melee is never *sought* (no approach at any range, for any target) but is still taken against a threat already in reach; `visibleMineNear` now guards the final closing step alongside the existing hazard/spike checks; `MELEE_APPROACH_RADIUS` deleted as dead. **Caveat:** the passing A/B (`ttkNormal` -34 to -42%) was measured with the older melee rules — the ranged-selection work is untouched and is where that win came from, but the melee half now differs, so re-run the A/B before treating those numbers as describing current behaviour

- [x] **Bot: weapon choice now weighs reach, ammo economy and self-harm instead of walking a fixed list.** `pickRangedWeapon` used to return the first owned weapon with ammo from `profile.weaponPriority` — no notion of how far a weapon reaches, what a kill costs in a finite reserve, or that a rocket can kill *you*. Replaced with a cost-in-seconds score: `killSec + ammoThrift x (ammoBurned / reserveHeld) x AMMO_BURN_PENALTY_SEC + selfHarmSec`, with `weaponPriority` kept as the tiebreak so profile personality still decides between economically-equivalent options. Per-profile knobs: `ammoThrift` (Casual 1.6 thrifty, Gamer 0.4 spendthrift, Pro 0.9) and `selfHarmAversion` (Casual 2.2, Pro 1.3, Gamer 0.9 — restoring the caution the old ghidra-last ordering encoded implicitly). **Range awareness was missing entirely** and is now modelled on the engine's own ballistics rather than a guess: Friday Hotfix's hard `maxRange` 3.5 (measured firing 74 shots for 16 hits and *zero* kills at 5 tiles before this), the Cone of Fire's `(dist/FOG_FAR)^3 x weapon.maxConeDeviationPx` cubic falloff (which is why gdb, at 20px vs the shared 38px, stays usable far out), pellet spread, and the target's own on-screen size (elites project 1.5x, Edge Cases 0.55x). **Melee** is now chosen as a last resort (every ranged weapon dry — previously the bot stood pulling a trigger that did nothing) or against a target a single swing kills within 3.5 tiles, since an Edge Case is 10-15 HP and melee also returns lifesteal. **Rockets** never fire at an Edge Case at all (a 150-damage round on a "tiny annoyance" is pure waste), and `rocketAimUnsafe` now accounts for how far a chaser closes during the rocket's ~1s flight — a "safe" 5-tile shot detonates a tile away against a 3.74 t/s Edge Case. **A/B'd 2026-07-29/30 and kept — guards pass on all three combos.** Matched scale (n=20/combo/side, `QUALIFYING_TARGET=999`, symmetric flags), baseline `98c0e63` vs candidate `65dc3f8` so the number-key fix is present on *both* sides and only the economics/melee/range change is under test. Run across three machines with each combo's two sides on the *same* host, so host speed can't confound `levelTimeSec`. **`avgTtkByCategory.normal` fell 34-42% on every profile** (Casual 1.885 -> 1.181, Gamer 1.438 -> 0.841, Pro 1.653 -> 1.093) with `combatVsExplorationRatio` down ~20% across the board and `levelTimeSec` flat (-2.0/-1.4/+0.1%) — the bot kills much faster without trading level time for it. Costs, recorded honestly: `enemyAccuracy` rose on Gamer (+9.6%) and Pro (+10.8%) though it fell slightly on Casual, and Gamer's `minHealthReached` dropped 35%; `damageBySource.enemyRanged` moved only +6-8% against a 34-42% faster kill, so the bot takes more hits per fight but fights far less. `trapSpike`/`hazard` percentages look alarming (+94/+126/+81%) but are 0.2 -> 0.5 absolute, which is exactly what `RELATIVE_CHANGE_FLOORS` exists to flag. **Open oddity:** `qualifyRate` fell exactly -5.0pp on all three profiles, always a single level-1 death out of 20 — identical across three independent runs on three different machines is a poor fit for noise and wants investigating before the next campaign. Separately, a full-campaign check showed the arsenal genuinely in use for the first time (pistol 350 -> 39 shots, gdb/Friday fired at all, Toolchain kills 77 -> 107) but **level 12 is unchanged** at 12/12 deaths — that wall is not a weapon-selection problem

- [x] **Bot: every weapon past the knife was mis-selected — asking for gdb equipped the rocket launcher.** `bot.mjs`/`multiplayerBot.mjs` dispatched `Digit${weaponIndex + 1}`, assuming number keys index the raw `WEAPONS` array. The engine reads a digit as an index into `NUMBER_KEY_WEAPONS`, which excludes melee weapons (knife *and* Toolchain both have `meleeRange: 1.5`), so the real mapping is `[pistol, shotgun, gdb, ghidra, friday]`. The bot therefore got: gdb -> **ghidra**, ghidra -> **Friday Hotfix**, Friday -> **a digit with no weapon behind it at all** (a silent no-op leaving whatever was in hand). Introduced when the weapon hotkeys were made contiguous (notes Task 56, which correctly updated the *engine* and `doc/user/mechanics.md` but not the bot). Consequence: whenever the bot wanted its SMG it equipped a rocket launcher and fired it at whatever range it was standing at — measured as 84-99 damage of `selfRocket` per run, once as the fatal blow, against a baseline of **0.0 across an entire 12-attempt campaign**. Hidden until now because Casual's `weaponPriority` starts with the pistol (`Digit1`, correct) and the old fixed-priority walk returned the first weapon with ammo, so Casual asked for anything else only rarely — **but Gamer leads with gdb and Pro with ghidra, so both have been mis-equipping for their entire measured history**, including the 2026-07-24 Hard campaign whose headline finding was that the aggressive Gamer profile "dies hardest". Fixed with `numberKeyCodeFor()` mirroring the engine's own derived mapping; both bots now resolve the digit in Node and pass the key code into the browser rather than computing it there. Three regression tests, including one asserting gdb and ghidra can never resolve to the same key

- [x] **Bot telemetry: the rotation warning now says where the turn came from.** `[nav-warn] implausible rotation` reported only the burst `turnBurstMs` *recorded*, which is not the hold actually dispatched — several branches widen `turnBurst` afterwards, so the line read as physically impossible ("turned 0.45rad, requested 0ms") and cost real time to explain twice. It now carries the computed turn *and* the hold the key really got, plus `origin` (`tick` vs `faceAngle`), the branch, which turn key, the decision length, and elapsed sim time since the check was recorded. That last field matters because `faceAngle` drives `applyAction` in a loop and never calls the detector itself, so a check it records survives until some later `tick()` — an inflated `actual` can be an artifact of that gap rather than a real over-rotation. Example output: `turned 0.46rad in one decision (computed turn 6.0ms, key actually held 50.0ms, expected <=0.22rad) [origin=tick branch=main turnKey=KeyQ decision=50ms elapsedSim=0ms]` — the cause is now legible at a glance. Pure diagnostics, never read back by decision logic

- [x] **Bot rework: the telemetry now measures the game, not the bot.** Five stages shipped, two attempted and reverted. Sprinting straight legs cut level times 27-41%; strafing while firing and then dodging actual inbound bolts cut ranged damage per second of exposure ~25% and took `enemyAccuracy` from ~0.63 to 0.49-0.63. The decision core moved to `scripts/lib/combatPolicy.mjs` (first automated tests `scripts/lib/` has ever had, 123 of them, CI-wired) and is shaped to be lifted into `src/engine/` later as a deathmatch opponent. New `scripts/report-balancing-ab.mjs` diffs two telemetry runs and fails on a survival regression; new `combatPacing.levelTimeSec`, `navigationMapFlow.distanceTraveled` and `dmg.enemyRangedPerSec` (both raw fields were computed and thrown away before). **Validated on levels 1-8 only** — every A/B ran `LEVEL_LIMIT=8`, so levels 9-17 were never exercised by any of it. **Two reverts worth more than the wins:** per-key hold durations (so movement outlasts a turn) made the bot *slower* and less accurate — the turn-truncates-the-step coupling is closed-loop control, not a bug; and extending the combat strafe to the re-aim branch cost 30pp of qualify rate because lateral movement fights the aim convergence that branch exists to do. Also fixed on the way: sprint silently halved the multiplayer decision window and broke `verify:multiplayer-transition` (a speed change is a timing change — `#moveBurstMs` caps the hold at travel time and that hold *is* the decision).

- [x] **Elite HP halved — `ELITE_HP_MULTIPLIER` 4 -> 2 — after a playtest confirmed the level-12 Elite was overpowered.** The multiplier stacks on *un-split* HP (the doc comment says so: "this multiple of what a *single* (non-pack) enemy would have at the same complexity"), which made the elite threshold a cliff rather than a ramp: complexity 39 spawns a 4-enemy pack totalling 976 HP at 1x damage, complexity 40 spawns one 4000 HP enemy at 2x damage — quadrupling a room's health and concentrating it. `stage12_render_engine.cpp`'s `computeVisibility` (complexity 44) produced **4400 HP against the 1100 its pack would have had**. Affects 7 elites across levels 12/13/15/17, now ~2000-2350 instead of ~4000-4700. Required regenerating `defaultHighscore.ts`, and note **the astHash check cannot catch why**: `astHash` hashes the parsed AST, which a generation change leaves untouched, so every hash stays valid while the baked replays *silently diverge* (a replay drives recorded inputs against a regenerated map, and enemies now die at different times). Verified independently after regeneration: 3 entries, 33 replay segments, 0 astHash mismatches. **Negative result worth recording: this did not unblock the bot.** All three profiles still finish at `stage12_render_engine.cpp` with `levelsCleared=11`, same as before — 2200 HP is still ~17s of bot TTK against a ~23s per-level combat budget. The player-facing balance fix and the bot's level-12 wall are separate problems; the wall needs kiting (plan Stage 7), not more HP shaving

- [x] **Bot: the "[nav-warn] implausible rotation" mystery, root-caused and then deliberately left alone.** Both combat sub-branches widen `turnBurst` to a full `stepMs` so a stall-strafe key produces real lateral movement (`Math.max(turnBurst, moveBurstMs(10, false))`, which always clamps to `stepMs`) — but `applyAction` applies one scalar to *every* key, so that also holds the **turn** key for the whole decision. Proven deterministically rather than by waiting for the rare log line, since the decision core is pure: with a 0.055rad correction the bot performs 0.455rad, an **8.3x overshoot**, precisely while stalled in a firefight trying to re-acquire aim. The magnitudes match the field warnings exactly — 0.45rad is one 50ms decision at Gamer's 9.1rad/s, 0.65rad likewise at Pro's 13rad/s, and Casual's 0.26rad falls under the detector's 0.3 floor, which is why Casual never warns. (Secondary: `turnBurstMs` records `min(stepMs, needed)` but returns `max(1, ...)`, so sub-1ms turns log "turnBurst=0ms" while holding 1ms — that is why the message reads as impossible.) **Gating the widening on "heading has converged" removed the overshoot entirely (8.3x -> 1.0x, nav-warns 15 -> 0) and was still reverted**, on a matched-scale A/B (n=20/combo/side, both sides sharing one dev server, symmetric flags): `enemyAccuracy` rose on all three combos (+1.6%/+0.6%/+4.9% — the win metric moving the wrong way) and Gamer/normal `qualifyRate` fell 15.0pp, hitting the pre-registered rollback trigger. Pro/hard went the other way (+25pp), so the combos disagree and none of it is a clean win. Mechanism for the loss, which should have been predicted: the widening is load-bearing for *dodging*, not aiming — without it the stall-strafe key is held for a turn-sized burst and moves the bot ~0.02 tiles, so the bot stops strafing and gets hit more. Trading aim precision for lateral movement is evidently the right trade. Both call sites now carry the measurement in a comment so this doesn't get "fixed" again without re-running the A/B

- [x] **Bot: combat starved the waypoint budget, and a drive shoved off-course never re-planned.** Two compounding defects behind the remaining `stuck` failures on deep levels, both captured on `stage06_pipeline.py`. (1) `MAX_TICKS_PER_WAYPOINT` (600 x 50ms = 30 simulated seconds) is a budget for *navigation*, but `tick()` lets a threat preempt navigation entirely — so a real firefight beside a waypoint burned the whole budget and the attempt was discarded. Captured directly: the bot stood near one waypoint killing **12 enemies** (34 down to 22 remaining) at 100% health with 291 bullets, and was recorded as "stuck". `driveToward` no longer counts an engaged-combat decision against the budget (gated on `pickThreat`, the same predicate `tick()` uses to decide whether to fight, so the two cannot disagree), bounded by `COMBAT_TICK_BUDGET_MULTIPLIER` so an unwinnable fight still terminates — and because the relaxation only applies while a threat is engaged, a genuine no-combat wedge still fails on the original budget. (2) That alone was not enough: with the budget relaxed the same spot still failed at *identical* coordinates (ticks 600 -> 731). `driveTowardWithReplan` checked displacement **once, on entry**, then handed off to `driveToward`, which steers in a straight line for its whole budget with no further path checks — so combat pushing the bot around a wall left it aiming into that wall forever. Captured: from (40.5,57.5), waypoint one tile north at (40.5,56.5), shoved west to (38.8,56.3) — the far side of the wall at (39,56). It now re-plans on any `stuck` result, **including inside the re-planned walk**, which is where the captured failure actually occurred. Measured: 6 consecutive full-campaign attempts with zero wedges (previously ~1 in 3), and `generate:default-highscore` now has **all three** profiles reaching `levelsCleared=11` (`stage12_render_engine.cpp`) — Gamer previously topped out at 8 — qualifying 3-of-3 within 4 attempts with zero `stuck`. `scripts/diagnose-level-wedge.mjs` gained `--level any` (now the default) after a fixed target made an attempt that wedged on a *different* level report "did not wedge" and discard its evidence

- [x] **Bot: an opened door stayed a wall in the bot's own map, wedging `stage03_legacy_api.php`.** The engine opens a door by mutating *its own* grid to floor (`this.map.grid[y][x] = 0`), flood-filling the whole doorway run for a key-locked door (`doorwayTiles`). The bot's `this.map` is a separately generated Node-side copy that never sees any of it, so every door it walked through stayed impassable in its path model — `bfsPath`'s own doc comment documents this and the `openedDoors` set is the intended compensation, but `driveLegs` only ever added doors opened via an explicit `openDoor` leg, never one the bot simply pushed through mid-leg or during a loot detour. Captured sequence: a loot detour to bullets pulled the bot 3.5 tiles off its leg, the leg's next waypoint was *itself* a door tile at (12,53), the drift re-plan asked BFS for a route to a tile the bot believed was a wall (`path=NULL`), and `driveTowardWithReplan` then fell through to a straight-line `driveToward` that ground into a wall for all 600 `MAX_TICKS_PER_WAYPOINT` before reporting `stuck`. Two fixes: `#noteDoorUnderFoot` records any door the player is *standing on* as open (sound because `isWall()` rejects a step into a closed door) and flood-fills the contiguous run to mirror `doorwayTiles`; and the no-path branch now retries BFS with every door treated as passable before falling back to a straight line bounded at `MAX_TICKS_PER_WAYPOINT / 6` instead of the full budget. Verified offline against the exact captured coordinates (BFS `NULL` -> 6-tile path) and live: both mechanisms fire in-game (`strict BFS failed, routing through doors (8 tiles)`, `learned open door run at (12,52) tile=3`). Measured end to end by `generate:default-highscore`, which previously reported *no qualifying Casual run in 40 attempts* (23x stuck level 10, 14x stuck level 3, 3x stuck level 5): now all three profiles qualify 3-of-3 within 4 attempts each, with **zero** `stuck` failures — the only non-qualifying attempt was Pro dying in combat at level 5

- [x] **Bot: "reached the exit" is not "finished the level" — `generate:default-highscore` wedged on demo-campaign level 10.** `checkExit()` only ends a level when a living player stands on `map.exit` **and** `exitRoomHasAliveEnemy()` is false, but `pickThreat` filters to `aggroed` enemies only — so an enemy homed to the exit's own room that the route never provoked was invisible to the bot permanently. The bot walked its whole 22-leg route, stopped dead on the exit tile (0.013 tiles off centre) at 100% health with 284 bullets, and its caller recorded the attempt as `stuck`. ~58% of Casual attempts, which is why `defaultHighscore.ts` could not be regenerated (20 of its 30 replay segments are stale, breaking all three "Watch Replay" buttons). Fixed with `Bot#driveToExit`: push onto the exit, and while it stays inert, BFS to a living enemy whose `map.enemies[i].home` rectangle contains the exit — the engine's exact predicate, since `this.enemies = map.enemies` makes the bot's planned map and the engine's roster the same records — walk at it until proximity aggros it and the ordinary combat branches kill it, then BFS *back* to the exit and re-push (bounded at 6 rounds). Both the generator and `run-balancing-telemetry.mjs` now call it instead of `driveToward`. Measured, not assumed: at the captured wedge all 1047 `driveToward` calls on that level had arrived, **zero** stuck, worst waypoint 15 ticks against a 600 cap — navigation was never the problem. It reproduced only under the generator because the A/B protocol runs telemetry at `LEVEL_LIMIT=8` and never reaches level 10 at all, *not* because the harnesses drive the bot differently. New `scripts/diagnose-level-wedge.mjs` captured it (headed browser + virtual clock + full nav/waypoint trace + screenshots + exit-gate dump); it reproduced on the first attempt once the harness itself was correct — an earlier run reported "no wedge" purely because it omitted `polling: 100`, and the virtual clock replaces `requestAnimationFrame` so Playwright's default rAF polling never fires. Six earlier diagnoses (route length, loot pickup radius, acid overflow, a near-180° atan2 branch cut, a `faceAngle` door leg, and a real-but-unrelated `__pumpVirtualTime` overshoot) were all wrong for the same reason: each assumed the bot was failing to *move*

- [x] **Bot: loot detours ignored hazard terrain entirely.** `maybeDetourForLoot` BFS'd to pickups with an *empty* `avoidTiles` set, so acid and spike tiles cost it nothing and it would walk a 5-tile acid gauntlet to grab an optional pickup — while `planRoute`'s main route correctly prices those tiles at `HAZARD_TILE_COST` (25x a floor tile). Surfaced by the new Exception Handling Zone on `stage03_legacy_api.php`: acid became the single largest damage source on level 3 (68-94 of a 100 HP bar, vs 34-44 from enemies), and Casual died there on 13 of 14 attempts. Fixed with a hard avoid rather than a cost — a pickup is by definition optional, so taking damage for one is never the right trade; `bfsPath`'s existing "except at the target tile" rule keeps a pickup that *sits* on acid collectable. Casual went 1/3 (13 deaths) -> 3/3 in 3 attempts, and level-3 hazard damage roughly halved

- [x] **Bot: the route planner picked a door order the map generator never promised was solvable.** `findReachableDoor` scanned the `reachable` tile set, so frontier doors were visited in tile order; `assertAllRoomsReachable` (`src/map/generation/pathing.ts`) runs the *same* greedy key-then-frontier-door simulation over the `doors` array in order, and that is the only order `placeKeys` actually guarantees — it scatters each key into the region reachable before its own door under that exact walk. The two greedies diverge: on `stage13_batch_job.scala` the planner spent 3 keys on 3 doors and deadlocked with no fourth key reachable, on a level a player can finish. `findReachableDoor` now takes the map's own `doors` array as its preferred order for locked doors (branch doors keep the scan — there's no array for them). Full campaign back to 17/17 routable

- [x] **Bot: a mid-route teleport was reported as "route finished".** `Bot.driveLegs` correctly bails the instant a `goto`/label pad warps the bot mid-route — every remaining waypoint was planned against a position it's no longer at — and reports `{state:"playing", reason:"teleported"}`. But both callers (`run-balancing-telemetry.mjs`, `generate-default-highscore.mjs`) only checked `state`, so "warped away" was indistinguishable from "walked every leg": they fell through to the 80-tick final push and tried to walk at the exit in a straight line from wherever the pad had dropped the bot, then reported the inevitable failure as `stuck`. Fixed by re-planning from the bot's actual post-warp position and resuming (bounded at 4 replans per level, since a bidirectional pad pair can legitimately warp a route more than once). `planRoute` gained an optional `from` start-tile override for it. Surfaced by the new Vendor Depot on `main.c`: the loot detour routes the bot back across a teleporter pad near spawn that the old route never touched, which turned a latent bug into a 100% reproducible `stuck@L1` (42/42 attempts). Not a level bug — the level is fine, the bot was mis-reporting

- [x] Acid Overflow had no cue that it had *started* beyond the tiles themselves changing colour underfoot — a player who walked in looking the wrong way found out by taking damage, which reads as an unfair hit rather than as a mechanic. Added both: `audio.playAcidOverflow()` (a low sinking triangle drone plus a bandpass-filtered hiss — deliberately the darkest cue in `audio.ts`, since every other one is a short bright blip confirming something the player *did*) and `drawAcidOverflowToast()` ("Memory leak — acid rising!", in the hazard tiles' own orange, one row below the out-of-ammo toast). Fires once per room, and only for the *local* player when they're actually inside it — a flood is started by any living player, so in coop a teammate triggering a room across the level must not put a warning on your screen. Purely cosmetic: `updateAcidOverflows` just reports which rooms started this tick and the engine hangs the cue off that, so no simulation state is touched and peers that cue differently stay in lockstep
